A,

DEDICATION 

This book is dedicated to the memory of my mother and to my wife, the two women who exercised the greatest and most beneficial influence on my life and my career as a physician. 

Much, indeed, do I owe my mother, and the recollection of her sweet character makes her dearer to me as the years roll on.

When, way back in 1901, she was a patient of the late Professor E. C. Seguin of New York, an authority on diseases of the nervous system, I was much impressed with the dietary advice the doctor gave her and the personal interest he took in her. Not only did he tell her what to eat and what not to eat, but he wrote everything down on paper, lest she forget or be mistaken-mindful, no doubt, of the words of the pupil in Faust: 

Was man schwarz auf weiss besitzt 

Kann man getrost nach Hause tragen. (What one possesses black on white 

One can with confidence take home.)

All this dietary advice was relayed to me in letters my mother wrote to me; and it was these letters which first impressed upon me the importance of diet in health and disease.

As to my wife, she became my trusted assistant, always on the job, always trying to shield me from the annoyances and intrusions of nervous patients. Besides, she nursed me through the long and tedious convalescent periods following three almost fatal gastric hemorrhages. A natural born cook, she attended and superintended the preparation of my meals for many years and, like the makers of a well-known candy in Cincinnati, did so with "loving care".

If this loving care was one of the guiding principles in the diet kitchens of our hospitals and homes, results would be better than they are. 

Special thanks are due to the author's son, Jack W. Wolf, now resident physician of the Kansas City General Hospital, and to the author's secretary, Louise S. Shane who took all the dictation and did all the typing of this volume. Their assistance is gratefully acknowledged.

PREFACE 

This volume is intended for the Layman. Its author has been a practicing physician over a period of 48 years, during which time he not only made a special study and use of various diets in his daily work but has also presented this subject in the form of lectures to the students of the Medical School of Kansas University. Practically every statement in this book is based on personal experience and all diets prescribed are those which the writer has been using in the treatment of the various diseases and conditions and for the efficacy of which he can vouch. 

Being intended for non-medical men and women, the subject will be presented in plain and simple language; intricate scientific terms and data will be avoided as much as possible and all controversial matter and references to the literature be omitted. 

In the chapter of dietetic treatment only those diseases and conditions will be considered in detail which are of general interest, always mindful of the fact that even dietetic treatment, if self-administered, without the advice and supervision of a physician, is a dangerous practice. This is best illustrated by the harmful effects often seen as the result of ill-advised obesity and constipation cures, not to speak of the treatment of more serious diseases such as diabetes, gastric ulcer, appendicitis and others, too numerous to mention. The dietetic management of these serious diseases is given only scant and superficial mention, just enough to make the reader realize that the treatment of these diseases is the function of the physician. On the other hand, nothing will be left unsaid to emphasize and reiterate the fact that in diet we have one of our most powerful and indispensable weapons in the treatment of nearly all diseases, be they medical or surgical, special or general, as well as the greatest factor influencing the welfare of individuals and peoples. It is this knowledge which will be a revelation to most readers, as it is a revelation to even many physicians, who have not yet paid that attention to diet which it merits.

PART I THE

1. NATURE, FUNCTIONS AND CALORIC VALUE OF FOOD 

From the point of view of food the human body is often compared with a physical engine. Food is to the human engine what fuel is to the physical engine. There are, however, two fundamental differences between food and fuel. Practically all foods have to be first digested and absorbed before they can be utilized and act as fuel for the human engine. Mineral oil, for instance, can never serve as fuel for the body because it can neither be digested nor absorbed. 

Above all no fuel put into a physical engine is capable of repairing a single worn out part or make new ones. The smallest screw, if rendered useless or missing, cannot be replaced by any fuel we may use. Neither coal nor wood, neither alcohol nor gasoline or oil is able to replace the smallest part that happens to be worn out. For the human engine, however, we have a fuel which not only furnishes heat and energy but which also repairs worn out parts and creates new ones. This fuel is protein such as is contained in all animal foods as well as in cereals, legumens, nuts and many others, too numerous to mention. It is this function which gives these protein foods their prominent place in nutrition. 

Without them growth and repair and perpetuation of life would be impossible; they are indispensable and cannot be replaced by any other food. 

Food, then, unlike fuel performs two functions. Like fuel it furnishes the material from which energy is produced either in the form of heat, to keep the body temperature constantly at about 100 F. or in the form of work; but, unlike any fuel, it also provides the material for growth and for the repairing of the body wear and tear which has to be replaced lest life ceases. 13

There are three primary food stuffs which enter into the composition of all foods, either singly or combined. 

1. The Carbohydrates (CH) which include starches and sugars. 

Fats (F) 2. 

3. Proteins (P) 

All these food stuffs are oxidized or burned within the body like fuel is in an engine and thereby generate heat or work. And just as an engineer can estimate the amount of energy available in the different fuels for the production of heat and power, so can a nutrition engineer determine by similar apparatus the fuel value of foods. The common yard stick in these energy determinations is the "calorie". The word, "calorie" is in such common use by the layman that it seems pertinent to define what a calorie is.

CALORIE 

A calorie is the amount of heat which raises the temperature of a liter (about 1 quart) of water 1 degree centigrade. These determinations disclose that 1 gram of fat produces about 9 calories. 

1 gram of carbohydrate produces 4 calories. 

1 gram of protein produces about 4 calories. 

Thus if we consume 100 grams (3 1/3 ounces) of any of these foods and burn them in our body we produce 400 calories in the case of proteins and carbohydrates and 900 calories in the case of fats. This shows that fats produce more than twice as much heat and energy as do carbohydrates or proteins. 2. 

AMOUNT OF FOOD REQUIRED 

The question arises; how much food does the human engine require to sustain life and maintain its various functions and activities? 

And how is this amount to be distributed over the three principal foodstuffs the carbohydrates, the fats, and the proteins? Shall we accept as correct that amount which the race has instinctively chosen, an amount remarkably uniform in all civilized countries, or shall we

adopt the figures arrived at by more recent and more exact nutritional studies?

The former method, i.e. the study of the amount of food consumed by large numbers of the population, discloses the fact that a man of average weight (70 to 75 klgr. or 145-150 lbs.) and average height (170 ctm. or 5f6in) and doing average amount of physical and mental work, consumes about from 75 to 120 grams of protein, from 50 to 100 grams of fat, and from 300 to 500 grams of carbohydrate, depending or habit, work, sex, climate, etc. This amounts to about 2500 to 3000 calories, of which the proteins furnish from 

10 to 15%, the fats from 18 to 28%, and the carbohydrates from 

60 to 66%.

On such a food intake our weight neither decreases nor increases. 

This estimate of the total food intake was accepted and known the world over as the old standard of diet and remained so until the beginning of this century, when dissenting voices, both in and out of the profession, became numerous, claiming that this old standard was much too high, at least in so far as the amount of protein was concerned.

This question of how much protein a diet should contain has always been the important one in nutritional studies. After all nearly all the heat and work of our body is furnished by the combustion of carbohydrates and fats, whereas proteins are consumed mainly to furnish new material for growth and repair. The amount of protein needed for that purpose is quite small. Why, then, should we use such large amounts as are generally advocated? 

Many claim that a high protein diet such as a high meat diet leads to intestinal putrefaction and that the products of protein putrefaction, when absorbed into the blood, cause many functional and organic diseases, such as high blood pressure, Bright's disease and hardening of the arteries.

Such fears are rarely entertained with regard to the two other foodstuffs, the carbohydrates and fats. Since they are burned in the body to carbonic acid and water, the former escaping by way of the lungs and the latter being easily eliminated by the kidneys, no solid

or poisonous residue is left behind to overtax the kidney or liver function. Accordingly it is assumed that carbohydrates and fats may be consumed in almost unlimited quantities, even in the presence of kidney disease or high blood pressure. Their intake is generally automatically regulated by the amount of physical work performed, by habit, availability, cost, climate and season.

Eskimos, for instance, depend almost exclusively on fat as their source of energy.

If the same amount of fat were consumed in a tropical climate it would lead to serious sickness. Hindus on the other hand, get their energy mostly from carbohydrates. 

It must not be assumed, however, that overconsumption of even carbohydrates or fats is harmless and a matter of indifference. Such overconsumption not only produces many gastro-intestinal disturbances, but may lead to obesity which often proves not only a burden but a menace to health. The frequent association of obesity with heart disease, gall stones, diabetes and angina pectoris is enough to caution moderation and discourage excessive consumption of even these two food stuffs.

But, after all, it is the question of the ideal amount of protein intake which is regarded as the more serious and important one in all nutritional studies.

To solve this problem of correct protein consumption, two American research workers, Professors Chittenden and Folin, set to work, each from a different point of view, to give a correct answer to the question.

Professor Chittenden, director of the Sheffield School of Yale University, published his experiments in an article entitled "The 

Physiological Economy of Nutrition."

He first experimented on himself. He had been a sufferer from arthritis of the knee joint, sick headaches and bilious attacks; and he placed himself on a diet containing only from 36-40 grams of protein. 

On this diet he enjoyed better health and greater vigor than ever before, and his several minor ailments disappeared. He reasoned that inasmuch as energy for the performance of work came from the

combustion of carbohydrates and fats, it seemed unnecessary to take large amounts of protein food for merely restoring the wear and tear, which after all, was very small even in heavy work. 

He then repeated his experiments on three groups of men: (1) Five students from the University, representing the purely mental workers. (2) Three volunteers of the hospital corps of the United States Army, representing the moderate physical workers, and (3) Eight men from the University, trained athletes, doing mental work along with severe physical labor. 

These men were first allowed their usual diet, during which time an exact record was kept of their fuel intake and energy output as well as their weight, strength, efficiency and well being. Then the proteins of their diet were reduced to one-half or even less, allowing, however, free use of fats and carbohydrates, according to their habits and appetites. On this restricted protein diet, their weight at first decreased about five pounds but soon became stationary, and their physical and mental vigor became more marked than ever; and this on a protein intake of from 40 to 60 grams per day. 

Folin, working in the chemical laboratory of the McLean Hospital for the Insane at Waverley, Massachusetts, attacked this same question by a different route and in a strictly scientific manner. The conclusion he arrived at was that theoretically the amount of protein necessary was not only not 100 grams as demanded by the old standard, nor even 40 or 60 grams as deemed sufficient by Chittenden, but only 5 grams per day. 

Of course nobody would seriously recommend such a ridiculously small amount of protein consumption; our instinct calls for ten times that amount. We must not forget that with an intake of only 5 grams there is no guarantee that all of it would be digested and utilized for replacement of the tissue wear and tear; some of it may remain undigested and unabsorbed and thus lead to serious protein deficiency. 

We have here a similar phenomenon to what we see in reproduction. 

Nature furnishes thousands of spermatozoa when, theoretically, only one is needed to impregnate the egg. It seems that nature takes no

chances when such vital functions are at stake as the preservation of the individual and the perpetuation of the species. 

Personally I believe that young, growing, active individuals require a liberal protein consumption of from 75 to 120 grams a day; after that period of life, when the activities of man decrease, when the various organs, especially those of elimination, begin to show more or less wear and tear, when the sexual and reproductive functions are on the decline, it is best to limit the protein intake to between 40 to 60 grams.

That low protein diets are compatible with perfect nutrition, provided the proteins are complete, is shown by studies in vegetarianism which demonstrate that an intake of only 28 grams derived from an exclusive diet of apples, oranges and figs fully supplies our needs. 

But it must not be concluded that a high protein diet is necessarily to be feared, especially in cold climates. Such a diet is quite prevalent among certain arctic people. A study of the inhabitants of Northern 

Labrador and Greenland, who live on an exclusive animal diet, such as fish, meat, liver with the addition of fat, shows no unusual prevalence of kidney disease, nor any tendency to high blood pressure. 

After all, meat is our best blood and tissue builder. Moreover, it satisfies and produces a feeling of well being. It hastens pubescence, increases fertility and reproduction, while a restricted protein diet represses sex instinct. 

That many people eat too much meat to the exclusion of vegetables, potatoes and fruits and often combine with such a diet excessive use of alchohol and tobacco, cannot be denied. Such people, during the prime of their lives, may look the picture of health, but when they reach the age of between 50 and 60 many signs of approaching trouble make their appearance which are directly traceable to this overindulgence in stimulating food, drink and tobacco. This does not mean that we should not eat meat but it does mean that meat should not be the main part of a meal but should be supplemented by a liberal use of potatoes, bread, vegetables, fruit and milk. 

I have treated this subject of the right amount of protein in our diet in greater detail because this question is the most important one

from the standpoint of health and disease, and one to which the final answer has not been given. 

3. OTHER ESSENTIALS OF A COMPLETE FOOD 

A fuel or food for the human engine to be complete must not only be able to furnish heat and energy and new tissue but must also be able to replace any wear and tear that is going on within the body. 

From this standpoint not only protein, fats, sugars, starches are foods, but water, mineral substances and vitamins as well; for while the last three create no heat or energy and are not ordinarily classed as foods, they are vital constituents of our body, are constantly eliminated and must be replaced by the food we eat lest death result. 

The following are the other essentials of a complete food. 

a. COMPLETE PROTEINS 

Such

A food protein to serve its purpose, must be complete and contain all the elements necessary for its conversion into body tissues. complete proteins are contained in milk, meat, eggs, nuts, bananas, potatoes, fruits and vegetables. The protein of cereals and legumens, on the other hand, are not complete and must be combined with those of other foods, lest nutritional failure ensues. This is why, for instance, a combination of milk and bread or oatmeal and cream make a satisfactory and complete diet, at least from a protein standpoint. 

b. WATER 

Water as an essential constituent of the diet does not, as a rule, receive the attention it deserves.

While water is not a food in the sense that it produces heat and energy, yet it is an indispensable part of every diet. The lack of water is sooner and more keenly felt than the lack of food. Certain it is, that it is easier to do without food than to do without water. 

Total deprivation of water means death within ten days; total abstinence from food is possible for six weeks. 

The functions of water are numerous. Among others it holds foods in solution and aids in their absorption; it makes possible

the removal of waste products, and by evaporation it assists in the maintenance of a constant body temperature in any climate, season or condition, and, finally, it is the vehicle for the transportation of the blood elements through the body. Without it circulation would be impossible. All this shows the importance of a sufficient amount of water intake in health. A decided diminution of the water content of the body is serious and often fatal. 

When drunk it passes rapidly through the stomach and is quickly absorbed in the intestines and carried to the different tissues and organs, to be finally eliminated through the kidneys, the bowels, the lungs and the skin, 3 pints are daily eliminated through the kidneys, ½ to 1 pint through the lungs, and 11/2 pints or more through the skin.

Some water is also lost by the way of the bowels so that the total daily loss amounts to about 5 pints, all of which has to be replaced if the body is to keep in water equilibrium. 

In health, water balance is automatically maintained through thirst.

That an excess of water intake may not only be harmful but actually result in water intoxication and even death is probably not so well known.

Such water intoxication has been experimentally produced in dogs. 

The animals first showed restlessness, weakness, muscular twitching, staggering gait and finally they became drowsy and developed coma and convulsions and died. 

An instance is related of a college Professor who was suffering from a urinary tract infection and was instructed by his physician to force fluids. He therefore took daily, 10 to 12 quarts of water. On the fourth day he complained of severe headache and nausea and became quite stuporous. When the excessive consumption of fluids was stopped he rapidly returned to his former state of health.-The amount of water in the various parts of the body has been estimated at between 5 and 99%, the bones containing the least and the secretions the most. Altogether water composes 60-70% of the body weight.

C. 

MINERAL SUBSTANCES 

What is true of water is equally true of mineral substances. 

Like water they are not strictly speaking foods in the sense that they produce heat and energy; but they are so indispensable that they must be included among the foods. All living matter contains mineral substances. Without them the body can neither be built nor function. 

Yet it was not until comparatively recent years that students of nutrition have given serious consideration to the importance of certain mineral substances in dietetics. And while the role which such minerals as calcium, phosphorus, iron and iodine play in medicine has long been known, the importance of many others, often found in minutest quantities only, has not been recognized or even dreamed of. And yet it is reasonable to assume that any substance which is a constant constituent of the cells and body fluids, no matter in what proportion, is there for some purpose and has some function to perform. That even the minutest traces of certain mineral substances may have a profound influence on life processes is well illustrated by zinc, which in doses of one part of zinc in twenty five million parts of water increases the growth of the mould fungus aspergillus 50%. Likewise manganese in extreme dilution has a similar effect, and zinc and manganese combined have a stronger effect than either alone. It seems that these metals, though required only in minute quantities, are absolutely indispensable to the best functioning of protoplasm.

We also know that iodine, in spite of the small amount found in the body, is one of the most important elements, and the same may be true of many other mineral substances which are just being discovered as regular constituents of the cell.

These mineral substances perform many functions in the animal body. Such vital and fundamental processes as respiration, digestion, muscular contraction, nervous impulses, regulation of heart beats, coagulation of blood, reproduction, glandular secretion and many others equally vital depend upon the presence of a correct amount of these mineral elements.

Fortunately, our daily diet under ordinary conditions of life

contains nearly all necessary mineral substances in sufficient amounts. 

Only occasionally does the mineral intake prove insufficient. It is then that physicians often feel constrained to prescribe iodine, calcium, iron and others in the form of medicine.

SALT i.e. SODIUM CHLORIDE 

One of the most important mineral substances of the body is sodium chloride (NaC1) or common table salt.

The exact amount of salt required daily to replace that which is lost is difficult to establish; while our average daily consumption is between 15 and 20 grams, it is certain that a much smaller intake, i.e. from 2 to 4 grams, satisfies our needs and keeps the body in salt equilibrium.

The excess taken serves not so much as a food constituent as it does as a condiment to render certain foods more palatable. Any such excess not needed for replacing wear and tear is promptly eliminated by the kidneys. Either an excess or a deficiency in salt consumption often leads to serious symptoms.

Our chief sources of salt are the animal foods, meat, eggs, milk, cheese, fish, oil sardines, oysters, fowl, bread, beef extracts, soups, biscuits, crackers, ham, and peanut butter.

Salt Free Foods 

Candy 

Chocolate (bitter)

CALCIUM

Sugar (granulated)

Of no less importance among the mineral substances is calcium. 

It is one of the main constituents of bones, is a factor in the coagulation of the blood, in the contraction of muscles, in the normal irritability of the nervous system and in the control of the heart beat. 

Experiments have demonstrated that the addition of calcium to normal salt solution forms a medium in which the excised heart continues to beat for a long time.

The body contains 3 pounds of calcium, which is 1/50 of the body weight. Of this 99% is present in the bones and teeth.

It is estimated that a daily intake of 1 to 2 grams, is necessary, to prevent loss of this indispensable element. It is especially in pregnancy, lactation and childhood, where there is a special call for calcium to supply material for the formation of bone in the foetus and for growth of tissue, that the amount of daily calcium intake has to be increased. This is done by consuming foods rich in calcium, such as milk, green vegetables and eggs, which are our richest dietary sources of calcium, Every pregnant mother should drink daily at least one pint (2 glasses) of milk in order to be able to furnish the necessary amount of calcium to her newly developing infant and to save her own calcium from being used up and prevent the dental caries and bone disturbances so often seen in pregnancy. 

The same holds good in childhood to insure strong bones and healthy teeth. Growing boys and girls require 6 to 8 times as much calcium per pound of weight as do adults.

Milk, cheese Foods High in Calcium:

Without the use of milk or cheese it is difficult to insure the amount of calcium needed.

IRON 

One of the most vital of the mineral elements is iron. It is especially needed for the production of blood. 

A lack of iron leads to various states of anemia, and an iron-free diet results ultimately in death.

The need of iron is larger in children than in adults. Women also require more than men to meet the demands of menstruation, pregnancy and lactation. 

The daily loss of iron and the amount needed to replace it is very small.

The amount needed is generally covered by our diet. Most foods in fact contain much more iron than is needed to replace the daily loss.

Milk, however, is notoriously poor in iron, and an exclusive milk diet leads ultimately to anemia. That is the reason why babies kept too long on an almost exclusive milk diet get pale and anemic. 

Our best sources of iron are meats, glandular organs, eggs, cereals, green vegetables, legumens, molasses. Salt water fish contain 40% more than fresh water fish; dark meat of poultry more than white meat; peels of fruit and hulls of grain more than the grain itself.

The iron content of internal organs is higher than that of muscle meat. Beef muscle, for instance, contains 3.9 milligrams per 100 grams, while beef spleen contains 40.2 milligrams per 100 grams.

Liver is almost equally as rich as spleen. Bacon, lard, butter, sugar, and starches are very poor sources of iron.

IODINE Iodine is found in the body mainly in the thyroid gland, though in remarkably small amount.

Lack of iodine is generally assumed to be the cause of adolescent and epidemic simple goiter, an enlargement of the gland, brought about to make up for the iodine deficiency. 

Iodine is most abundantly found in sea water and in foods grown in and near the sea. Irish moss, agar-agar, oysters, seafish, seaweed (also called kelp) some legumens as peas and beans, have fair quantities of iodine.

Water and foods found inland away from the seashore, especially those near the Great Lakes and in mountainous regions, are poor in iodine. 

Milk from cows raised near sea water contains from 5 to 11 times as much iodine as milk produced inland. 

The water of Lake Superior contains 1 part of iodine to 100 billion parts of water; that of the lower Mississippi 10,000 to 15,000 parts of iodine to the same amount of water; and vegetables and grains contain 100 to 1,000 times as much iodine in goiter-free regions.

In addition to the scarcity of iodine in foods and water found away from the sea, the use of so-called refined table salt instead of ordinary coarse salt, is also a factor contributing to iodine deficiency. 

Iodine in the form of iodized salt, or in the form of other iodine preparations, if given early in life, generally prevents or controls endemic goiter. The Swiss give one tablet a week, containing 5 to 10 milligrams of iodine, to all children. This iodine treatment is especially necessary for girls; all of them ought to be given some iodine during their adolescent years.

ACIDOSIS 

Intimately connected with the mineral intake is the subject of Acidosis.

The word "acidosis" means a decrease in the natural alkalinity of the blood. It never means an acid condition of the blood which would be imcompatible with life. 

There are two kinds of acidosis: The food acidosis brought about temporarily by a preponderance of acid-producing foods, and the ketosis or acetone type of acidosis, which is due to over-consumption of fat and under-consumption of carbohydrates. It is this latter typ of acidosis which is serious and is often found in diabetes, in starva tion, in fevers, after operations, after anaesthesia, in excessive vomiting, and occasionally in the course of extreme obesity cures. 

A common test for acidosis is to hold the breath. Normal individuals can hold the breath for twenty to forty seconds. This time is much reduced in acidosis.

From a medical standpoint we divide foods into alkaline, acid producing and neutral foods.

Cow's Milk 

The advisability of treating certain diseases such as epilepsy and urinary infections by acidosis producing diets, as recently advocated, must be left at all times to the judgment of the physician, under whose direction such diets are to be used. 

d. VITAMINS 

A diet may contain an adequate amount of complete proteins to insure tissue growth and tissue repair; it may be ever so perfect so far as carbohydrates and fats are concerned with which to produce the necessary heat and energy; it may offer the correct amount of water and all the mineral elements that go into the making of the cell, and yet be deficient and incapable of maintaining health or promoting growth and reproduction unless it also contains certain substances, without which life is impossible, and to which the name "Vitamins" has been given. (The word vitamin is derived from the Latin word “vita” meaning "life".)

The absence of these vitamins from an otherwise perfect diet causes symptoms and diseases, to which the name of "deficiency diseases" has been given. Night blindness, beri-beri, scurvy, rickets, pellagra and a number of less accurately defined disturbances of growth and health all are the result of such vitamin deficiencies. And while our knowledge of these vitamins is still very fragmentary and the realization of their significance awaits their isolation and artificial production in the chemical laboratory, there is no doubt but that we are on the threshold of new discoveries which are bound to revolutionize the science of nutrition.

Fortunately, under most conditions of life, our foods contain enough vitamins to keep us in health. Only when a one-sided diet, poor in vitamins is used for many consecutive months by the poorer classes of certain countries or in babies or in the treatment of gastrointestinal diseases, does vitamin deficiency become a real threat. 

While all vitamins contribute equally to the general well being of the individual, each one has a specific function, and if totally lacking, produces a specific disease. From this latter standpoint no vitamin can take the place of another.

Up to date about six of these vitamins have been definitely identified. They are: 

1. Vitamin A. also called the "Anti Infection or Cold Vitamin" which causes "Night Blindness and Xerophthalmia" when absent from the diet and colds and other infections, when insufficient. 

2. Vitamin B, responsible for the disease known as "Beri-Beri or

Epidemic Polyneuritis." It is also called "appetite vitamin", because it often improves the appetite especially in children. 

3. Vitamin C, the vitamin of scurvy. 4. 

Vitamin D. or "Sunshine" vitamin the absence of which from the diet produces rickets, caries of teeth and related diseases. 

5. Vitamin E. the vitamin of reproduction. 

6. Vitamin B. or G. a lack of which is responsible for the disease called "Pellagra".

For the purposes of avoiding unnecessary repetition, a description of these vitamins will be given in the chapter dealing with "Vitamin 

Deficiency Diseases". 

e. 

CELLULOSE-ROUGHAGE-FIBRE 

A diet to be complete must also contain a certain amount of roughage or cellulose. Cellulose belongs to the starches and is only found in vegetable foods. We get our supply of cellulose mostly from fruits, green vegetables, and outer coats of grains, legumens and nuts. Cellulose is intended to give bulk to the diet and to stimulate bowel movement. Foods without cellulose do not furnish the necessary stimulus and lead to constipation. A normal diet must

contain a fair amount of cellulose. Yet often, with the hull of grain, the peel of fruit, and the great abundance of vegetable fibre excluded from the diet a deficiency is created which forces man to turn to artificial means to supply this necessary food constituent. The use of bran and allbran cereals is the result, and a real health cult is founded and based on the bran-eating mania. Bran has its distinct indication and contra-indication. If taken in excess it is apt to prove irritating to the bowels, and when such irritability already exists in the form of an irritable colon, appendicitis, ulceration, inflammation, diarrhoea or hemorrhoids, bran has no place in the diet. In its stead agar-agar and especially mineral oils are to be used.

Vegetable Purees 4. DIGESTION 

Before foods can be of service they have to undergo a series of changes in their passage through the intestinal tract, so that they can be absorbed and transported to the cells where they are utilized. 

These changes are brought about by digestion. 

Digestion begins in the mouth. Here the food is subjected to thorough mastication in order to divide it so finely that the digestive juices and ferments have easy access to the nutritive material. This thorough mastication is especially important when we eat vegetable foods in which the nutritive materials, the carbohydrates, fats and proteins, are enclosed in cells which are surrounded by cellulose mem-

branes. These cellulose membranes are impervious to the digestive juices and have to be broken so that the nutritive material is set free and becomes accessible to the digestive secretions. This is accomplished by mastication. The more thorough the mastication, the more symptom less will digestion proceed. Hasty eating and poor mastication are at the bottom of many digestive disturbances, especially those of a gaseous nature.

The second function of mouth digestion is the insalivation of the food. Both mastication and insalivation go hand in hand. The more complete the one, the more thorough the other. Insalivation of food has a two-fold purpose; 

1. To bring the starch granules of the food in contact with the starch digesting ferment, ptyalin, a secretory product of the salivary glands which converts starch into sugar, the only form in which carbohydrates can be absorbed. 

2. To mix the food with water and mucus so as to facilitate its descent into the stomach.

Once in the stomach food is exposed to the gastric secretion, hydrochloric acid and pepsin, by means of which the proteins undergo their first stage in digestion. 

There is also a milk-curdling ferment, rennin, secreted by the stomach, the sole function of which seems to be the curdling of the milk, which is the first stage in the digestion of milk. 

Gastric digestion is much affected by psychic influences. This has been beautifully demonstrated by the classical experiments of the Russian Physiologist Pawlow, when delight and disgust were seen to start and stop secretions at will. That the sight and smell of food will, if agreeable, make the mouth water, i.e., start secretions, is an old observation. On the other hand such emotions as fear and anger and also bad news are known to stop secretions and to seriously interfere with appetite and digestion. 

Immediately after the food enters the stomach it begins to leave and enter the small intestines by a series of alternating opening and closing of the outlet of the stomach. It takes from four to six hours for the stomach to rid itself completely of a dinner; less time of

smaller meals. Naturally, thorough mastication will materially affect the emptying time of a meal. Once the food has entered the intestines it is exposed to the various secretions of the liver and pancreas which complete the digestion of the proteins, starches and fats. 

5. ABSORPTION 

After the food is thoroughly digested, the final end — - products are absorbed into the blood and lymph stream and carried to the cells, where they are finally utilized and disposed of. The greater part of absorption takes place in the small intestines. 

Digestion and absorption end at the first part of the colon; what is left of the food is carried into the transverse and descending colon and rectum, to be finally expelled as feces. In health the character of the feces and their rate of expulsion depends mostly on the character of the food eaten. whether dark or light 

The color of the stools depends a great deal on the amount of meat consumed; the more meat, the darker the fecal mass; iron, bismuth, spinach, and blood cause a black discoloration; milk in large amounts renders it very light yellow in color; lack of bile produces a clay color; blood in large quantities, either from high up or low down, gives a blood red color. The odor of the feces depends on the bacterial flora found in the colon, and this again on the kind of food eaten. Meat, eggs, and other high protein foods encourage the growth of putrefactive bacteria, and give the feces the foul odor characteristic of the normal stool of meat eaters, whereas fruits and vegetables and especially milk and milk sugar favor the growth of fermentative bacteria producing an acid reaction and odorless stools.

The bulk, too, is entirely dependent upon the food. Vegetables and fruits, cereals and legumens with their large amounts of roughage, which is difficult to digest, will form large bulky stools, while cellulose poor or cellulose free foods are more completely digested and leave a comparatively small residue behind. 

Often, too, feces contain intestinal worms and their eggs; and when blood in moderate quantities originates high up, from a gastric

ulcer f.i., it gives to the stools a tarry consistency and color. Such tarry stools should always attract the attention of people and cause them to seek their physician's advice forthwith. In case of a hemorrhage from lower down, like from hemorrhoids, the blood is bright red.

All these findings reveal a tale of their own. Much more could be said of the character of the feces of interest from a dietetic standpoint. The diagnosis and treatment of gastro-intestinal disorders and other conditions is only possible after thorough examination of the stools which should be a routine procedure and should never be omitted. The very least one can do is to look at the stools. The layman especially is urged to report to his physician any unusual condition of the stool, such as color, consistency, presence of blood or mucus or worms, etc. 

6. METABOLISM 

After the foods are digested and absorbed they are transported to the cells, where they undergo further transformation. These ultimate changes which constantly are going on in every living cell and by which food is converted into heat and energy on the one hand, and into new tissue on the other, are called Metabolism. (The word Metabolism means change.)

The metabolism of each of the three principal foodstuffs is different, but is too involved in scientific terms to be treated in detail in a book of this kind.

Metabolism is at its lowest possible point when the body is at rest and without food. It is at this time that heat production, physical work and activity of the various organs is at an irreducible minimum. 

Its rate is regulated and influenced by the activity of the thyroid gland. When this gland is over-active metabolism is abnormally high, as, for instance, in Graves' Disease. On the other hand, when the gland does not function enough, as in myxoedema, we get a lower rate of basal metabolism. The intensity of this basal metabolism can be fairly accurately measured by our clinical metabolism machines, through which the fasting and resting patient inhales oxygen, the

amount of which in a given time allows us to calculate the heat and energy produced by the tissues. This is called the "Basal Metabolism 

Test", which is frequently used by the physician to determine the condition of the thyroid gland and the diagnosis between a toxic and an ordinary goitre.

Other glands of internal secretion also affect the metabolism though to a lesser degree.

This is especially true of the sex glands. The tendency of castrated men and women, or women past the menopause to accumulate fat is well known and speaks in favor of a lower basal metabolism. 

Among the principal factors which raise the metabolism and increase the actual energy needs of the body are the taking of food, the rise of body temperature such as we see in fever, growth and repair and last but not least work. Work in fact, increases metabolism more than anything else. The mere change from a resting horizontal to an upright position constitutes work and increases the basal metabolism by 700 calories a day. This shows that when in bed we save 700 calories and need that much less food. With moderate work the increase amounts to 1400 calories; with heavy work to more. To what extent work increases metabolism and the number of calories needed is shown by the following table.

PART II FOODS

FOODS 

Man is an omniverous animal; he takes his foods wherever he finds them; his teeth enable him to dispose of vegetable foods the same as of those from the animal kingdom. 

It must be remembered, however, that so far as digestion and utilization of food is concerned, there is a fundamental difference between the vegetable and the animal foods: Most of the foods taken from the vegetable kingdom, contain cellulose or roughage and require thorough mastication in order to be well digested and utilized. Such is not the case with animal foods. We can observe this difference when we study the different animals. Vegetable eating animals, like cows, horses, camels, etc., with their well developed jaw teeth, chew their food thoroughly, whereas those living on animal foods, like lion, tiger and dog gulp their food down with hardly any attempt at mastication.

Moreover, the cellulose of vegetable foods stimulates bowel movement. This is why constipation calls for a cellulose rich diet, whereas irritability of the digestive tract, diarrhoea, inflammation or ulceration demand a cellulose poor diet, selected mostly from the animal kingdom. 

The second important difference between the two classes of foods, lies in the fact that all vegetable foods contain starch or sugar, whereas animal foods contain none, with the exception of milk which contains about 4% sugar. It is this starch, so richly contained in many of the vegetable foods, which is our chief source of energy and which therefore forms the bulk of at least one half of all diets of normal people. 

Moreover they represent the most economical foods and are thus the mainstay of the diet of people of moderate means. 

In addition to these two classes of foods we also speak of protective foods, the foods which do not so much produce energy or new tissue but which promote well being, growth and reproduction and protect us from malnutrition, and deficiency diseases. They are 37

represented especially by green, leafy vegetables, fruits and milk and owe their virtue to the vitamins and minerals they contain. 

A. ANIMAL FOODS (1) MILK 

Of all foods, animal or vegetable, milk is the most important, the most complete, and the best balanced food we have, and one which occupies a unique place in dietetics. The fact that the newborn of all mammalian animals are able to live and thrive on milk for weeks and months without the addition of other foods proves the correctness of this statement. I have known mothers who nursed their babies almost up to the age of two years or longer, with no signs of failure of nutrition; and while we do not advocate such a course today, it shows what a complete food milk is. 

Milk is the most important food, because during the period of growth it forms the basis of all diets, and even after growth is completed it might well be incorporated in our daily menus. 

According to the United States Department of Agriculture, milk and cream, either singly or in combination with other foods, make up about one-sixth of the weight of all the foods eaten by the average American family. The United States, however, does not consume as much milk as do some of the European countries. This country produces a billion pounds yearly, or 55 gallons per person; 

Sweden 70 gallons, Denmark 681/2 gallons per person; Switzerland 

67 gallons and Germany 61 gallons. 

The average cow yields 4500 pints yearly; some often three or four times that much.

Milk varies according to the period of lactation, according to the food intake of the milk animal, the season of the year and the breed. Jerseys, for instance, furnish the fattest milk. A disagreeable taste sometimes noticed in milk is due to certain weeds on which cows feed while in pasture. This taste is not at all injurious. 

The composition of milk, in round numbers, is 4% protein, 4% sugar, 4% fat and 88% water.

FOODS 

39 Milk is the best balanced single food because it contains all the necessary food elements in abundance and in fair proportion. Its protein, mostly in form of casein, is complete and is ideal for tissue growth and repair. Its carbohydrate is milksugar; and its fat, also called butter, is the most palatable and digestible of all fats. 

Among the mineral substances found in milk the most important are calcium and phosphorus, which are very essential for the building of the bones and teeth of the growing child as well as of the adult. 

In fact milk and its by product cheese are our richest dietary source of calcium. Milk is also rich in Vitamin A and Vitamin B, and contains Vitamins C and G in appreciable quantities. Ninety per cent of Vitamin A is removed by skimming the milk. The vitamin content of milk depends largely on the season of the year and the food on which the cows feed. If permitted to graze in good pasture or feed on silage, their milk contains a greater supply of vitamins than if fed on grain food. Boiling milk destroys C completely. 

Vitamin D is contained in milk in an insufficient quantity. This deficiency is corrected by the use of irradiated milk. This is milk which has been exposed to ultraviolet rays which impart to it a high vitamin D content.

From its chemical composition it is seen that one quart of milk will supply three-fourths of the energy needs of a one year old child, and one-fourth of those of a grown sedentary man. Thus, it takes 4,000 cc or about 4 quarts of milk for an adult in 24 hours, if he lived on milk alone. Such an amount is easily tolerated by many persons, contains approximately 160 grams of protein, 160 grams of sugar, and 160 grams of fats, besides an abundance of mineral substances and vitamins, and nearly 312 quarts of water. A complete food, indeed.

Milk effectively corrects the deficiencies of and supplements other foods, especially cereals. This supplementary value milk owes to its calcium and phosphorus content, to the complete quality of its proteins, its fats and its vitamins.

Its main deficiency is iron, a very indispensable element in nutrition; but by adding to the milk diet egg yolk, meat, cereals, fruits

and vegetables this deficiency is corrected. The latter also supply the roughage which milk lacks.

As a food for infants mother's milk is ideal. Only when this cannot be had, should cow's milk be substituted. It must always be remembered, however, that there are marked differences between cow's milk and mother's milk. To avoid repetition,the reader is referred to the chapter on infant feeding. 

Many people complain that they cannot take milk, that it produces gas and other discomforts. In most of these cases the complaints are imaginary rather than real, but there is such a thing as milk idiosyncrasy; in such cases milk acts like a poison. I have met with a number of such instances; one especially in a boy a year old who when the time came for him to be weaned from the mother's breast, exhibited such a violent idiosyncrasy to cow's milk that it had to be eliminated from the diet altogether. The smallest quantity would, within five minutes, produce giant hives all over the face and body, close the eyes and cause the face to swell to such an extent as to make it almost unrecognizable; intense itching accompanied the eruption,and in addition the child commenced to wheeze and have difficuty in breathing.

As far as the nutritional value of milk is concerned, experiments on a large scale have shown that older children receiving, besides their regular diet, a quart of milk a day, showed better development of teeth and bones, a more rapid growth and a better state of general nutrition than those from whose diet milk was eliminated. A quart for children and a pint for adults represents the minimum of nutritional safety.

Its economic value can be seen from the following figures: 

10 cents buys 457 calories of beef. 

10 cents buys 198 calories of eggs. 

10 cents buys 736 calories of milk. 

10 cents buys 886 calories of cheese.

It is, therefore, cheaper than either meat or eggs. 

The main precaution to be taken in the use of milk is that it be neither adulterated nor contaminated. Adulteration of milk is

FOODS 

41 effected by either adding water or removing some of the cream. This form of adulteration is really not harmful but lessens the food value of the milk.

As far as contamination is concerned, this refers generally to contamination with known disease-producing germs. Theoretically milk as it escapes from the udder of a healthy cow is or ought to be germ-free, but in the process of milking, no matter how carefully done, milk quickly becomes mixed with various germs from the atmosphere, from the skin of the udder, from the hands of the operator, from the water used as a lubricant or the vessel into which it is received.

Even so-called non disease-producing germs, when too abundant, may cause illness, especially in infants. More serious, however, are contaminations with disease-producing germs. A diseased udder may be responsible for epidemics of streptococcus sore throat, many fatal epidemics having been traced to the milk supplied by a certain dairy. 

Typhoid fever, too, at present almost exclusively a farm disease, is communicated to milk by an infected dairy attendant or by a polluted water supply and is an ever potential result of consumption of raw milk.

Likewise, tuberculosis is often transmitted through the milk of tuberculous cows. In years gone by, when inspection of dairies and dairy herds was not practiced as it is now, sometimes as many as 75% of a dairy herd were found to be infected with tuberculosis and thus constituted a real danger to the infants. 

And last, the transmission of undulant or malta fever by the contamination of milk from animals suffering from "epidemic abortion" is attracting daily more and more attention.

With rigid care in the inspection of herds and dairies as well as in the handling of milk, both during and after milking, and its safe delivery to the ultimate consumer, such contaminations can be largely avoided. 

The use of so-called certified milk (Walker-Gordon milk) seems to satisfy these requirements. It is a milk certified to as to its absolute purity and cleanliness. The rigid and periodic inspections of the herds,

including tuberculin tests and tests for undulant fever; the examination of the water supply with a view to its possible contamination by outhouses; the most scrupulous cleanliness of the stalls and utensils used in milking, if necessary their sterilization in boiling water; the absence of flies around the milk; the requirement of a certificate of health of all dairy attendants as to their freedom from skin infections, tuberculosis, sorethroats, diphtheria, scarlet fever, typhoid fever; the proper equipment of such dairies for the straining of milk and immediate cooling to 50 degrees F. or less; its maintenance at this temperature until it reaches the consumer: all these are the measures which are in use to produce "certified milk". All these measures have to be made into a law by the respective departments of health, and these laws have to be conscientiously and continuously enforced without fear or favor.

But even with all these precautions I have not the courage to recommend such milk in the hot, sultry summer months for the use of babies. Moreover, such milk is naturally expensive and beyond the reach of the masses. Safer by far for general use is pasteurized milk. Subjected to a temperature of 145 degrees F. for not less than 30 minutes, and promptly cooled to 50 degrees or less, such milk is free from living germs.

It must be understood, however, that pasteurization destroys Vitamin C; hence, the necessity of adding fruits and vegetables to a child's diet at an early age. It must also be understood that dirty milk cannot be rendered clean nor adulterated milk be made genuine by pasteurization.

Pasteurization does not unfavorably influence the nutritive quality of the milk nor its digestibility; and the added safety which it confers on milk makes it the desirable milk for all large cities, which are often compelled to get their supply from long distances, requiring twenty hours or more before it reaches the consumer. Pasteurization, refrigeration and prompt delivery make this kind of milk safe. It prevents the spread of typhoid fever, scarlet fever, septic sore throat, tuberculosis, undulant fever, as well as the many digestive disturbances known by the name of "summer complaint", such as diarrhoea, colitis and dysentery.

FOODS 

43 In that way, pasteurized milk has become a great factor in reducing infant mortality.

MILK PRODUCTS 

Milk is the source of so many important by-products which are daily used by everybody, that the story of milk is but half told unless they are enumerated in detail.

BUTTER 

When sour cream is churned, the fat drops coalesce and form a semi-solid substance called "butter". 

Good dairy butter contains 90% fat, a little casein and milk sugar, and 8% water. The addition of salt to butter is supposed to improve its keeping qualities.

The consumption of butter is large, 17 pounds being consumed per capita in the United States per year. 

Butter is the easiest digested fat and has a very high fuel value, 4 ounces of butter furnishing about 900 calories. 

It is also a rich source of Vitamin A. CREAM Cream is the fat of the milk, separated from it either by gravity or centrifugal force. The latter method of separation is used in modern dairy industry; in the homes the gravity method is used, allowing the cream to rise to the top. There are two strengths of cream-20% cream, containing 20% butter fat and the 40% or double cream containing 

40% butter fat.

Cream is very palatable and easily digested and is the source of a great deal of energy. One-half pint of 20% cream, a quantity almost everybody is able to consume, furnishes 500 calories, and when added to fruits, cereals or puddings it adds immensely to the nutritive value of those foods. Like butter, so is cream a rich source of Vitamin A. WHEY Whey is the watery part of the milk and is made by coagulating milk with rennet and separating the solids of the milk from the liquid.

It may also be made by fermenting milk and separating the cheese from the whey.

It contains all the mineral substances of the milk, but none of the fat-soluble vitamins nor any of the proteins or fats, except in traces. 

Its caloric value is but 20 calories per 100 cc. of whey. 

It is mildly laxative, and its use in form of a "whey cure" is quite popular in Germany.

CANNED MILK 

The object of canning milk is to preserve it over long periods of time so as to have a pure milk supply in emergencies or while traveling. 

Canned milk is cheap and readily available; besides, it is easily digested in suitable dilutions-all of which makes it a very valuable food. 

Condensed milk is generally much maligned. It is claimed that it is deficient in mineral substances, vitamins, fats and proteins, and that it contains too much sugar; yet it is due to some of these very defects that it owes its value as a baby food, and often agrees with infants when nothing else will.

I have been able to raise very young, feeble and premature babies successfully on condensed milk when all other forms and modifications of cow's milk disagreed.

The same is even more true of evaporated milk which is of uniform composition,sterile, cheap and easily digested. Moreover, the heating process employed in its manufacture makes the curd finer than that of any other form of milk, thus increasing its digestibility. Nor does the heating process affect its Vitamin A. D and G content. Vitamin C, of course, is destroyed and Vitamin B is reduced in strength. 

Dried milk or milk powder is made either from whole or skimmed milk; 100 pounds of fresh milk are reduced to 12½ pounds of fine powder or flakes, which contain all the proteins, fats, sugars, and mineral substances of the milk. Mixed with the right proportion of water it looks and tastes like milk, lacking only Vitamin C.

Skimmed milk powder keeps a little better than the one which is made from the whole milk. ener. 

Ice Cream contains 14% fat, with gelatin added as a cream thick-

FOODS 

45 

FERMENTED MILK 

Fermentation of milk is an old method used by many pastoral people in order to better preserve it. It is much used in Asia, Arabia, the Balkan States, North Africa and Abyssinia. The fermentation is effected by the lactic acid bacillus, which changes the milk sugar into lactic acid. Such milk is generally called "buttermilk”

There are two kinds of buttermilk-the natural and the artificial. 

The former represents the liquid residue which remains in the churn after separating the butter. It has a slightly acid taste, and its curd, precipitated by the lactic acid, is very fine, and digestible. It contains little fat and its taste and flavor make it a welcome substitute for sweet milk. 

Its composition and nutritive value are below that of milk, but it contains all the mineral elements and most of the vitamins, except Vitamin 

A. One pint of such buttermilk furnishes about 130 calories.

Artificial buttermilk is made by the addition of a pure culture of lactic bacilli to either whole or skimmed milk. Its caloric value is higher than that of the natural variety. 

A species of buttermilk, known as "Klabbermilk”, easily made in the home by natural fermentation, constitutes a very wholesome, palatable and easily digested food; with bread broken into it it makes a very complete, cheap and well balanced meal, especially in the hot summer months.

It has only been within the last thirty years that physicians have become interested in the therapeutic properties of fermented milk. This renewed interest has been largely due to the late Professor Metchnikoff, the head of the Pasteur Institute of Paris, who in 1908 published his views and his experiments in a book called "The Prolongation of Life” (English translation by P. C. Mitchell). In it he expressed the belief that the longevity of the people of certain Balkan states, notably Bulgaria, was due to the widespread use of fermented milk as an important part of their diet. More people in Bulgaria, he observed, reached the age of 100 years than anywhere else. By his writings and the weight of his authority, he popularized the drinking of buttermilk, claiming that its use prevented intestinal putrefaction and auto-intoxication and prolonged life.

Acidophilus milk, the result of acidophilus bacillus fermentation is very much like buttermilk in appearance, taste and flavor. It has a smooth, creamy consistency, a very fine curd and a refreshing taste. 

Whether by its constant and daily use in quantities of 1 to 2 pints, intestinal intoxication can be prevented or cured and thus our span of life increased, is still a much debated question. 

There are other kinds of fermented milk, such as Koumiss, Kefir, Matzoon, Yoghurt and others most of which contain in addition lactic acid 1 to 2% alcohol and carbonic acid. Their taste and flavor are exquisite and they are relished by most invalids who tire of ordinary milk; besides they are well tolerated even by a sensitive stomach, and are often a great help in loss of appetite, nervous vomiting, vomiting of pregnancy, and nervous nausea. It is to be regretted that outside of New 

York and the larger eastern cities it is very difficult to procure such milk products for sick people. Their manufacture at home is generally not very successful, as the possibility of contamination with other germs is too great. CHEESE

Another important by-product of milk is cheese. The manufacture of cheese, both in Europe and in this country, has developed into a very formidable industry.

Cheese is made from whole or skimmed milk and contains accordingly all or part of the casein, fats and mineral substances of the milk. Its flavor, odor, consistency and appearance depend largely on the method of manufacture and on the nature of the organism concerned in its ripening. 

There are two main types of cheese: (1) The hard varieties such as American, Swiss, Edam, Roquefort. (2) The soft varieties, also called cream cheese, like Brie, Camembert, Neufchatel, Gorgonsola, Limburger, etc., made from sour cream.

The high food value of these cheeses can easily be appreciated by glancing at the following table.

For a description of all varieties of cheese see United States De partment of Agriculture Bulletin 608. It lists 299 varieties of cheese, gives their mode of manufacture, etc.

Cheese is one of our chief sources of calcium. American Cheddar cheese contains 0.71% of Calcium. 

Swiss Cheese contains 1.05% of Calcium. 

Cottage Cheese contains 0.077% of Calcium. 

Cheese also retains much of the Vitamin A.

Economically cheese is a cheap food. One pound of cheese furnishes over 2,000 calories and represents the proteins and fats of about a gallon of milk. Thus a meal of bread or crackers and cheese, with a little fruit and green vegetables added, is a well balanced, wholesome, economical, easily digested diet. It even takes the place of meat because its proteins are complete. 

1 ounce of cheese equals 2 eggs or 8 ounces of milk or 3 ounces of meat.

Milk toast and milk soups, oyster stews, junket, frozen custard and ice cream, all are ways of serving milk and cream in a form that most people find attractive. Many kinds of nutritious puddings may be made by slowly baking sweetened milk with rice or bread and raisins.

Milk may also be used as the basis of a great variety of hot and cold beverages very pleasant in flavor and often acceptable to people who do not like plain milk. The addition of a little whiskey, more as a flavor than as a stimulant, also will make milk more palatable to the sick. A combination of milk and whiskey and sugar makes milk punch; eggs, milk whiskey and sugar makes an egg-nog.

Such is the story, only partly told, of the most wonderful food. 

Much of it is omitted for want of space; special works on the various products of milk can easily be consulted to supplement the rather meagre information given here. I especially recommend to those in-

FOODS 

49 terested in the subject the various publications issued by the U. S. 

Department of Agriculture which are very complete and authoritative and can be had for the asking. Milk is a most remarkable food and well deserves the place it occupies in the daily dietary of young and old. 

2. EGGS 

Equally as complete a food as is milk, though not nearly as well balanced, are eggs. When we reflect that from the egg springs the complete organism of a chicken, it is apparent that in the egg are contained all the elements that go to make muscle, bone, nerve tissue, etc. Not being well balanced, however, it is necessary that eggs be combined with other foods to serve as a proper diet for people. 

The average egg (chicken egg) weighs from 50 to 60 grams i.e., almost 2 ounces. The edible portion is about 75% water, 10% protein, and 10% fat.

The proteins of the egg are of high biological value. 

The fat of the egg is practically all contained in the yolk, of which it represents about 33%. It appears in a finely emulsified form and is very easily digested and assimilated. There is practically no fat in the white of the egg.

The digestibility of eggs increases with cooking. Raw egg albumin, unless mixed with other foods, is rather indigestible and poorly absorbed. Soft-boiled eggs, hard-boiled eggs and scrambled eggs are digestible in the order named.

If cooked, i.e., hard-boiled, the white becomes so firm in texture that unless it is finely chopped or thoroughly masticated, the digestive juices are not able to penetrate it, and much of it escapes digestion and undergoes decomposition in the colon. To discourage this tendency to putrefaction, eggs should be eaten along with much starchy food. 

As a food eggs rank next to milk, and on account of their high iron content they serve as an excellent adjunct to milk. Two eggs furnish about 130 calories.

From an economical standpoint eggs are expensive. Analysis shows that it takes about 40 eggs a day if one attempted to live on eggs exclusively. Such a number of eggs would cost $1.25.

Besides, few people can tolerate more than 4 eggs a day along with their regular diet without experiencing that condition known as biliousness, consisting of headache, bitter taste in mouth, lack of appetite, together with various intestinal symptoms, such as cramps and diarrhoea.

Some people, especially children, are sensitive to eggs and are forced to eliminate them from their diet entirely. Hives exzema and other symptoms are the result of such idiosyncrasy. In such cases eggs act like a poison.

Nevertheless eggs, are an important, almost a necessary food. 

They enter into nearly every meal in some form or other and are served in combination with other foods in a greater variety of ways than almost any other dietary article. They furnish an abundance of calcium, phosphorus, sulphur, iron, and especially Vitamins A, B, and D.

The preservation of eggs on a wholesale scale, so that they become as available to the masses in the winter as in summer without getting prohibitive in cost and beyond the reach of the average family has become quite an industry.

Commercially this is done by collecting the eggs in the spring and keeping them in cold storage for use during the winter. For home use they may be successfully stored by preserving them either in a solution of waterglass (silicate of soda or potash) or in a solution of lime water.

Waterglass solution is made by diluting the commercial concentrated solution with ten times the amount of boiled water. A solution of lime water is made by placing two or three pounds of unslacked lime in five gallons of water, allowing the mixture to stand until the lime settles and the liquid is clear. 

A gallon of such a solution will be enough to cover 50 dozen eggs. In these solutions eggs keep generally throughout the winter months without undergoing any decomposition.

But before being put away for storage or preserved in solutions, eggs must be tested for their freshness. Candling eggs is one method used. It is done by holding the eggs up against a bright light. Fresh

FOODS 

51 eggs are unclouded and almost translucent so that the yolk can hardly be distinguished from the white. In older eggs the yolk appears much darker and blotches are visible all through the egg. The other method consists in immersing eggs in salt water. newly laid egg placed in solution consisting of two ounces of salt to one pint of water, will sink below the surface but not to the bottom; an egg three days old will swim about, just half immersed; if more than three days old, the egg will float upon the surface.

The egg yolk owes it characteristic color to a pigment whose depth depends largely on the food of the hens; the more green food, the deeper the color of the yolk. Pale colored yolks indicate that such feed was scarce in the dietary of the hen. 

The methods of preparing and serving eggs generally fall under three headings: 

(1) An egg in the shell is cooked by immersing in hot or boiling water for various lengths of time, depending upon whether you want to serve it soft-boiled (2-5 minutes or hard boiled (5-10 minutes). (2) An egg removed from the shell may be cooked in hot water, i.e., poached and served on toast. (3) It may be cooked in hot fat, which we call "fried eggs" or stirred and beaten and called "scrambled eggs".

Eggs may also be broken, the yellow and white mixed, and then frozen in large containers. Such frozen eggs are much used and are very popular in bakeries, restaurants, etc.

The latest variety of eggs are iodized eggs, the result of feedin chickens iodine. 

3. MEAT, FISH AND FOWL 

Next in importance amongst the animal foods are the meats. 

Meat, strictly speaking, means the muscle meat of an animal. By common consent we also include the various organs of animals, such as the liver, spleen, kidney, pancreas (also called sweetbreads), and brain because they all are used as food; some as delicacies, others even as valuable therapeutic agents. The role played by liver f.i.,

in the cure of pernicious anaemia, a hitherto fatal disease of unknown origin, is too well know to require more than a passing mention at this point. And an extract from the heart is manufactured and is seriously advocated as a valuable remedy in various affections of the heart.

Included in this list of meats are also such foods as froglegs, turtle, oysters, clams, lobsters, crabs, shrimp and scallops which are not quite as valuable and a little too expensive except at the source of supply, but are nevertheless a welcome addition to and change of our diet.

Meat, whether in the form of beef, veal, mutton, fish or fowl or organs is above all an important source of perfect protein. 

Meat also contains certain extractives which add flavor and stimulating properties to this food. 

The vitamin content of meat is low, though opinions differ about this. Certain it is, that as a source of vitamins other foods are more valuable. That meat contains Vitamin C is proven by the fact that people who live almost exclusively on a meat diet do not suffer from scurvy. It also contains Vitamin B and G, and serves, therefore, as a food for the prevention and cure of pellagra. The internal organs are rich in Vitamins A, B and C. 

The mineral content of meat consists mainly of iron, magnesium, sodium, potassium, phosphorus, sulphur and salt. As a source of calcium, other foods like milk and vegetables are far superior to meat. 

The average composition of meat is 20% protein and varying amounts of fat ranging from 5% in the lean varieties to as much as 75% in pork and bacon. Five hundred cc., i.e., 1 pound of lean meat, yields about 625 calories, equal to 10 eggs. On this basis it requires about 4 pounds of meat daily if one chooses to live on meat exclusively. Such a diet would contain 400 grams of protein and 

100 grams of fat, with no carbohydrate-not a very well balanced diet.

The economical value of meat is midway between milk and eggs, milk being cheaper and eggs dearer. The cost of meat depends

FOODS 

53 entirely on the kind of meat people buy. Some cuts can be bought very cheaply; others are much more expensive. 

The digestibility and utilization of meat differ largely with the age of the animal from which the meat is derived and the amount of fat it contains. The more fat, the less digestible. The same is true regarding the age of the animal; the older, the tougher and harder it is to digest.

Some meats are so tough that it is impossible to masticate them no matter how much time and effort one spends on this process. A large portion of such meat reaches the colon undigested, or only partly digested, there it falls prey to the putrefactive bacteria present. 

Of all meats, beef is the most satisfying; it imparts a feeling of strength and satisfaction, such as no other food does. 

Veal, the meat of calves, should come from animals at least three weeks old. Why veal is not held in the same high esteem in this country as it is in Germany, for instance, both for common consumption and for the use of invalids, is hard to understand, unless it be assumed that the source of supply is not as carefully controlled here as it is abroad.

Mutton and lamb are a little rich in fat, especially the former, and, therefore, not as easily digested and tolerated as some of the leaner varieties. The same is even more true of pork; but lean ham is well born by most people.

Generally speaking, meat is a delicious, easily digested, satisfying food for the sick as well as those in good health, for adults and for children after the age of four or five. Its perfect proteins and its stimulating influence on appetite and digestion make it a very important and desirable part of everybody's diet, and its absence of roughage renders meat especially valuable in gaseous indigestion and irritability of the intestines where restriction of vegetable foods is desirable or even necessary.

The importance of meat in our national dietary is best seen by the statement that our meat consumption is twice that of European countries, amounting to 178 pounds per capita yearly or ½ pound a day for every man, woman and child.

Moral scruples against the use of meat as a food are purely sentimental exhibitions; the most vigorous, intellectual, civilized people all over the world are meat eaters.

Especial attention is called to the use of scraped meat for invalids or those suffering from gastro-intestinal disorders. It is prepared from lean round steak, from which the muscle fibers are scraped off with a dull knife or spoon. These are made into the form of a beef ball about two inches in diameter and 3/4 of an inch thick; the thicker the better. It is then put, either with or without a little butter, on a very hot pan, is slightly seared on both sides to increase its flavor, leaving the inside, however, rare or raw. There is no more delicious and more digestible form of meat; even babies may partake of it with impunity.

The possibility of contracting trichinosis or tapeworm disease from the use of raw meat must not be entirely forgotten. Rigid government inspection, both macroscopic and microscopic, as practiced in the packing industry, goes far towards minimizing and eliminating this danger.

POULTRY 

(Chicken, Turkey, Duck, Goose, Squab, etc.)

What is true of meat, is equally true of poultry. There is not the difference between white and dark meat, which popular belief assumes. Both are equally nutritious. Dark meats, however, as a rule, contain more fat and are somewhat tougher and harder to digest. 

The fatter the fowl, the more difficult is digestion. 

MEAT OR CHICKEN BROTHS AND SOUPS 

Meat or chicken cut up and slowly boiled in water for three or four hours makes meat broth. This contains a small portion of the soluble proteins of the meat, a large part of its extractives and mineral matter and a small quantity of fat. The addition to the broth, while simmering, of various substances such as vegetables, potatoes, cereals, or legumens makes what we call "soup". The nutritive property, i.e., the caloric value, of soup, therefore, depends entirely on those

FOODS 

55 added foods. The broth alone has practically no food value and is used mainly for its flavor and for its stimulating properties. It promotes the flow of gastric juice and thus aids digestion. To begin a meal with broth or soup is a logical procedure. 

BEEF EXTRACTS 

Beef extracts are made by chopping up fresh meat, heating it under pressure with a little water, filtering and evaporating it in vacuo. 

The result is the brown, sticky material, familiar to all. 

Liebig, its originator, calculated that 34 pounds of beef yield 1 pound of extract and that this should make 70 pints of beef tea.

BEEF JUICES 

Distinct and different from these extracts are the beef juices. 

They represent the juices of the meat, like fruit or vegetable juices represent the juice of the fruits and vegetables. They are prepared without heat by simply subjecting the meat to a strong pressure. 

We have the natural raw beef juice, as prepared at home with the aid of a meat press built on the order of a cider press. This beef juice is also called "freshly expressed beef juice". Its composition is 

88.10% water 

6.97% proteins 

3.90% extractives.

Given in tablespoonful doses, either pure or mixed with broth, or wine, or whiskey, it is easily digested and especially suited for fever patients.

BEEF TEA 

Beef tea is made either by chopping beef finely and placing it in jars or bottles and subjecting it to slight but prolonged heat, after which the mixture is strained. 

Or the chopped beef is put in bottles with more or less water and slowly heated for several hours and strained. The place of beef tea in nutrition is like that of beef extract.

FISH 

The composition of fish is much like that of meat or fowl. Their protein content and digestibility depend upon the amount of fat they contain. From this point of view we divide all fish into three classes. (1) Those containing less than 2% fat, like black bass, brook trout, smelt, pike, codfish, sea bass, croppie, and a few others. (2) Those having a fat content of from 2 to 5%, like perch, striped bass, buffalo fish, etc. (3) Those containing 5% fat, or more, like salmon, halibut, herring, mackerel, trout, catfish, shad roe, caviar, etc.

Their economic value is very high, especially if one lives near or at the source of supply. Near the Great Lakes and seashore fish are cheap and offer a low priced source of protein. The idea that more people exhibit an idiosyncrasy to fish than to other meats is probably a mistake and due to the fact that fish spoil easier in transport and in that way may cause sickness. It requires a temperature below the freezing point to keep fish in good condition. Food

The canning of fish has become a great industry.
