SHELLFISH 

Related to fish, in the minds of people at least, are the so-called sea foods or shellfish, which include such varieties as clams, oysters, mussels, scallops, lobster, shrimp and crab.

When we eat clams, oysters and lobster we eat the entire animal; this insures that all minerals and the accessory food substances are present in the diet. Their consumption by human beings resembles the diet of carnivorous animals. 

The iodine content of all these sea foods is high—a thousand times higher than that of other foods. 

Oysters and other shellfish are a valuable addition to our diet and to our list of foods. A pint of oysters is equal in caloric value to a pint of milk. They contain Vitamins A and B; are rich in iron, copper, zinc and manganese, which are valuable for blood regeneration. 

Besides, they are rich in iodine and thus contribute to the prevention of adolescent goiter.

They are best eaten raw. Boiling oysters renders the muscle which opens and shuts the shell very tough and hard to digest. The habit of swallowing large raw oysters whole, without previous mastication, easily causes acute gastric indigestion. There is no reason why oysters should not be masticated. Oysters and similar shellfish spoil easily in shipment and the possibility of contracting typhoid fever when oysters are "fattened" in polluted ponds, must always be remembered. 

Besides, many people are sensitive to sea foods and have to avoid them altogether.

Of lobster, little need be said. They are more a delicacy than a food and are too expensive for ordinary use in our daily diet. 

Amongst the reptiles, the green turtle is the only one used as a food, and that chiefly in the form of soups.

All Year JELLIES 

The basis of jellies is gelatin. The meat of young animals is especially rich in gelatin-yielding materials. Veal, for instance, is a favorite source for the making of strong gelatin soups. Calves' feet yield 25% of gelatin on boiling and have long been known as an abundant source of pure jelly.

The chief physical property of gelatin is its capability of dissolving in boiling water and setting again into jelly on cooling. Even a 1% solution of gelatin will do this.

Gelatin is easily digested. But it is not a perfect protein food and is not capable of building tissues. 

As a pleasant addition to the diets of convalescent patients, however, jellies are of service.

B. VEGETABLE FOODS 

From many points of view, vegetable foods are more important than meats or eggs. Being the only starch-containing foods, they are our greatest and cheapest source of energy. They make up more than 

50% of our daily diet; in addition, all are more or less rich in cellulose.

FOODS 

61 This cellulose furnishes our necessary food roughage and the main bulk of our intestinal contents, thereby stimulating normal bowel movement. This makes vegetable foods the principal anti-constipation foods.

As to the protein and fat contents of the vegetable foods, many of them compare favorably with the richest of animal foods. Legumens, for instance, i.e., peas, beans, lentils, and peanuts contain from 

20 to 25% protein, i.e., more than meats contain; others, like nuts, olives and avocado pears are as rich in fat as any fat meat, not excepting pork. Many are characterized by a high carbohydrate content, such as cereals, legumens and potatoes; and some of them are our main sources of vitamins and mineral matter, such as green vegetables and fruits. The fruits, besides, contain pectose substances, which give them, when boiled, their power to form jellies, which are finally converted into sugar. 

The principal vegetable foods are: 

(1) CEREALS 

Cereals are the seeds of grasses and belong to the most important of vegetable foods. It has been stated that nearly three-fourths of our energy is furnished by them. Their per capita consumption in the United States amounts to 10 ounces per day. 

They are rich in carbohydrate and protein, and their cheapness makes them the world's chief source of food. Their fuel value is high; no other food is quite as cheap. Two pounds of bread, for instance, cost, at the most, 20c, and furnish enough calories for 24 hours.

It is to be emphasized, however, that by themselves cereals are neither a well balanced nor a very complete food. Their proteins, for instance, are, from a biological point of view, inferior to those of either milk, eggs, meat, potatoes or nuts. Moreover, in the process of cooking they are deprived of their vitamins and the greater part of their mineral substances.

These deficiencies of the cereals have to be supplemented by the addition of other foods, especially milk, cream, eggs, and meat,

fruits and vegetables. All cereals are poor in Vitamins A and D but contain Vitamin B in abundance, unless this is lost in the process of milling. It is for this reason that the use of whole grain cereals and bread made from them, is more desiable. They not only contain an abundance of Vitamin B, but of mineral substances as well. An almost exclusive cereal diet with its lack of Vitamin D has been advanced as the chief cause of poor teeth and of rickets. 

The principal cereals are: wheat, rye, barley, oatmeal, corn, rice and buckwheat. Of these only wheat and rye can be used for making bread. The glutinous quality of their principle protein permits of leavening the dough; without gluten, bread in its present form would be impossible.

Breakfast foods are cereal products. Their nutritive value is generally that of the grain from which they are made; and their attractiveness, palatability and the fact that they are generally eaten in combination with milk, cream and sugar makes them a very valuable addition to our daily diet, especially in the summer, when heated foods are less desirable. But compared with the unaltered grains they are by no means cheap. 

(2) LEGUMINOUS FOODS 

Next in importance to cereals are the legumens,-the peas, beans, lentils, peanuts and others. While equally as cheap as cereals, their caloric value is even higher. The average composition is from 20 to 

25% proteins, from 65 to 70% carbohydrates and from 2 to 5% fat. Peanuts are considerably richer. The proteins of legumens are not complete, but the protein of the peanut is superior to that of wheat for growth and reproduction.

All of them are rich in mineral substances, especially iron and phosphorus, but are deficient in salt. As to vitamins, they all contain an abundance of Vitamin B, but lack Vitamins A, C, and D. 

On account of the great amount of roughage they contain, they cause much intestinal fermentation, putrefaction and flatulence. This, however, can be lessened by serving them in puree form. Puree of bean or pea soup, for instance, is non irritating and very nutritious

FOODS 

63 and can even be used in an ulcer diet. It lacks the gas-producing quality of the whole bean. If cooked with a little smoked beef or sausage and left over night in the baking oven, it makes a soup which is a perfect delicacy and is nutritious enough to constitute a whole meal.

Beans, peas and lentils are best cooked in soft or distilled water, because with hard lime-rich water they get hard and difficult to digest. 

The soy bean is an important part of the diet of the Chinese and Japanese. Its proteins are complete and adequate for maintenance and growth. Of late, soy beans have been much used for infant feeding in place of cow's milk, especially in cases of eczema, when cows milk is found to disagree.

Peanuts are generally eaten roasted or in the form of peanut butter, which makes excellent, nutritious sandwiches for children. 

Since most legumens are deficient in calcium and vitamins and complete proteins, they should be supplemented by fruits, vegetables, milk and eggs. If these latter foods form a part of the so-called vegetarian regime, it is quite possible to be a vegetarian and not suffer from dietary deficiencies.

E.P.-Edible portion (3) NUTS 

In many respects, nuts resemble peanuts. They are rich in proteins and fats but rather poor in carbohydrate.

Their proteins are as complete as those of meat or fish or eggs and are ample for nutrition and for growth. Like meats and legumens they are a poor source of calcium but they are a fair source of Vitamin B, but lack Vitamins A. C, and D. Their caloric value is very high. 

15 grams (½ ounce) of nuts or 12 grams (1/3 ounce) of peanuts or 6 medium sized pecans furnish 100 calories

But while very nutritious, their high fat and cellulose content makes them rather indigestible. Often, too, they prove irritating to the mouth and tongue causing tiny blisters to appear.

COMPOSITION OF NUTS 

Protein Fat

Carbohydrate

65 (4) 

ROOTS AND TUBERS POTATOES 

Amongst the roots the potato plays an important part in the dietary of many countries. In Germany, for instance, the rural population lives largely on potatoes, and even in this country they are an important source of starch and a valuable part in the dietary of adults as well as of children.

Many students of nutrition regard potatoes very highly as a food. 

They cantain 2% protein and 20% starch, with very little fat. Their principle protein is of high biologic value; besides they are rich in calcium and contain a considerable amount of vitamins. 

When peeled and boiled they lose 75% of their mineral content. 

They are, therefore, best steamed or baked with their skins on, in which form they are a highly satisfactory and easily digested food. 

Idaho potatoes are huge in size and are especially suited for baking. Eaten with their skins they have a taste and flavor of their own, besides containing all the minerals and vitamins originally contained in them. In their raw form, potatoes are an excellent antiscorbutic food: 

It takes about 5 pounds to satisfy the energy needs of man, and if taken with butter, they form a very satisfactory diet; 70% of their proteins and 90% of their carbohydrates are utilized; in addition they are a very cheap and economical food. 

TUBERS AND RELATED VEGETABLES 

Sweet potatoes, sugar beets, parsnips, radishes and carrots on account of their yellow pigment, are good sources of Vitamin A and fair sources of Vitamin B.

The composition of these foods and their value as part of our diet can best be learned from the food tables. 

Of the green vegetables we have many varieties: 

Stems: asparagus, celery. 

Bulbs: onions, artichokes and leeks.

Their composition and nutritive value is like that of other green vegetables.

(5) GREEN LEAFY VEGETABLES 

Amongst the leafy vegetables we have lettuce, cabbage, spinach, chard, beet greens, turnip greens, endive, watercress, kale, broccoli, brussels sprouts, and a few others. They have much in common with fruits. 

All are characterized: (1) By their low protein content, which is between 1 and 2%. (2) By their low carbohydrate content, which is between 3 and 5%. (3) By their entire lack of fat. (4) By their high vitamin content. (6) By their large amount of roughage.

All this makes these foods a valuable and even a necessary addition to every diet. Next to milk they constitute our main protective foods; and the average daily diet should contain about 1½ pounds of this group. They are especially valuable in every diabetic and obesity diet and, in general, wherever a low caloric intake is wanted. 

Their digestibility depends on the amount and kind of cellulose they contain. The younger the vegetables, the better they are tolerated. 

Cabbage, on account of a large amount of sulphur, causes much flatulence and is hard to digest. 

Whenever vegetables, on account of their high cellulose content, become objectionable, like in small children or in patients suffering from digestive troubles, they can be served either in puree form or in the form of vegetable juices. Ready made pureed vegetables are today much used as a valuable addition to the diet of infants. 

Some of these vegetables like lettuce, watercress, endive and slaw, are best prepared and served raw. Others, like spinach, brocolli, green peas, green beans, cabbage, brussels sprouts, cauliflower, onions, red cabbage and turnips, are best steamed or boiled. Eaten raw or steamed best preserves their mineral and vitamin content. If cooked, they ought to be mixed with as little water as possible so as to retain their sugar, starch, proteins and vitamins, and the water in which they are cooked should never be thrown out. 

Addition of alkalis, like soda, in cooking destroys their vitamins,

FOODS 

67 but intensifies their color, hence the practice of adding soda to green peas and spinach.

It is in the green and yellow leaves of plants the Vitamin A is formed. Spinach, for instance, has as much Vitamin A as butter or egg yolk, has twice as much Vitamin B as tomato, and has as much Vitamin C as the latter, and even if subjected to the cooking process, it retains enough to be equal to many raw vegetables. 

In cabbage and lettuce it is the outer green leaves which are richest in Vitamin A; the inner, whiter layers are much poorer; mushrooms, devoid of pigment, are completely lacking in Vitamin A. 

Green vegetables are also rich in mineral substances, especially iron and calcium.

In this connection it might be worthwhile to stress the value of watercress as a tasty and valuable addition to our raw vegetables. 

It contains not only vitamins but also chlorophyl, iodine and iron. 

In the case of ill-nourished children excellent results have been gotten from the addition of watercress to their daily diet. 

Very valuable also are Marine algae, especially Kelp (a seaweed) which have always been used by shore dwellers to supplement their diet in the same manner that the California Indian used to do. These 

Indians were, as their skeletons show, remarkably free from deficiency diseases and from decayed teeth; nor was goitre known amongst them, all of which is due to the fact that sea plants contain iodine. 

Experiments made on man in the laboratories of the California State University showed that all those suffering from deficiency diseases were benefited to a degree almost unknown and unbelievable by using kelp in their diet. 

(6) FRUIT 

Fruits are almost as valuable as protective foods as green vegetables, from which they differ mainly in containing much less calcium.

Like vegetables, they are rich in mineral matter and vitamins, and on account of their large cellulose content they make up much

of the bulk of the intestinal contents and promote regular bowel movement. Their use in contraindidated in all conditions where green vegetables are to be avoided. As fruit juices, however, their addition to diets in such cases is desirable whenever possible. 

Their caloric value, like that of vegetables, is small and depends largely on the amount of sugar they contain. Their protein content rarely exceeds 1%, and all of them, except the avocado pear, are devoid of fat. Eaten raw, they are the best sources of Vitamin C; that is especially true of the citrus fruits and tomatoes which, for that reason, are widely used for the prevention and cure of scurvy and ought to be a part of every diet, almost from birth on. 

Fruits lack Vitamin A and D, but contain some Vitamin B. 

Their color, aroma, and flavor make them a most delicious food, stimulating to the appetite.

Bananas differ from most other fruits in containing more starch. 

Their chemical composition is not unlike that of Irish potatoes, i.e., 1% protein, 20% starch and no fat. Their proteins are complete, they contain Vitamins A, B, and C. Taken with milk they form a well balanced meal. Besides, they are economical and easy to digest when ripe. Black spots in their peeling show that they are ripe. They are an excellent anti-scorbutic food and are well tolerated by children. 

Tomatoes also contain Vitamins A, B, and C. Vitamins A and B are not much affected by cooking or canning. Even Vitamin C is to a large extent preserved. They rank with lettuce and string beans, weight for weight, as sources of Vitamins A and B; with oranges and lemons as sources of Vitamin C, and their high acidity makes their 

Vitamin C content more stable.

The canning of fruits and vegetables, a valuable industry to absorb a temporary over-supply and to make them available at all seasons of the year, preserves their flavor, their nutritive value, their mineral substances and even their vitamins. A warning, however, must be uttered here against home canning. Frequently, insufficient heat is used in the homes and, as a result vegetables canned in such a way, such as string beans, green corn, asparagus, and others,

FOODS 

69 have become the cause of a fatal disease called "Botulism", caused by the bacillus botulinus. Sterilization for three days will kill this germ.

Canned strained vegetables such as spinach, carrots, green beans, green peas, prunes, tomatoes and vegetable soups, manufactured with air excluded and cooked under steam pressure, effect a greater conservation of the rich vitamin and mineral content than is possible if the same vegetables were cooked at home in open vessels in the presence of oxygen.

Vegetables and fruits combined secure sufficient material for bone, teeth and body-building in any diet. They are much used in dietary prescriptions for the management of diabetes, obesity, gout, kidney, heart disease, and diseases of the blood and skin; being mildly laxative and diuretic, they promote elimination. Being rich in vitamins they constitute our main supply of these protective substances. Being rich in iron, they are a valuable aid in anemias. This is especially true of spinach, raisins, plums, apricots, prunes, figs, dates, olives and others. 

Being rich in calcium, vegetables are valuable for the formation of bone and teeth.

Being devoid of uric acid, they are a good food in gout; and their low carbohydrate content makes them an indispensable part of every diabetic and obesity diet.

Both fruit and vegetable juices or pureed vegetables ought to be part of the dietary of all children, almost from birth on.

(C) CARBOHYDRATES 

(Starches and Sugars)

In spite of all the criticism of and prejudice against sugar as a food it cannot be denied that it is still the most assimilable and the quickest available source of energy we have. But not only as a source of energy do we use sugars, but also in order to sweeten many foods and drinks and make them more palatable, and especially for the manufacture of candies.

Thirteen per cent of the energy of the American diet comes from sugar. Our consumption of sugar amounts to 2 pounds per week per capita of poulation. Our expenditure for candy has risen to a million dollars a day and that of ice cream soda to five million dollars a year.

Sugar is used in many forms and is derived from many sources. 

Beet sugar, cane sugar, milk sugar, glucose, corn syrup, maple syrup, molasses, candies, jams, jellies, honey, and marmalades are some of the sugars which are much in use in our diets. Maple sugar is the concentrated sap of the sugar maple tree and molasses is the fluid which remains after corn sugar has been made. Honey consists mainly of fruit sugar and grape sugar and is composed of 1/5 water and 

4/5 sugar.

The great value of sugar lies in the fact that it offers in a concentrated form the most readily available and quickly utilizable source of energy, which throws little burden on the digestive and eliminative functions. We might even call sugar a real and perhaps the best

stimulant we have. When fagged and tired out by heat and work, it quickly revives our energies; soldiers on the march, athletes before and during a long drawn out contest and mountain climbers are better able to withstand the hardships of their profession if fed sugar. 

As an addition to the dietary of children it has a distinct place. 

Children often crave sweets; there is no reason why they should be deprived of this most valuable food, but it must be remembered that sugar or candy contains neither proteins, no vitamins, nor mineral substances of any kind. It should, therefore, be taken only in limited quantities and at the end of a meal and only after the more vital foods have been consumed by the child. 

Economically sugars are equal to bread or cereals. One pound of sugar furnishes 1820 calories at a cost of less than 5c. 

D. FATS AND OILS 

We get our fats from the animal and vegetable kingdom. Nuts, almonds, olives, cocoanuts, peanuts and avocado pears all furnish an abundance of fats, as do butter, cream cheese, cream, eggs, and fat meats. Fruits and vegetables are poor sources of fats, except the alligator pear (avocado) which contains 15% fat. Pork is rich in fat, so are shad and salmon, goose and duck. Lard made from the leaf fat of the pig contains 99% fat and is largely used in cooking. Olive oil is the chief ingredient in salad dressings; but beyond its agreeable flavor it possesses no advantages over other vegetable oils. 

Mineral oil is not a food because it can neither be digested nor itilized, but in cases where the use of oil in salad dressings is contrandicated, like in severe diabetes of obesity, mineral oil serves as a good substitute for olive oil.

Oleomargarine is a mixture of animal and vegetable fats. It has the same nutritive value as any other fat but lacks Vitamin A. Our richest source of Vitamin A are cod liver oil and similar fish oils. 

While not used exactly as a food, cod liver oil is, perhaps, the most valuable of all fats. There was a time, about 30 years ago, when the value of cod liver oil was belittled and when it was thought it was mainly a good source of energy like any other fat or oil, and this in

FOODS 

73 spite of the fact that for many years prior to that time its virtue as a preventive and cure of rickets was well known and it was empirically used for the treatment of all wasting diseases such as scrofula, tuberculosis, glandular enlargements, etc. Today we know that cod liver oil is not merely a fat and a food, but a wonderful remedy, owing to its curative effects to the presence of abundant amounts of Vitamins A and 

D and also of iodine.

A certain amount of fat seems to be a necessary constituent of the diet, especially in childhood, in order that digestion proceed normally and that the stools have a proper consistency. 

While fats in moderate quantities are easily digested, there are many people who are sensitive to them and easily become bilious and nauseated from ordinary amounts. 

The caloric value of fat is 9 calories per gram; not more than 

15 to 20% of the fuel value of a diet should be derived from fat. 

Next to cod liver oil, the most important, because the easiest tolerated fats are cream and butter.

According to the United States Department of Agriculture, vegetable fats, with the exception of cocoa butter, have for all practical purposes the same digestibility and are utilized as completely as animal fats. Likewise, chicken fat, goose fat, brisket fat are all satisfactory sources of fat for our dietary; but since cream, butter and egg yolk are easily obtainable, very palatable, thoroughly digested and in addition contain the necessary food factors for growth and general well being, a wide use of those three fats is especially recommended. 

(E) STIMULANTS (1) ALCOHOL 

It is probably not generally known that after a starchy meal alcohol is a normal constituent of the blood; 1 liter i.e., 1 quart of blood contains 21 milligrams of alcohol. 2 liter of beer (2 glasses), or 225 grams of wine (1 tumblerful), or 33 grams of whiskey (2 tablespoonsful), do not raise the alcoholic content of the blood beyond normal, but with a larger intake of alcohol this content rises, although

this rise is one one-half, if food is taken simultaneously. This shows the advisability of the use of alcohol with the meals. 

The question is often asked if alcohol is a food. This must be answered in the affirmative. Alchol is a food. It is oxidized in the body and yields 7 calories per 1 cc. But while alcohol is a food it is not a desirable food. There is no doubt that in large quantities it is a poison, leaving a depression behind where stimulation is desired, and in toxic doses it acts as a narcotic. It is well known that before the discovery of anaesthetics it was common practice to anaesthetize patients with alcohol before an operation. 

The intensity of drunkenness depends on the concentration of the alcohol in the blood. This is highest two hours after drinking and remains high for five hours. As much as 2% alcohol has been found in the blood of a drunken man. Those accustomed to its use oxidize and eliminate it in 7½ hours! abstainers, however, require twice that time.

The end products of oxidation of alcohol are carbonic acid and water. The portion not oxidized is eliminated through the lungs and kidneys.

Economically, alcohol is a costly food. It is 8 times as expensive as bread; and beer, for instance, is 25 times as expensive as potatoes. 

We have fermented and distilled alcoholic beverages: (1) Beer is the result of the alcoholic fermentation of barley, malt and hops the latter giving the beer its slightly bitter taste. Its alcoholic content is rarely above 4% except in beers used for export. The use of anything but barley, malt and hops should constitute adulteration. (2) Wine is the product of natural fermentation of grapes. We have some wines like Rhine wine, Bordeaux, etc., which are used as table wines and which have an alcoholic content of from 10 to 12%. Then we have sweet wines, such as Malaga, Port or Greek wines, with an alcoholic content of 20% or more, and which on account of their sweetness are rarely used with meals. (3) Whiskey is distilled from fermented corn or rye or wheat.

FOODS 

75 

Brandy is distilled from fermented fruit.

Rum is the product of the distillation of molasses. 

Gin is distilled from rye or barley and is flavored with oil of juniper.

All these and similar distilled liquors contain from 40 to 50% alcohol and should, therefore, never be drunk except diluted. 

When taken in moderation alcohol contributes to human happiness; it stimulates the circulation and the nerve centers of the brain, relieves mental depression, anxiety, worry, and fear; promotes rest and relaxation, increases gastric secretion and aids digestion, especially in debilitated patients, in old age and in fatigue. In fact, wine is called "the milk of old age".

In fever it is oxidized rapidly, yields energy and prevents destruction of tissues. One tablespoonful of brandy, or more, given every hour causes no symptoms of intoxication; instead, it favorably affects temperature and calms the brain and nervous system. 

Many physicians of wide experience extol its virtues and regard it almost as an antidote in malaria, erysipelas and blood poisoning from whatever cause, especially in feeble and elderly subjects and those who are accustomed to its use. In typhoid fever, as well as in pneumonia, it is highly thought of by many of the medical profession. 

In those diseases, 1 tablespoonful of whiskey or 2 tablespoonfuls of strong wine may be given every 2 to 4 hours, and if given in that way, it frequently slows the pulse, renders the skin and mouth and tongue moist, improves the appetite and promotes sleep and calm. 

The fact that it is difficult to make a typhoid fever patient drunk with alcohol shows that it is well utilized, and it is for that reason better to give fairly large amounts than too little. 

It is especially in the feeding of the sick and elderly people and those with poor appetites, that alcohol in the form of wine or diluted whiskey proves a welcome addition to our dietary. Mixed with milk and eggs and sugar and flavored with cinnamon, to make either a milk punch or egg-nog, whiskey adds flavor to those foods and makes them more acceptable to the sick. 

But there is another side to the story of alcohol. Not all physicians

are agreed that alcohol is a safe remedy. In addition to being absolutely contraindicated in all inflammatory or ulcerative conditions of the throat and stomach and in urinary affections, it is well known that hardening of the liver is, in the great majority of cases, of alcoholic origin. As a stimulant it surely can be dispensed with and replaced by safer drugs, such as strong coffee and others. Nor are all physicians agreed as to its beneficial influence in blood-poisoning and fever. 

Moreover, there is no doubt that it is a habit producing drug, the same as opium and cocaine and that through its deteriorating effect on the brain cells and nervous system it makes slaves and beasts of those who partake of it in excess. Nor is its harmfulness limited to the drinker. His whole family and even the unborn infant are frequently the innocent sufferers from its abuse. 

Besides, in this age of automobiles and aeroplanes, even its moderate use and, of course, its excessive use, becomes an additional danger. It has been determined by experiment that the normal reflex time or reaction time between the reception of a stimulus by the eye to the response of muscle or hand to put on brakes or turn the steering wheel, which is normally 1/5 of a second, is even by such small doses of alcohol as are contained in a pint of beer or a drink of whiskey and soda, increased to 2/5, 3/5 or even 4/5 of a second, thus constituting an enormous danger in an emergency.

A car travelling, for instance, 35 miles an hour will go 20 feet farther as a result of this increase in reaction time, thus often making the difference between life and death; and the possible damage done to life and property by drunken drivers of automobiles and aeroplanes is well beyond the wildest imagination. 

And when we reflect that with its abuse are intimately connected so much loss of efficiency and work, so much misery and unhappiness to innocent parties, it must be admitted that alcohol cannot claim a place along with other foods and stimulants, and that prohibition, if enforceable and enforced, could be made a real blessing.

If the United States Government had changed its inconsistent attitude in the enforcement of prohibition and included in it also the manufacture and sale of the many 20% and stronger alcoholic drinks,

FOODS 

77 parading under the guise of tonics and sold by the carload to the knowing as well as to the unsuspecting public; if it would likewise have stopped the manufacture and sale of so-called cooking wines, all of which contain over 11% alcohol, and were sold in unlimited quantities to restaurants and hotels, it could have with better grace enforced prohibition.

Above all, it is hard to understand why under such conditions physicians should have been limited in their prescriptions of whiskey for the sick to a pint in 10 days. If alcohol was at all permitted as a medicine, which it was and a great many sincerely believe in it -its use by physicians should have been no more limited and restricted than is now the case with opium or other narcotic drugs. Surely not 

Volstead, nor even Congress, but the physician, and the physician alone, ought to have been and is the judge of how much should be used in a given case. In a case of blood poisoning or pneumonia or typhoid fever, for instance; an adult can consume not only 1 pint in 10 days, but that much in 1 or 2 days, with benefit and without showing the slightest sign of intoxication. How the great American Medical Association could ever meekly bow to such limitations in the use of a legitimate drug is hard to understand. 

In spite of what has been said about the harmful effect of an intemperate use of alcohol and its danger as a social evil, it cannot be emphasized too strongly that it is not morally wrong to drink a glass of beer, or wine, and certainly not a crime. Only in times of emergency, like war, elections, earthquakes and floods does prohibition seem to be at all justified.

Why should the whole country be deprived of a little beer or wine because a few fools abuse it and drink to excess? There should be ways and means to deal with that kind of animals and to eliminate them from the possibility of causing trouble. 

As a social drink, beer and wine often remove those inhibiting influences which frequently interfere with a real enjoyment of life.

(2) COFFEE, TEA AND COCOA 

The use of coffee, tea and cocoa is much preferable to the use of alcohol, both for social and medicinal purposes.

Coffee is an infusion made from the roasted bean of coffea arabica or liberica. It owes its virtue to its alkaloid caffeine and to its aromatic flavor. This caffeine is a true stimulant to the circulation and by increasing the blood supply of the brain and the kidneys, it likewise stimulates the central nervous system and the kidneys, causing insomnia, on the one hand, and profuse secretion of the kidney on the other. 

There are about 2 grains of caffeine in a cup of good coffee.

Tea is an infusion of the leaves of thea. It contains an alkoloid called "thein", which is chemically almost identical with caffeine, and also exhibits the same physiologic function, only to a minor degree.

Whether the tea is green or black depends on the method of preparation of the leaves. The black variety is subjected to a fermentative process by which some of the tannin is removed. 

Neither tea nor coffee has a food value as such. 

Cocoa and chocolate are made from the seed bean of Theobroma, the cocoa tree native in Central America. The roasted bean, after being freed from the germ and shell, is ground and mashed into a solid mass to form the plain bitter chocolate of commerce. Mixed with sugar and flavoring extracts, it becomes the sweet chocolate, and mixed with milk it becomes milk chocolate.

Both cocoa and chocolate contain an alkaloid, theobromine, which like caffeine, acts on the heart and kidneys. In addition, cocoa also has a food value. It contains some carbohydrate and fat and a little protein. 

Its fat is known as cocoa butter; the cocoa bean itself contains 50% fat. The composition of chocolate is: 

6% Protein. 

21% Fat. 

54% Sugar. 

Equal to 400 Calories per 100 cc. (38 ounces).

FOODS 

79 Coffee as a beverage has always been very popular, especially so when prohibition was in effect. The percapita consumption in 1926 was 12.54 pounds per year.

Brazil furnishes half of the world's supply. 

Like tobacco, coffee produces no habit in the sense that alcohol does and, therefore, never becomes a social menace, even if taken in excess; but, like alcohol, it has its medicinal and its poisonous dose. 

Insomnia, tremor, palpitation, and irregularities of the heart, are some of the poisonous results. But man becomes gradually used to it so that it takes larger and larger doses to produce the same effects. 

Coffee is prepared by pouring boiling water over very finely ground coffee and by straining the decoction through a muslin cloth.

Tea is prepared by pouring freshly boiled water on tea leaves in a heated earthenware pot; after letting it stand for 3 to 5 minutes, the tea is poured off.

In spite of their similarity, there is a distinct difference in the effect of tea and coffee. Coffee frequently produces hyeracidity; tea does not. The difference is so pronounced that in gastric ulcer, for instance, the use of coffee is to be forbidden whereas tea may be allowed. 

Tea is also more constipating than coffee. Coffee, on the other hand, produces more violent insomnia.

As to their use in health, it may be said that tea and, more so, coffee, is the cup that cheers. To their moderate use there can be no objection. For the purpose of keeping awake at nights, if need be, or for infusing joy and cheer and comfort into a social intercourse, and as a finishing touch to a full meal, coffee and tea have no equal. 

It must be stated, however, that they have no real recuperative power over tired muscles in the sense that sugar has. 

The stimulating effect of coffee or tea on the heart, the kidneys and the nervous system, make them undersirable drinks for children. 

Regarding their use as a medicine, I might mention the use of tea in the treatment of exhausting diarrhoeas of children. Its stimulating properties, its high tannic acid content, its almost 100% proportion of water, and its agreeable flavor, together with the small amount of sugar generally added to it, makes it an ideal drink in such cases.

As a quick heart stimulant good, strong coffee, has no equal. 

It quickens and strengthens the heart beats, it increases the activity of the kidneys, stimulates the cerebral functions and overcomes fatigue and depression. Whoever has missed the experience of seeing a moribund patient, giving no response to ordinary measures, with a pulse of 20 per minute, or no pulse at all, regain consciousness and open the eyes, answer questions and come literally back to life after an injection of caffeine, has missed one of the dramatic incidents of the practice of medicine.

In excessive doses, however, coffee produces nervous irritability, tremor of the hands, insomnia, irregularity and rapidity of heart action, symptoms which make the use of coffee highly contra-indicated in cases of nervousness, insomnia, toxic goitre, etc.

Of late years, the use of de-caffeinized coffee (Sanka, Dekaffa, Kaffee Hag) has been introduced for the benefit of those who love coffee but who should not drink it. The caffeine content of these brands is actually only about 1/10 of the normal coffee, and while such coffee lacks the stimulating effects, it retains the flavor and aroma and makes it acceptable to those fond of coffee. 

(3) TOBACCO 

Tobacco is neither a food nor a stimulant. Thus it hardly comes within the scope of a book on dietetics. Its widespread use, however, and the constant discussion going on and the eternal question put if the use of tobacco is a harmless pastime and indulgence or a poison responsible for many untoward symptoms, justifies its consideration. 

And it is especially the ever increasing cigarette smoking habit amongst men, women and children, which deserves serious attention. 

There is no doubt that cigarette smoking has become an addiction which must be reckoned with both from a hygienic and economical point of view. In the United States, for instance, the number of cigarettes produced has increased from nearly three billion in 1902 to one hundred and twenty-three billion in 1930, amounting to an annual consumption of more than 1,000 cigarettes for each man, woman or child. The cost to the American people is about nine hundred

FOODS 

81 and twenty-two million dollars, a sum sufficient to build 21½ Panama Canals.

The very fact that the first cigar invariably makes one terribly sick, causing nausea, dizziness, vomiting, headache, often also diarrhoea and cramps, stamps tobacco as a poison. But there is a wide difference in the tolerance people exhibit towards tobacco, and with habitual use its poisonous effects diminish, a fact true of nearly all poisons.

While the nature of the actual substance which causes the poisonous effects is still under debate, most of the harm of the use of tobacco, be it in the form of cigars, cigarettes, smoking or chewing tobacco, is blamed on an alkaloid-nicotine-which becomes gaseous at a temperature of between 60 degrees and 70 degrees C. and proves especially injurious to those who inhale.

The average nicotine content of tobacco is between 2.30 and 

2.74%; analysis of 12 samples of pipe tobacco, cigars and cigarettes, including all well known brands, showed an average of 2.31% nicotine. 

A cigarette smoker, puffing away steadily for 1 hour, may absorb as much as 35 milligrams of nicotine if he inhales and 271/2 milligrams of nicotine if he only puffs. Such an amount is enough to cause serious ill effects. 

There is very little that can be said in favor of the use of tobacco. 

It is claimed it promotes companionship, peace of mind, contentment; this naturally more so in people who are addicted to its use and in whom deprivation would cause restlessness and nervousness. 

The story goes that when Bismark and Thiers met to draw up the Peace Formula after the Franco-Prussian War, the French representatie got very excited, whereupon Bismark offered him a cigar. The negotiations thereafter proceeded to a successful end. 

Whatever beneficial effects tobacco is credited with in medicine can be secured much better and with greater certainty of success, with various drugs and harmless remedies.

To offset these few benefits there are a great number of symptoms and diseases, some of them serious and fatal, whose origin is ascribed

to the intemperate use of tobacco, as for instance: angina pectoris, intermittent limping, numbness of fingers and toes ending in so-called gangrene, high blood pressure, disturbances of cardiac rythm such as fast heart, irregular heart beats, especially extra beats. In addition it often causes diarrhoea, nausea, gastric ulcer and colitis as well as blindness, dizziness, tremors and loss of appetite.

It is also a common experience that the abuse of tobacco often keeps people thin, whereas their weight increases immediately on stopping the use of tobacco.

In addition tobacco has been held responsible for cancer of the lip and tongue. 

When you reflect that extreme smokers use 15 heavy cigars or from 50 to 60 cigarettes a day, and also inhale, the possibility of harm can easily be visualized.

Whether or not all these symptoms and diseases can be blamed on nicotine is doubtful, but the fact that many of them disappear when tobacco is omitted, proves their relationship. Personally I am convinced of its extreme harmfulness in gastric ulcer, in hyperacidity of the stomach, in angina pectoris, in intermittent limping, in vertigo, in disturbances of the heart, in high blood pressure and in some forms of gangrene of the extremities. In all these cases the use of tobacco is to be strictly forbidden. Nor does it matter very much whether tobacco causes these symptoms or simply aggravates them. 

With tobacco the same is true as with alcohol-it is the intemperate use of it which proves harmful. 

Of late years an attempt has been made to denicotinize tobacco. 

An analysis of so-called denicotinized tobacco for cigars and cigarettes shows a maximum of 2.51% and a minimum of 0.67%, with an average of 1.28% nicotine. Nicotine-poor means less than 0.5%; nicotine-free means less than 0.1%.

A bulletin issued by the Connecticut Agricultural Experimental Station, New Haven, Connecticut, gives the result of their investigations into the nicotine content of the so-called denicotinized cigarettes on the market. While they found an actual reduction of from 30 to 50% of nicotine, none were so low in nicotine as to warrant unrestricted.

FOODS 

83 indulgence on the part of consumers who may be suffering ill effects from their use. 

A method of smoking nicotine-free cigarettes and cigars consists in the use of a cigarette or cigar holder filled at the proximal end with ferric chloride cotton. In this way, only traces of nicotine reach the mouth. For cigars, the cotton inlay is three times as big as for cigarettes. 

The intemperate use of tobacco has, of course, not the social ramifications as has the use of alcohol. The tobacco addict will as a rule neither impoverish nor abuse his family nor become a menace to the community. Hence restrictive measures as to its use are uncalled for except perhaps in the case of the very young, the school children, who certainly can only be harmed by a premature use of this poison. 

(F) SPICES AND CONDIMENTS 

Spices and condiments are aromatic vegetable substances which have no food value but impart taste and piquancy to certain foods and promote appetite. In large doses some of them may prove irritating not only to the gastro-intestinal tract but also to the kidneys. and bladder.

Among the spices we have pepper, cloves, ginger, allspice (dried berries of a tree of the West Indies), cinnamon (the inner bark of the cinnamon tree), horseradish (the ground root of a plant), mustard powder (which grows in the Malay Archipelago), and paprika. 

Amongst the flavoring extracts and condiments we have chiefly the alcoholic solutions of the fragrant principles of certain aromatic plants. Vanilla, peppermint and wintergreen, also lemon and orange extract belong to this group.

All of these are especially valuable to make a salt-free diet more palatable; with much skill in their use a good cook can easily disguise the lack of salt. Salt, itself, while a mineral substance, is largely used as condiment in cooking. That is the reason we use an excess of it over that which is actually needed to replace the wear and tear. 

Vinegar, a 4% solution of acetic acid made from wine, is used to soften the fiber of vegetables and the connective tissue of meats;

also as a preservative. It gives a sour taste to foods which otherwise would be rather insipid. 

As flavoring substances we also use onion, garlic, sage, thyme, vanilla, lemon, almonds, brandies and wine; also sauces like tobasco, worchestershire, ketchup, white sauce, (which is milk, water and soupstock) or brown sauce, (which is water and soupstock) etc. 

As relishes before meals to promote appetite we use: (1) Meat broths, which due to their extractive substances stimulate gastric secretion. (2) Hors d'oeuvre (the name means "something apart from the main thing") such as smoked fish, shrimp, caviar, anchovies, etc.

They are generally served in the form of tiny sandwiches. 

(G) MINERAL WATERS 

Amongst the mineral water we have: (1) Carbonated waters:

These are contra-indicated in gastric ulcers and acute gastritis; also in most diseases of the heart. (2) Sodium-chloride water: (waters containing mainly salt) often used for chronic gastritis. So-called seltzer water is its chief representative. (3) Lithia waters and Alkaline waters: 

Such as Vichy-indicated in gastric ulcer and hyperacidity; also in kidney and liver troubles. 

(4) Iron waters: 

For anemia. (5) Cathartic waters:

Containing chiefly epsom salt or similar salts—used in constipation, diseases of the liver, stomach and kidneys.

In Europe, especially in Germany, France and Austria (now Czecho-Slovakia) the systematic use of these mineral waters in the form of a "cure" taken at the various health resorts has reached a high degree of development, and while we can probably duplicate every one of these mineral waters in this country, their use has not been made as attractive and has not been as well advertised as in

FOODS 

85 Europe. Most of these European health resorts are located in beautiful parts of the country; and their delightful scenery, their systematized attractions in the form of daily concerts; the intimate co-operation existing between patient and physican; the special diets served in the various hotels under the doctor's direction; the opportunity for the use of physical therapy: all make the use of these resorts desirable and their mineral waters effective.

PART III 

DIETS

DIETS A. DIET IN HEALTH (1) Diet In Infancy and Childhood 

(a) Infant Feeding

If anything in medical management is an art more than a science, it is infant feeding. The fact is that the experienced mother and the family physician will often succeed where his more scientific colleague, the pediatrician, fails. Every infant is a problem by itself, which must be solved individually, and many times first one food, then another must be tried before the one that agrees is found. 

Our ideas about feeding infants have undergone many changes in recent years. The former tendency to keep them unduly long on a baby diet has given way to supplementing the milk diet early in life with a variety of wholesome, simply prepared foods. 

From a scientific standpoint the feeding of infants is a comparatively simple problem. We know the caloric and nutritional requirements of infants, as well as the caloric and nutritional qualities to the different infant foods; but when it comes to applying this knowledge to certain infants, it fails. After all, an infant is not a test tube, but a highly complex and complicated organism, whose functions are governed by many factors not always under our control. 

Constitutional defects, idiosyncrasies, faulty habits and harmful environment, all play their part in infant feeding and often defeat our most scientific and painstaking efforts. 

The proper feeding of infants commences before birth. It is commonly accepted that the welfare of our coming generation depends largely upon the intelligent and scientifically correct supervision of the diet of the expectant mother. The life history of all mammals is influenced for good or ill by the diet of the mother during pregnancy and lactation as much as by the diet of the young following these periods. An adequate amount of correct proteins, sufficient intake 89

of mineral substances, especially of calcium, iron and iodine, together with the necessary vitamins, especially A and D, are the principles upon which such a diet must be built. This is especially important in view of the constant increase in consumption of demineralized foods, such as sugar, syrups, candy, starches, artificial butter substitutes, white flour, etc.

Undoubtedly breast feeding meets all requirements of an ideal food for babies. The milk of an animal is of a composition especially adapted and suited to the ideal rate of growth of the respective species. 

There is no doubt that the average baby has a greater chance of survival with breast milk than with any other food. This is particularly true during the hot summer months when bacterial contamination of cows milk is especially liable to occur. It is also the ideal method of feeding while traveling. Nor have the advances in artificial feeding of infants changed this. It is true that in well-to-do families successful artificial feeding is the rule these days; breast feeding, however, is still the method of choice for the average baby. 

For the first few days the baby is put to the breast about three times a day; later about every three or four hours. There is generally a slight loss of weight during the first three days due to the fact that colostrum, the initial secretion of the breast, is not very nutritious. 

Whether or not it is wise to try to improve on nature and stop this loss by giving the baby a few extra bottles is very questionable. 

Personally, I believe that most babies can be trained to a 4-hour schedule and, except for the first few weeks, to no night feeding at all. Such a schedule gives the mother the necessary rest and enables her to produce the required amount of milk. The length of time the baby should be on the breast is between 20 and 30 minutes.

There are a few contra-indications to breast feeding. Active tuberculosis in the mother is one of them; another is inverted nipples which cannot be corrected by breast pumps or similar devices. Sore nipples are not necessarily a contra-indication. The pain when the baby first takes hold of the nipples may be severe, even excruciating, but once the baby is nursing the pain is minimal, and as a rule healing proceeds in most cases even while the baby is being kept on the breast.

DIETS 

91 Where this is not the case, nursing one breast only while the other is allowed to rest and to heal, is my method of choice. It is remarkable how fast healing takes place when the nipple is put at rest. Thus alternating between right and left breast, it is found that usually within 10 days or 2 weeks, nursing can proceed normally from both breasts.

The use of nipple shields is, to say the least, very unsatisfactory and seldom enables the babies to get enough food. 

Often it happens that the breasts do not furnish a sufficient quantity of milk. As a result the babies become fretful, colicky, suck their fingers, have frequent small, greenish bowel movements and instead of gaining weight, lose weight. In such cases there is frequently a spurious overflow of milk. But if you test such breasts by milking them with your fingers or with a good breast pump, you will quickly find that the supply is woefully inadequate. Initiation of artificial feeding in these cases will quickly change the whole picture and bring peace to the household.

It is my custom to give the babies one or two bottles a day, even while on the breast. This gives the mother more liberty and gets the baby used to the future bottle. The two forms of feeding, given simultaneously, are generally found to agree perfectly. 

Breast feeding, or an exclusive milk diet, is not generally continued longer than six months. This prevents the nutritional anaemia which we often see in babies who are kept too long on cow's milk, a food which is rather poor in iron. It is best to initiate artificial feeding after 6 months, unless this comes in the summer, in which case a continuation of breast feeding for a month or two longer is probably safest.

Breast feeding, while undoubtedly the ideal method, is at times impossible. Inverted nipples, sore nipples and insufficient milk supply which cannot be corrected, sickness of the mother or the social and economic condition of the mother, often makes early weaning desirable. In such cases the best substitute for mother's milk is undoubtedly cow's milk, with the understanding, however, that it has to be modified in such a way as to more nearly resemble mother's milk.

The first important step is the modification of the curd. It is due to the tough curd of cow's milk that vomiting, colic and indigestion of a young baby is produced. To render this curd soft and flaky the milk is either boiled or modified by the addition to it of lime water or cereal decoctions like rice water, barley water or oatmeal water.

Cow's milk, because it is not as sweet as mother's milk, requires the addition of sugar. I generally use cane sugar and have seldom found that babies have any difficulty in digesting and utilizing it. Nor do I see any objection to the use of milk sugar for this purpose. 

The use of milk sugar would seem to be the natural thing, in view of the fact that all milks, including mother's milk, contain milk sugar as their carbohydrate. Why then, under such circumstances, it should be necessary to use the more expensive sweeting agents, is beyond comprehension.

Whether it is best to use whole milk, as some advocate, or diluted milk, is still a question. Personally, I always dilute the milk during the first 3 to 5 months, using whole milk thereafter. This dilution is especially desirable when using Jersey milk which is generally too rich in fats. Milk may be diluted with water or cereal decoctions, in a proportion of 1 part of milk to 2 parts of water the first 2 months; equal parts the next 2 months, and 2 parts of milk to 1 part of diluent the 5th and 6th months. The addition of vegetable juices, such as spinach juice, tomato juice, carrot juice, a teaspoonful to each bottle, right from the start, gradually increased to a tablespoonful, provides additional mineral substances and vitamins for the babies. 

To insure a fairly germ-free food, milk ought to be either pasteurized or sterilized. Pasteurization is done at 140 degree F and sterilization at 212 degree F. The use of so-called raw certified milk is all right if its certification can be relied upon, which, however, is not often the case. Besides, the production of certified milk is such an expensive process that its price for poor people becomes well nigh prohibitive. Remembering that pasteurization, or more so sterilization, is apt to destroy, partially or completely, the different vitamins in the milk, it is advisable to add as soon after birth as possible, usually

DIETS 

93 after the first month, orange juice or tomato juice and cod liver oil, in increasing doses, three times a day, to supply Vitamin A, C, and 

D. Commencing with a teaspoonful of orange juice fresh or canned, twice daily, together with 5 to 10 drops of standardized cod liver oil, the dose is gradually increased to make certain that an adequate supply of these vitamins is present. 

After the third or fourth month a daily feeding of some well cooked cereal, like Cream of Wheat or rice cooked for 3 hours and mashed or oatmeal strained to remove the cellulose, mixed with milk, increases the caloric intake. 

After the sixth month, the use of pureed vegetables and grated fruits is advisable. Grated apples, grated carrots, mashed potatoes, finely mashed vegetables such as asparagus tips, cauliflower tops or pureed vegetables, agree with most babies of that age and increase the vitamin and mineral content of their diet. These vegetables should always be given before the main feeding. The amount of milk for all purposes, including that added to the cereals, should not exceed 

1 quart in 24 hours. Such a diet insures perfect babies.

That bottles and nipples have to be properly taken care of and scrupulously cleaned goes without saying.

It is also recommended that an infant be held in the mother's arms when given the bottle. The practice, so frequently indulged in, of placing the bottle in a slightly inclined and seemingly secure position beside the baby is reprehensible. 

It seems almost ridiculous to mention that an occasional failure of bottle feeding is often due to the fact that the holes in the rubber nipple are not large enough. In such cases the babies become fretful and refuse to nurse altogether, and unless the mother tests the nipple herself, the cause of the trouble often remains undiscovered. I have had a number of such experiences.

From the ninth month on, or even before, the yolk of an egg may be added daily, perhaps best mixed with the pureed vegetables. 

It contributes iron, vitamins, and valuable fats to the diet. may

At this time, too, the use of hard bread crust or hard crackers be started in order to teach the child to masticate.

A word of warning might here be apropos. Do not forget to give the babies an extra amount of water during the hot summer months. The loss of water through perspiration is often so great as to produce a fever, the so-called water fever, which immediately subsides on the administration of additional amounts of water. 

During the second year the number of feedings in 24 hours ought to be reduced to 4; 1 quart of milk daily should continue to form the basis of the diet. In addition, a cereal, or toast, or zweiback, or plain puddings, or milk toast, may be given twice a day; ½ cup of pureed vegetables, such as spinach, carrots, string beans, asparagus tips, cauliflower tops, potatoes, tomatoes-fresh or canned, should be given once a day; a soft boiled egg once a day; fruit juices; especially orange juice and tomato juice, once or twice a day; cod liver oil, 1 dram 3 times a day, except in the summer when sunlight takes its place.

On such a diet, a baby's growth proceeds at a normal rate, i.e., it doubles its weight at 6 months, triples it at 12 months, and quadruples it at 2 years, or even better.

The following is a feeding schedule for a normal 1 to 2 year old child: 7 a.m. 

8 ounces (240 cc) 

1 glass of milk. 

9:00 10:00 

1/2 to 1 teaspoonful of cod liver oil. 

3 ounces (90 cc) plus 1/2 cup of orange juice.

A cooked cereal with 8 ounces of milk (part of it used with the cereal), or pudding made of tapioca, farina or bread. 

2 p.m. Half a cup of pureed vegetable with 1 egg, followed by 8 ounces of milk; ½ cup of tomato juice. 

6 p.m. Cereal and milk or milk toast or pudding and milk. 

6.30 p.m. 12 to 1 teaspoonful of cod liver oil.

During the third year and thereafter, the use of rare, scraped beef, 3 or 4 times a week, adds valuable mineral substances, proteins and vitamins, as well as variety, to the diet. 

The subject of infant feeding would not be complete without mentioning some modifications of cow's milk when this either produces

DIETS 

95 indigestion or is not available or is dangerous on account of lack of refrigeration or during travel. 

The best known modifications are: (1) Evaporated milk, unsweetened, evaporated to 1/2 its volume and canned. Dilute this milk with equal quantity of water before using. (2) Dried milk powder (Klim, Dryko); 1 ounce of this milk powder added to 8 ounces of water makes whole milk or skimmed milk, according to whether whole or skimmed milk is used in the preparation of the milk powder. Such a milk powder preserves the full mineral and even the vitamin content of the milk, and being always sterile, is excellent when traveling. 

Klim made from whole milk furnishes 160 calories per ounce and Dryko made from skimmed milk 130. (3) Another modification of milk which was extensively used in former years and which is much condemned and damned today is sweetened condensed milk, which is milk evaporated to onehalf, plus the addition of 50% cane sugar. While condensed milk has many faults, it is due to these very faults that it often agrees with very young babies when nothing else will. Naturally, its limited protein and fat content, as well as its lack of vitamins, makes an early change to some more complete food desirable and imperative. 

(4) A synthetic milk introduced by Dr. H. J. Gerstenberger of 

Cleveland called S.M.A., consisting of skimmed milk, milksugar, and cod liver oil, has given almost universally excellent results. Its composition, when prepared according to directions, is 3.5% fat, 7.2% sugar and 1.2% protein. Its fat is in the form of cod liver oil so that the only addition necessary to secure the vitamins is orange juice. Its caloric value is 20 calories per ounce.

Such proprietary foods as Imperial Granum, Mellin's Food, Malted 

Milk, Nestle's Food, and many others, much in use in years gone by, have mostly been discarded in favor of cow's milk and its modifications.

The use of irradiated milk is no doubt a step in advance. Irradiation increases the vitamin D content of milk without changing its chemical and other properties.

100

DIETS 

101 For children from 3 to 6 years of age, the daily use of whole wheat bread and butter, of cereals and cream for breakfast, of vegetables and fruits, especially the citrous fruits, at least twice a day, of eggs, cottage cheese, nuts and bacon, of simple desserts such as junket, custard, puddings, blanc mange, or jello, should be the routine; tender meat, such as scraped beef, meat loaf, also fish and fowl may be added. Two vegetables ought to be served each day; spinach, lettuce, endive, cabbage, brussel sprouts, broccoli, kale, watercress, tomatoes, dandelion and mustard greens, onions, beets and turnips as well as potatoes and green peas should have a prominent place in the dietary of every child. Green leaves are richer in iron and vitamins than are the bleached leaves; and tomatoes are as effective canned as fresh. 

Variety is very desirable. Such foods, however, as hot breads, pork, sausage, fried foods, rich pastries, much candy, pickles, condiments, tea and coffee, coca-cola, carbonated drinks, and alcohol, are best omitted. Candy should never be allowed before meals, but only after the appetite has been satisfied with more healthful foods. Candy, while a good energy producer, often establishes a craving for sweets to the exclusion of other foods, and if given before meals, it ruins the appetite. If given in excess, it irritates the gastro intestinal tract and often causes fermentation. Moreover it contains no tooth or bone forming material, no vitamins, no proteins, no minerals. Besides candy, raisins, dates, jellies, jams, preserves, cakes and cookies, ice cream and sherbet are suitable sweets for children. The use of tea or coffee should be forbidden because they are non-nutritious, are stimulating, and unsuitable for children at almost any age. In fact, there is no room in the stomach for such drinks after the necessary quantity of milk has been taken.

A regular schedule of three meals a day with the food requirements fairly evenly distributed over the three meals, seems to work best for most children of that age. For a very active, rapidily growing child a little extra food given not too near the regular meal hour is often advisable. An apple, or orange, or banana, is the best food for such intermediate feedings.

On these diets, as outlined for the different ages, such diseases as scurvy, rickets, malnutrition and anaemia become rare occurrences, bone formation and dentition proceed normally and the existence of caries is much diminished. Besides, such children will grow more rapidly, will attain larger size, show more vitality and exhibit greater resistance. 

It is especially in children that the supply of vitamins and mineral substances should not be left to chance. A liberal supply of milk, orange juice, tomatoes, fruits and green vegetables insures their vitamin supply and must be insisted upon. Their calcium for bones and teeth comes mainly from a quart of milk daily, supplemented by vegetables, cheese and eggs.

Their complete proteins are derived from milk, eggs, meat, and potatoes. 8 a.m. 10 a.m. 

DIET FOR A THREE YEAR OLD CHILD 

Orange juice, cereal with cream and sugar, 1 egg and a piece of toast and butter, a glass of milk. 

A banana. 

12:30 a.m. Vegetable soup, or cereal soup. A helping of meat, fish or fowl (especially white meat of chicken, scraped beef ball, meat or veal loaf) or stewed chicken with rice. 6 p.m. 

Mashed or baked potato. Vegetable puree made of peas, carrots, asparagus, string beans; tapioca pudding and cream. 

A glass of milk.

Cereal and cream. Custard. A salad of lettuce and tomatoes or watercress. A glass of milk.

SCHEDULE OF FEEDING EVAPORATED MILK 

Schedule for 6 months' old baby 

6 a.m. Bottle of 1:1 or 1:2

(b) DIET FOR OLDER CHILDREN 

The importance of milk in the diet of older children is well illustrated by the experience with 93 school children of the ages between 5 and 14, who received from 12 to 1 pint of fresh cow's milk daily for 13 weeks. Compared with a control group, who received no milk, they showed twice the increase in height and 21½ times the gain in weight. Besides, they seemed mentally more alert.
