(c) SCHOOL LUNCHEONS 

For children between the ages of 5 and 16 who go to school and who, on account of the distance from home or for other reasons, cannot have their noon meals at home, school luncheons have to be provided.

These school luncheons have to be adequate, particularly in cases of needy children who do not get sufficient and correct food at home. 

SAMPLE SCHOOL LUNCHEON 

(1) Eggs, boiled, coddled, poached or scrambled, whole wheat bread and butter, tomato and lettuce salad, a pudding, 1 glass of milk. (2) Meatloaf (beef or veal) potatoes, baked or mashed, asparagus on toast, sliced tomatoes, ice cream and cake, milk. (3) Peanut butter sandwich or chicken sandwich with lettuce between. Macaroni and cheese and milk. (4) Lamb chops, baked potato, bread and butter, sliced bananas, and oranges, cookies and milk. (5) Baked omelette with bread and butter, applesauce and cake, milk. (7) Cottage cheese with lettuce leaves, baked potatoes and butter, fruit salad, milk.

To insure the necessary vitamins and minerals in the daily diet the manufacture of a bread has been suggested in which the Vitamin D is incorporated in the form of Viosterol in the shortening used in making the bread so that each 24 ounce loaf contained the Vitamin D equivalent of 3 drams of standard cod liver oil. 

And a new breakfast cereal is recommended to take the place of our refined and finely milled infant cereals, which in taste, appearance and keeping qualities, resembles the other cereals and which in addition contains the necessary vitamins and minerals in sufficient amounts. The composition of this cereal is; farina 53%, oatmeal 18%, cornmeal 10%, wheat germ 15%, bone meal 2%, dried brewer's yeast 

1%, and machine dried alfalfa 1%. A scrutiny of this composition shows that the wheat germ furnishes abundant vitamins, minerals, and proteins; the yeast makes it rich in Vitamins B and B2 and E; alfalfa supplies Vitamin A and valuable minerals. The iron content of alfalfa is 10 times the amount found in egg yolk. The bonemeal, an odorless, tasteless, white powder is rich in calcium and iron. 

A study made of the rate of growth of children in the Hospital for Sick Children, Toronto, fed daily 2 ounces of this cereal mixture in place of ordinary cereals, showed that at the end of 10 weeks the

DIETS 

107 children on the special cereal mixture gained 4 to 5 times their expected rate, i.e., the rate of gain obtained by children on ordinary cereals. 

(2) NORMAL DIET FOR ADULTS 

It has only been within the past twenty-five years that the superiority or inferiority of certain diets was gradually recognized. 

Prior to that time it was thought that satisfying the appetite meant adequate nutrition, and emphasis was laid on the caloric value of the food i.e., on quantity rather than quality. But we now know that factors in the diet, not entering into caloric calculations, frequently determine its adequacy and are most vital to proper development, well being and life. This knowledge constitutes one of the most significant advances effected for the welfare of mankind in recent years. A diet f.i., consisting mainly of cereals, legumens, bread, muscle meat, sugar and coffee is not satisfactory; on such a diet animals neither grow well nor remain young long, unless by the addition of green leafy vegetables and especially of milk the deficiencies of such a faulty diet are made good.;

The importance of the addition of milk to a cereal diet is well illustrated by a study made on two groups of 200 rats each, all originally from the same litter. These animals were studied for 20 generations. 

One group was placed on a Diet A composed of 1/6 whole milk powder and 5/6 ground whole wheat and a little table salt; the second group was fed on a Diet B, consisting of 1/3 whole milk powder and 2/3 whole wheat. The result was that the animals on 

Diet B proved superior in size, length of life, length of reproductive life, number of litters to each female and number of young to each litter.

Such experiments demonstrate the difference between adequate and optimum nutrition and the importance of certain dietary articles like milk to the welfare of the individuals and races. 

What we really desire to accomplish by diet is optimum nutrition and well being. This expresses itself in (1) Uninterrupted growth to full adult size. (2) High fertility.

(3) Low infant mortality. (4) Perfect bones and teeth. 

(5) Resistance to infection. 

(6) The ability to reach an advanced age before symptoms of senility appear. 

To accomplish this, the diet must provide: (1) Sufficient calories to furnish heat and energy. (2) Biologically perfect proteins for the building of the body. (3) All the various mineral elements so necessary for the vital function of the cells and to replace the daily wear and tear. (4) The different Vitamins. (5) Bulk and water.

Such a diet is ideal. 

In addition every diet must be suited to age, climate, race, work and many other factors. A diet, for instance, fit for people living in the Arctic Zone would be almost fatal to one living near the Equator; a diet suitable for an office worker would hardly satisfy a woodchopper; nor would a diet for a school child or a young man be tolerated by a man of advanced years.

That in spite of the great progress that has been made in the science of nutrition our results are not what they ought to be is because changes have come over our American diet which were not always for the better. The consumption of sugar, for instance, which in 1823 amounted to 8.8 pounds a year for each person has risen in 1931 to 108 pounds per capita of population, furnishing one-sixth of our daily energy need or 600 calories. Naturally, this increase in the consumption of sugar must have been at the expense of other and better foods in our national diet, and since sugar contains neither vitamins nor minerals nor proteins, its consumption to the exclusion of more vital foods is decidedly harmful.

Likewise, the other five-sixths of our diet has lost much of its vitamin and mineral potency in the refining process to which so many modern foods are subjected. The polishing of rice, the refining of cereals, the pasteurizing of milk, etc., has resulted in a loss of much of our mineral and vitamin supply and of our roughage.

DIETS 

109 To offset this we now know that the so-called protective foods are the basis and sine qua non of all diets and under any condition of life and that the attempt to live on white bread, cereals, oleomargarine, tea, cakes and other devitalized and deficient foods is futile and dangerous; and this knowledge has become one of the fixed and fundamental principles in the science of nutrition and bids fair to maintain this position for all time to come. 

It thus has become evident that ranking in importance are: (1) Those foods which furnish the minerals and vitamins, such as milk, eggs, green leafy vegetables and fruits. (2) Those which supply the perfect proteins or tissue builders, such as milk, meats, eggs, potatoes, nuts, bananas, etc. (3) The foods which supply the necessary energy such as cereals, legumens, bread, sugars, and fats.

Therefore the money spent for food for a family with children should be divided as follows: 1/3 for milk. 

1/3 for vegetables, fruits, potatoes, and nuts. 

1/3 for meat, fish, fowl, eggs, bread, cereals, legumens, sweets, and fats. 

Or, as another authority put it "Spend at least as much for fruit and vegetables as for meat and fish; and as much for milk and cheese as for meat and fish."

In spite of all this new knowledge a considerable portion of our population is living on diets which cannot be regarded as ideal. This is a paradox. Next to ignorance, no doubt poverty has much to do with this state of affairs.

Cereals, legumens, flour, bread, and sugar are cheap and, therefore, form a large part of the diet of the masses, while milk, eggs, green vegetables and fruits, except in certain seasons of the year, are expensive and often beyond the reach of the poor. The result is that many diseases which ought to be under absolute control are still too prevalent.

The new knowledge of nutrition has been perhaps more promtly utilized by the progressive farmer and stock raiser because he could

readily see its benefits in the form of larger financial returns. Increased milk supply through the simple procedure of altering the quality of the cow's food; selective breeding and attention to the nutrition demands of his animals has largely increased his profits. 

The modern dairyman knows how to prevent bone disease in his cattle; he has learned how to prevent sterility in high priced bulls by feeding them amply on green succulent vegetables, and in the goitre belt he has succeeded in markedly reducing the incidence of the "hairless pig" malady that affected both lambs and pigs, by incorporating iodine in their food, and, in general, he has been able to produce a more superlative stock by the application of the knowledge of sound nutrition, careful breeding and scientific housing. 

In contrast to this, most people take it for granted that the type of human being that constitutes a normal man today is the best that can be produced.

It is my opinion and conviction that the superman of Nietsche, both in a physical and mental sense, can become a reality by the application of the same knowledge. Already we have a greater relative number of superlative specimens of boys and girls than we had ever before. When once the recognition of what constitutes an ideal diet has become public property and stress is laid on quality rather than quantity, man, too, will produce superlative stock.

But not only in the life of the individual, but in that of whole nations does diet play a vital role. In fact, it is doubtful if anything influences the welfare of nations as much as does the food on which they live. A careful study of the dietary habits of certain peoples shows that whenever meat and dairy foods, such as milk, cream, butter, cheese and eggs with vegetables and fruits added constitute the larger proportion of their diet, the physical development is excellent, but when agricultural products, especially cereals, become the dominating food, physical deterioration results as evidenced by stunted growth, carious teeth, lack of resistance and early old age. 

This is well illustrated in the food habits of two Indian tribes, the one, the Sikhs, inhabitating the north, and the other, the Bengalis, living in the south of India.

DIETS 

111 The diet of the people of the North consists largely of whole wheat, barley and corn, which undergo no milling process which could deprive these cereals of their mineral content and vitamins; in addition, they live on milk, butter, buttermilk, curds, legumens, fresh carrots and cabbage, tomatoes and root vegetables, and also oǹ fresh meats with bone and fat.

The people of the South, on the other hand, subsist largely on polished and cooked rice, white bread, legumens, condiments, vegetables oils, coffee and sugar, with very little milk, butter or fresh vegetables, a diet deficient in protein, fat, calcium, iron, phosphorus, salt and vitamins. What little fruit and vegetables and milk and meat there are available are wholly consumed by the better classes; the poorer classes find them too expensive to include them in their diet, with the result that millions of the Indian peoples exist the greater part of the year on a diet which means not only under-nutrition but faulty nutrition.

This difference in the diet between the North and the South reflects itself markedly in the physical condition of the two races. 

Malnutrition, anaemia, fevers, dysentery and other nutritional diseases are common occurrences in the people of the South, and they exhibit a marked inferiority in stature, weight, in stamina and efficiency, muscular development and endurance compared with the people of the north.

To definitely and scientifically demonstrate the superiority and inferiority of the respective diets of these two Indian races an English research worker, McCarrison, tested them on various animals. 

Albino rats living on a Sikh diet, under conditions of perfect hygiene and being exposed to the sun rays, showed for three years. not a case of illness amongst them, no deaths from sickness, no infantile mortality, a record which speaks for itself and needs no further analysis.

McCarrison also experimented on monkeys. They received a diet similar to that of the Sikhs of northern India, consisting of whole flour bread, butter, legumens, raw carrots and cabbage, unboiled whole milk and once a week small rations of raw meat with bones. On

that diet there was no infant mortality and no death of adult animals from natural causes.

On an unbalanced diet, however, such as the southern Indian people use, all kinds of diseases occurred amongst the monkeys, such as disease of the nose, sinuses and lungs, ears, diseases of the skin; gangrene of tail and feet, diseases of the blood, of the nerves, such as poly-neuritis and instability of the heart with dropsy and two cases of gastric cancer, a formidable array indeed. 

Experiments on other animals, such as guinea pigs, rabbits and pigeons gave similar results.

The same species of animals properly fed remained remarkably free from disease.

While the result of these experiments on animals cannot all be applied to man, we may nevertheless feel justified in concluding that many of the ailments to which man is heir are the result of faulty nutrition, and that a milk, fruit and vegetable diet, with the addition of a few eggs and meat, is the diet which insures the highest physical efficiency and health. 

(3) DIET AFTER FIFTY 

From a dietetic point of view we may divide our span of life into three periods. The first from birth to 25, when our food intake should provide not only for repairing the wear and tear but also for new growth and for the many activities in which young people engage; the second period from 25 to 50, during which the maintenance of the status quo is all that is necessary; and, finally, the third period from 50 to 75 or more, during which a general retreat and moderation in the intake of all foodstuffs is advisable. During this period of life we are beginning to go down hill, whether we are aware of it or acknowledge it or not. No use to pat ourselves on the back and boast that we are just as good as we were when we were 30; that may be true as far as some of our mental faculties are concerned, but it is certainly not true of our physical state. We all go down hill after 50; some go very fast so that it escapes neither their own nor the attention of friends; others go down hill very slowly so that the

DIETS 

113 change is hardly noticeable to the superficial observer, but they go down hill just the same.

There are two factors which influence the food intake after the age of 45 or 50. In the first place our physical activities grow less and less, and with this our energy output, i.e., metabolism diminishes, all of which calls for less food consumption. The tendency to put on weight which is a characteristic sign of ageing certainly points to a desirability of lessened consumption. Obesity is not desirable; it increases the incidence of gallstones, diabetes, heart disease and shortens the span of life. Thin people have better chance to grow old than fat people. In addition the wear and tear on our digestive and eliminative organs, especially the kidneys, begins to increase after 50 to a point where limitation of protein intake become desirable. 

This is the age when the new standard of diet is to be our guide, calling for from 25 to 60 grams of protein and from 1800 to 2500 calories a day.

Fletcherizing at this age is often made impractical, even impossible, by defective teeth and poorly functioning dental plates; but buttermilk drinking as advocated by Metchnikoff and the use of softer foods which require no mastication becomes more desirable as age advances. Much of the digestive troubles of old age would be eliminated if people would heed this advice. There are plenty of soft foods which require no teeth for mastication and which offer plenty variety and contain all the necessary food constituents, including vitamins, mineral substances, etc., for the proper maintenance of the status quo. 

The diet during these years, and especially during the later years of this period, should consist of buttermilk, acidophilus milk, klabbermilk, milk toast, Fruit juices and stewed fruits, some cooked vegetables ---lettuce and tomatoes finely cut up, well cooked cereals, mashed or baked potatoes, soft boiled or poached eggs, custards, plain pudding; sparingly, finely chopped and scraped beef, minced chicken, fish, with soups and weak tea or coffee.

Such a diet fills all the needs of the declining years and makes allowance for the slowing down of those processes and the changes

which are sure to come with age. Frequently, too, such a diet keeps the blood pressure within reasonable limits. 

Heavy, concentrated sweets; rich, highly seasoned and fatty foods; pastries, condiments and fried dishes; highly indigestible foods like beans, corn, pickles, nuts and rich cheeses, lobster, shrimp, and also fried foods have no place in the dietary of old people. 

And while the skill of the dentist can postpone the inevitable, and for a while at least deceive us into a state of security and tickle our pride and improve our looks, the realization of the fact, which nature indicates, that we go out of this world as we entered it, toothless and drinking milk, ought to guide us along the right way.

(4) DIET AND REPRODUCTION 

The influence of diet on reproduction is not yet fully appreciated. 

Records show that during the serious famine in 1917 in France and Germany many women noticed cessation of menses, resulting in a reduction of 50% in the number of conceptions; and there is little doubt that the semi-starvation diet to which so many American women subject themselves to remain stylishly thin has likewise an unfavorable effect on reproduction.

A study made by a physician of 206 women who complained of sterility, disclosed that all lived on a diet of but 1600 calories; in addition, most of these diets were found on closer analysis to lack vitamins, especially Vitamins A and D, and were deficient in calcium and iron. In 33% of these women menstruation was delayed two or more weeks, was scanty and of short duration. Correction of these dietetic errors resulted in regulation of their reproductive functions, with the result that of the 206 women studied 40, i.e., nearly 

20% became pregnant.

While our knowledge of the relation of dietary deficiencies to sterility, especially in man, is as yet rather fragmentary, enough has been learned to show that many of these deficiencies have a great influence on reproduction. Certain it is that with milk and milk products to furnish calcium and phosphorus, with green vegetables and fruits and whole grains to supply the necessary vitamins, with meat and eggs to provide complete protein, and with cereals, bread, potatoes

and sweets to furnish the required calories, no fear need be entertained of any harmful influence of our diet on this important function. 

(5) DIET AND PREGNANCY 

Pregnancy is a physiological condition. True, it occasionally leads to trouble and calls for special measures, which can only be formulated by a physician. But it is likely that many of these complications are caused by a failure to meet the fundamental dietetic needs of this state. Correct diet in fact has always been one of the principal problems in pregnancy. In former years, up to comparatively recent times, the fact that a pregnant woman had to take care of two seemed to demand a diet of high caloric value. But this has been found to be erroneous, if not dangerous.

It is the consensus of those best informed that gain in weight of the mother from the fourth month of pregnancy on should be restricted to 3 or 4 pounds a month. Only during the last 10 weeks of pregnancy is an increase in the food intake desirable as it is during this period that at least half of the total gain in weight of the foetus occurs.

More important than an increased food intake is the daily use of a pint of milk and an abundant consumption of vitamins, especially Vitamins A and D. These are generally provided by a liberal allowance of vegetables, salads, fruits, eggs, milk, butter and cream, and in winter, when fresh vegetables are not available or are too expensive, by the use of cod liver oil or viosterol. 

A liberal mineral supply, too, is important during pregnancy. 

Among the mineral elements, calcium is probably the most important because pregnancy creates an extra demand for calcium and phosphorus in the mother's diet, to enable her to furnish her unborn child with the necessary bone and tooth forming material. Moreover, if calcium is deficient in the blood of the pregnant mother certain symptoms make their appearance such as muscular weakness and a feeling of exhaustion, following slight exertion, muscular cramps of the lower extremities which usually come on in the night and towards morning

DIETS 

117 which often last an hour or more and which cause such severe pain that they arouse the patient from her sleep. 

Such calcium deficiency also causes puffiness of the face, hands and fingers, numbness and tingling of the extremities, loss of hair, dental caries and increased brittleness of teeth and nails as well as irritability of the nerves.

The prevention and treatment of all these symptoms consists in an adequate supply of calcium through a liberal milk and vegetable intake and the daily use of Vitamin D in form of Viosterol in doses of 20 drops 3 times a day, in order to insure the absorption of calcium. The combined use of calcium and Viosterol ought to be continued throughout the period of pregnancy and lactation which makes such heavy demand for increased calcium intake on the mother both for her own and her baby's sake. Such treatment will absolutely and completely prevent dental caries in the mother. 

Again, a patient on Viosterol exhibits greater strength and wellbeing; labor becomes more active and shorter. Above all it must be stated that the use of Viosterol during pregnancy has never been known to do harm, therefore there can be no objection to entrust the patient with its use.

I have given this subject much space and prominence on account of its obvious importance to every pregnant and lactating woman and her child. If all these claims are correct (and I can attest to their validity) a great advance has been made and the management of pregnancy has been completely revolutionized and simplified as well. 

Iron, too, has to be provided in the food in sufficient amounts. 

The fact that mother's milk and cow's milk are poor sources of iron, demands that the baby comes in the world with a sufficient supply of iron to last through the period of lactation. Fruits, meats, eggs, green vegetables and liver are good sources of iron. 

For the prevention of goitre iodine also ought to be a regular constituent of the diet of every pregnant woman. 

All these nutritional needs are best answered by a pint to a quart of milk a day, fruits and green vegetables adlibitum, eggs, whole grain cereals and potatoes once a day, meat three times a week, with

cheese, nuts and leguminous foods as meat substitutes and, finally, cod liver oil or Viosterol for Vitamin D.

(6) DIET AND LACTATION 

Successful lactation is to a certain extent a constitutional characteristic. It is an every day experience that the thinnest, frailest, hardest working women and those in poor circumstances seem to be best milk-producers, at least so far as the quantity is concerned,

whereas well-fed, fat and fullblooded women, living in affluence, produce very little milk and can hardly nurse their babies beyond the first few weeks of life. It seems that in the one type all the food consumed goes toward the making of milk, where as in the other type it produces mostly fat. 

On the other hand, it can hardly be doubted that successful lactation is largely influenced, either favorably or unfavorably, by diet. 

Generally speaking, it seems certain that when a mother's diet contains protein of high biologic value, an abundance of vitamins and mineral substances, together with a sufficient amount of carbohydrates and fats as a source of energy, a milk will be produced which will induce the greatest gain in weight for the nursing baby. 

Amongst the mineral substances, calcium is needed in greatest amounts to produce healthy bones and teeth. This is provided for by milk and green vegetables; the addition of eggs furnishes an abundance of phosphorus. The iron needed comes from the meat, eggs, fruits, and vegetables.

Last, but not least, the vitamin content of the mother's milk is almost entirely dependent on food, such as a liberal intake of leafy vegetables, fruit, milk, cream and butter.

Therefore, the daily diet of a nursing mother, to be best for the young offspring must contain: (1) Milk-from 1 to 2 pints daily, furnishing calcium, phosphorus and proteins. (2) Salads composed of lettuce, cabbage, carrots, celery, tomatoes, etc., to supply Vitamin A. (3) Leafy vegetables spinach, beet greens, turnip greens, kale, brussels sprouts, for the same purpose. (4) Citrous fruits to supply Vitamin C. (5) Eggs to supply iron and phosphorus. (6) Meats and organs, such as liver, to furnish proteins of high biological value. (7) Cream and butter to supply fat, Vitamins A and D. (8) Potatoes and cereals to furnish energy. (9) Whole wheat bread and whole grain cereals to supply Vitamin

DIETS 

121 B. (10) Vitamin D is best given to the nursing baby in the form of cod liver oil.

Dinner:

(7) DIET FOR CLIMATE AND SEASON 

a.

Diet in Summer and in Hot Climates 

There is no doubt that much of the inconvenience, discomfort and suffering in hot weather is due to injudicious use of food or drink. 

Seldom do we seek advice as to what clothes we shall wear in the summer. Everybody seems to know and instinctively feel that the less he wears, the cooler and more comfortable he is; and few people

would think it anything but crazy to wear winter clothes in the summer. But when it comes to food and drink, our desires seem to get the better of our judgment, with the result that many people fail to change their diet with the on-coming of the hot season or with a change of residence to a tropical climate, thus causing to themselves unnecessary discomfort, and even sickness.

On every side we meet people who are constantly complaining of the heat, hardly realizing that they, themselves, are to blame for many of their unbearable moments. For while undoubtedly heat, especially if combined with humidity, causes a good deal of mental and physical suffering, the food we eat, if it is unsuitable, intensifies the suffering caused by high temperature and high humidity, and has the greatest influence on how we bear up against the heat of the summer and of a tropical climate.

The heat of the body is produced by the oxidation of the carbohydrates, the fats and the proteins of our food, and depends, therefore, largely on the food intake, just as the heat of a stove depends on the fuel intake. The more food, the greater the production of heat. 

Some foods, like some fuels, naturally produce more heat than others. Fat, for instance, produces more than twice as much heat as does starch or sugar or protein; so does alcohol; and it stands to reason that such concentrated heat-producing foods should only be used in very limited quantities, if at all. Surely the use of alcohol in the summer is one of the great absurdities of a summer diet, be it in form of whiskey, wine or beer. For unless the increased production of heat is compensated by an increased loss through sweating and evaporation of the sweat, the body temperature rises and trouble results. 

It is largely through the evaporation of the sweat that the regulation of the body temperature is affected and the body kept cool in the summer; but this regulatory mechanism often fails on humid days when evaporation of sweat is made difficult.

The ingestion of large amounts of proteins, especially meat proteins, in hot weather, also increases heat production. Meat, increases heat production 40%; meat, therefore, should be eaten in great moderation in hot weather.

DIETS 

123 What is true of the average individual is doubly true of fat people. Fat is a poor conductor of heat and makes the regulation of the body temperature through evaporation difficult. 

It is known that on a warm and humid day the temperature of a fat individual at hard work rises considerably above normal. This effect, if carried to extremes, leads to heat stroke, which manifests itself in a high fever, rapid pulse, dizzziness, delirium, unconsciousness and death.

This combination of heat and humidity also has a bad effect on the nervous system, weakening the control of the higher nerve centers over the lower ones, leading to outbursts of "tropical fury", fits of passion caused by trivial incidents and an increase of crime. 

Another important factor in nutrition to be taken into consideration in hot weather is the fact that bacteria flourish in foods at an amazing rate and that extra care must be taken to guard against this bacterial contamination. Even an ordinary ice box is not always safe. Meat and milk are especially liable to bacterial decomposition. 

The immense increase of mortality from summer complaint in infancy always coincides with the first great wave of heat, especially if accompanied by humidity. And the great incidence at that season of the year of ptomaine, i.e. meat, sausage, fish, ice cream poisoning, and what not, should be a warning to be careful in the use of those foods in the summer. Milk in hot weather, especially that used for infants, should always be boiled, no matter what its source or name. 

True, we thereby kill the vitamins but those vitamins can easily be supplied in other less dangerous forms.

Meat and similar foods should be beyond suspicion of bacterial contamination, and all food should be screened against flies to minimize the danger of typhoid fever. 

From what has been said it follows that: (1) A summer diet should not contain an excessive number of calories, if possible, not over 2500. (2) Fat meats and other fatty foods, especially pork, bacon; rich oily salad dressings should be largely restricted or avoided

altogether; nor are waffles and wheat cakes and syrup, baked beans and pork good hot weather foods. (3) Meat generally should be eaten sparingly and only once a day. (4) Alcohol should be regarded as a poison in hot, and especially in humid weather. (5) Milk, especially buttermilk, can be taken in fair amounts 5 glasses a day, but the milk should be boiled or pasteurized. (6) No canned food when once opened should be saved for another meal. (7) The possibility of sausage poisoning and other foods poisoning should always be kept in mind and guarded against, especially at picnics. (8) Flies should be kept from the dining room, and the food should be screened. (9) A diet of vegetable proteins in the form of cereals, together with a small amount of milk and cream, bread and butter, supplemented by an abundance of fresh fruits and vegetables three times a day and the necessary amount of water, forms an ideal summer diet.

Babies, especially, should be provided with an extra supply of water in the summer. It would certainly seem that Nature points the way towards a sane and safe diet in the hot climates and in the hot season. The opportunity for outdoor life, the abundance of sunshine, filling the body with ultraviolet rays; the great varieties of fruits and vegetables offered in the summer, supplying our organism with all the necessary vitamins and storing them for use in the winter; the large intake of good, pure water forced on us by the increased thirst engendered by the profuse sweating, causing a decided lowering of the body temperature, all this should make the summer season a period of delight and increased health to all those who will heed the advice "Change your food as you would your clothes".

A good rule in the selection of foods for a summer diet is: 

Take fruits three times a day. 

Vegetables, cooked and raw, twice a day. 

Milk, skimmed milk, buttermilk, or klabbermilk twice a day.

DIETS

Potatoes, bread, breakfast foods, plain puddings, tapioca, etc., once or twice a day, with a little meat or fish or fowl three or four times a week and eggs three or four times a week.

Following is a sample diet for summer:

b. Diet in Very Cold Weather or in Cold Climates 

In contrast to a summer diet, cold weather calls for an abundance of protein foods especially in the form of meats, and a large amount of fats which increase heat production and create a pleasurable sensation of warmth. To these should be added vegetables, fruits, eggs, milk and water, with such other foods as the geographical location, the pocket book, the taste and habits dictate and allow. Hot drinks are specially desirable.

Alcohol has no place in the diet of either extremely cold or very hot climates. In hot weather it increases heat production and makes life miserable; in winter it tends to paralyze the peripheral circulation and favors freezing of the extremities. 

Breakfast: A cup of hot tea or coffee or cocoa or milk.

(8) TRAINING DIET 

There are two dietary problems involved in training which can only be solved by the application of well known principles so frequently enunciated. Of prime importance is the necessity to meet the increased expenditure of energy during training hours. Whereas the energy requirements of a man at moderate work are about 2500-3000 calories, in sports 9000 to 13000 is nothing uncommon. And since there must be a balance between energy output and intake, lest loss of weight results, and since all increase in work requires an increased use of carbohydrates and fats, especially the former, it follows that during training hours the increased output of energy must be met by an increased intake of carbohydrate and fat. 

In the second place, training for athletic contests calls for an enormous increase of muscular tissue. This can be brought about only by an increased protein consumption. 

With these 2 dietary principles as a basis, the needs of a diet in sports may be summed up as follows: (1) The protein intake is to be increased from 75 cc to 150 or more to provide for additional muscular tissue. (2) The increased energy output involved during the whole training period demands an increased consumption of carbohydrates or fats. The carbohydrates are far preferable for this purpose since their digestion is easier and their utilization more complete and

DIETS 

127 the energy derived from them is quicker available and there is less danger of an increase in fatty tissue. (3) Fluid and salt intake during the duration of training should be limited to the usual quantity unless there is a great loss of fluid through sweating. (4) The use of alcoholic drinks has no place in training since they produce quicker fatigue and cause defective accuracy and loss of concentration. (5) Tobacco, too, is to be avoided or at least reduced to a minimum. (6) Vitamins and minerals have to be supplied the same as in health. (7) Intestinal fermentation which causes distention of the abdomen and interferes with the proper functioning of the heart and lungs must be avoided. Insufficient mastication, the use of certain foods like cabbage, peas, beans, corn, hot breads and hot biscuits, poorly baked pies, fried and fatty foods all are liable to increase gas formation and endanger success in athletic contests. 

The use of glucose candy, or other sugars immediately before and during a contest to supply quickly available energy is one of the recognized dietary measures. Sugar, in fact, is the best and most natural stimulant for an athlete.

It goes without saying that early resting hours and plenty of restful sleep are a necessity.

(B) DIET IN DISEASE 

I have repeatedly stated that diet is the most important single factor in the treatment of disease. About 1/4 of all diseases could be prevented, if the application of known dietetic principles kept step with the great progress we have made in the last 25 years in the science of nutrition. But this does not imply that diet is a "cure all"; other remedies such as drugs, surgery, physiotherapy and even psychotherapy (suggestion) will continue to play their part,—often even the most important one, in the treatment of some diseases. 

Under these circumstances it would be folly to expect a layman to successfully undertake his own treatment. So much, too, depends on the correct diagnosis that the conclusion is all too evident that the treatment of disease, even the dietetic, is the function of the medical man. Any advice given here is for the purpose of enabling the patient to co-operate intelligently with the physician in charge hoping that no more of it will be put in practice by the reader than happens to agree with the doctor's directions. 

With this reservation I think the consideration of the dietetic treatment of at least a few of the commoner ailments to be well within the scope of this little book.

From a dietetic standpoint I divide all diseases into (1) Those in which diet constitutes the only remedy. Included in this class are: (a) All the so-called deficiency diseases. (b) All food allergies-idiosyncrasies.

DIETS 

129 (c) Other diseases for which a dietetic cure has been discovered. I refer here to the treatment of dysentery by the raw apple diet and rhinitis (acute nasal cold) and sinus trouble by the thirst cure. (2) Those diseases in the treatment of which diet, while not the only remedy, is by far the most important one; such as diabetes, obesity, and gout; diseases of the stomach and intestines, tuberculosis, kidney and liver troubles. (3) All other diseases, medical or surgical in which diet, while not the most important is a very essential part of the treatment. 

Included here are diseases of the skin, nervous system, fevers, surgical diseases and many others, too numerous to mention. 

(1) DISEASES WHICH ARE CURED BY DIET ALONE (A) DEFICIENCY DISEASES 

The term "deficiency diseases" which has orginally been applied only to "vitamin deficiencies", also includes deficiencies due to mineral substances. 

It is generally an abnormally one-sided diet which causes deficiencies. The exclusive use of sterilized milk in babies, large consumption of preserved foods, of milled grains and of patented flour, with a greatly restricted intake of fruits and vegetables, long continued milk diet in gastric ulcer-all these lead to vitamin deficiency diseases.

Much of our knowledge of these deficiency diseases has been derived from experiments on small animals. Valuable and indispensable as all these animal experiments have been in the past, it cannot be too strongly emphasized that not all the information gained from these experiments necessarily applies to man, and it is clear that the bedside must be the final place, the Supreme Court, so to speak, where knowledge concerning human beings is to be obtained. 

Carefully controlled and prolonged observations on patients, as well as mass experiments such as were made possible during the late war, must supplement our knowledge gained from animal experiments.

The World War, in the course of which large parts of the population of certain countries were forced to live on a diet of but 1300 calories daily, containing an average protein intake of only 33 grams, with very little milk, cream, butter, eggs, vegetables and fruits, taught us that such grossly deficient diets considerably increased the incidence of tuberculosis, anaemia, sterility and a number of other diseases.

How large the quantity of a food substance should be to prevent deficiency symptoms is not accurately known; perhaps it differs greatly in individual cases. Certain it is that for perfect health the body requires not a minimal but optimal amount. 

That this advice is frequently not heeded, is an every day experience. Patients often state that they take plenty of green vegetables, fruits, milk, eggs, etc., but on a closer investigation it is revealed that the amount taken is wholly inadequate. Ready-to-serve foods, canned, pasteurized and highly milled, with little butter and cream, and a preponderance of cooked vegetables and cereals, with an excess of concentrated carbohydrate foods like sugar, syrups, candies, together with the use of tea and coffee in place of milk, often make the bulk of their diet and thus account for the deficiency symptoms from which they suffer. This is especially true of girls employed in shops and stores; in the hurry to get downtown in the morning their breakfast consists of tea or coffee and toast, or often nothing at all. Economic and other reasons restrict their lunches to soda fountain counter lunches, pies, chili cancarne, coca cola, pork and beans, ice cream and pie, and coming home in the evening after a day's hard work they are too tired to fully partake of and enjoy the full family dinner that is waiting for them.

Such defective diets, often accentuated by an insane desire to remain thin, are sadly lacking in the so-called protective foods as well as calcium, iron, iodine and proteins, which go to produce health and vigor and well being.

DIETS 

131 

(1) VITAMIN DEFICIENCY DISEASES (a) NIGHTBLINDNESS XEROPHTHALMIA 

Not until comparatively recent years has the importance in nutrition of Vitamin A, its power to prevent and cure certain ailments in man and to promote growth and vigor and health in children and adults been duly appreciated. Experiments made on small animals by several American investigators laid the scientific foundation and evolved our present conception of the important role played by Vitamin A in nutrition. These experiments disclosed that animals fed for a long period of time on purified food mixtures, which resembled milk in their composition but in which the butter fat was replaced by lard, or olive oil failed to show normal growth and developed a number of abnormal symptoms and signs, which were promptly cured by merely substituting butter fat for the lard; this failure of the animals to thrive on lard or olive oil was due to a deficiency in these fats of the accessory food factor called Vitamin A. 

Vitamin A is found especially in green, leafy vegetables-green asparagus, green peas, yellow corn, alfalfa, lettuce, cabbage, watercress, celery tops, tomatoes, carrots, all strongly colored fruits, whole milk, cream, butter, cream cheese, heart, liver, kidneys, blood, herring, eel, cod liver oil or similar fish oils, egg yolk, sardelles, pineapple, oysters and sweet potatoes. In less amounts in potatoes, celery, beets, kohlrabi, radishes, white cabbage, cereal grains, flour, rice, sago, mushrooms, apples, pears, meat, fish, honey and onions. Our richest source by far is cod liver oil. 

Starches, sugars, wheat flour, degerminated corn meal, polished rice, mushrooms, pork, lard, olive oil, cottonseed oil, peanut oil, are practically devoid of it. 

It is thus evident that a plentiful supply of this vitamin by our natural foods is guaranteed throughout the year and that it is unlikely that a mixed diet under ordinary circumstances lacks this important food factor.

The Vitamin A content of milk depends entirely on the kind of food the animals eat. Milk of cows, for instance, fed in pasture is

richer in Vitamin A than when fed in stalls; likewise summer milk is richer than winter milk. This shows that the Vitamin A content of milk and butter and cream can be increased or decreased at will and that the green vegetable foods are the original source of this Vitamin. 

The Vitamin A content of foods also bears an intimate relationship to their coloring matter; carrots, yellow corn, yellow milk, yellow butter are all richer in Vitamin A than the white varieties. In fact carotine, the yellow color substance of carrots and other foods, is regarded as the mother substance of this Vitamin. 

In man, Vitamin A deficiency frequently leads to night blindness. 

There is a pigment contained in the retina of the eye, called the "visual purple". In bright light this is constantly being bleached; as long as the light is bright, however, but little of this purple is needed for good vision, and consequently the bleaching does not become noticeable. But in faint light and especially in darkness vision becomes very defective unless this visual purple is being regenerated. In Vitamin 

A deficiency, however, the power of the retina to regenerate this visual purple is lost; the result is night blindness (also called moon blindness in animals). An epidemic of this trouble was observed amongst the fisherfolk of Labrador and Newfoundland. Spending their days in the bright sunshine they suffered a loss of this visual purple. Their diet being poor in Vitamin A (it consisted of white flour, biscuits, salted beef, pork, molasses, oleomargarine, potatoes, peas and beans), they could not form the pigment anew. Hence when night came they became so blind that they were a menace to themselves and others. 

They could not safely find their way and would step into ditches or off precipices. The addition of Vitamin A containing foods to their diet quickly put a stop to this epidemic. Cod liver oil in doses of 

2 to 4 teaspoonfuls daily was especially effective.

When the Vitamin A deficiency becomes complete, a characteristic eye disease develops called "xerophthalmia". This leads to a general infection and destruction of the eye and blindness.

An outbreak of this disease was reported during the World War from Denmark, where, due to the fact that all the available butter was exported and sold to Germany, their own children were deprived

DIETS 

133 of it and as a result developed a serious Vitamin A deficiency resulting in ulceration of the eye ball.

Vitamin A has also been called the "Anti-Infection Vitamin”, because a complete absence of it from the diet of experimental animals was found to render these animals susceptible to infections. But this relationship between infection and Vitamin A deficiency so commonly found in experiments on animals is by no means an established fact in man. Investigations extending over 4 to 12 months, and carried on with infants and children, some of whom were given daily skimmed milk (lacking Vitamin A) others milk and Viosterol, others milk and 3 teaspoonfuls of standardized cod liver oil and a fourth group milk and 6 teaspoonfuls of cod liver oil, revealed no significant difference in the incidence of colds or respiratory infections in the 4 groups.

This justifies the warning not to place unlimited confidence in the propaganda published by manufacturers of cod liver oil as a preventative of colds. (b) BERI-BERI Beri-Beri, also called polyneuritis, is a disease which for centuries has been prevalent in an epidemic form in Japan, China, Dutch East Indies and a few other countries where large numbers of the population live mainly on rice, which by the process of polishing has been deprived of its Vitamin B.

In the United States the disease is rare unless imported or caused by greatly restricted diets taken over a long period of time. 

In 1887 the discovery was made that pigeons fed on polished rice developed multiple neuritis, became helpless and died from symptoms which resembled those seen in human beri-beri. 

A watery extract made from rice polishings cured the symptoms and frequently saved the lives of even dying pigeons in the most dramatic way.

Vitamin B is widely distributed in both vegetables and animal foods. It is abundant in milk, eggs, glandular organs, green leaves and seeds, whole grains, roots and tubers, carrots, spinach, cabbage and tomatoes, oranges, lemons, grapefruit, prunes. Apples and pears,

endosperm of grains, muscle meat, fish or fowl, white flour, polished rice or degerminated corn meal are all poor sources of Vitamin B. 

Our best sources, however, are yeast, especially brewer's yeast, and wheat germ.

This abundant supply of Vitamin B in our foods insures us ordinarily against this Vitamin deficiency. 

One of the early symptoms of Vitamin B deficiency is lack of appetite. Vitamin B, in fact, has been called the "appetite vitamin”. 

Soon the characteristic signs of Beri-Beri make their appearance consisting in weakness, severe nerve pains (neuritis), followed by paralysis of the extremities, dropsy and finally death.

In infants one of the early symptoms of Vitamin B deficiency is impaired alertness; and the experiments on rats carried on at the 

University of Chicago indicated that a deficiency of Vitamin B seriously affected their ability to learn. And the Department of Public Health of Illinois published the statement that the administration of Vitamin B in the form of yeast or wheat germ decidedly improved a childs intelligence. 

(c) SCURVY 

Scurvy was probably the first deficiency disease known and the one for which the cure was discovered long before the real nature and cause of the disease was suspected. 

As early as the thirteenth century the armies of the Crusaders were afflicted with scurvy, and from then on to the middle of the nineteenth century it was a common and fatal disease on board of sailing vessels, when the long voyages compelled the sailors to subsist for many months and even years on little or no fresh food. It was also a common occurrence in jails, during war in beleaguered cities, in prison camps and in time of famine. Today it is much rarer. 

It is still, however, too frequently met with amongst babies. 

The disease is due to a lack of Vitamin C, either because the foods containing Vitamin C are absent from the diet, or because the Vitamin C in the food has been destroyed by cooking.

135 

DIETS 

Our best sources of Vitamin C are oranges, limes, lemons, tomatoes, fresh or canned, carrots, paprika, lettuce, spinach, onions, apples, pineapples, bananas, strawberries, raspberries, fresh raw meat, raw milk, especially raw summer milk, all of which contain this Vitamin in abundance.

Vitamin C is water soluble and extremely sensitive to heat; in the absence of air, however, heat does not wholly destroy Vitamin C. 

This is especially true when the food is acid or when acid is added to it.

Vitamin C exerts considerable influence on resistance to bacterial infections, as well as on regulation of body temperature. An instance of this occurred in a baby who was fed for a long time on Vitamin C poor dry milk and in consequence developed frequent rises of temperature which were promptly controlled by changing the food. and by adding Vitamin C in the form of orange juice.

The danger of an insufficient consumption of Vitamin C is always to be reckoned with. Vitamin C is the most sensitive of all Vitamins; cooking, sterilization, pasteurization and conservation of foods rob them of Vitamin C; but this danger can easily be overcome by eating foods which, like oranges, tomatoes, green vegetables, carrots, and apples require no cooking and are rich in Vitamin C. The feeding of children on such foods almost from birth has had the effect that infantile scurvy has practically disappeared and is only found in children of immigrants who still cling to the old method of feeding. 

It is in the prevention of scurvy that raw food diet finds its greatest justification, especially in children whose needs of this accessory food substance are the greatest. This does not argue in favor of a raw food diet as a panacea for every ill and for everybody as advocated by certain modern reformers. An addition of a reasonable amount of raw food to our diet is, however, imperative. 

The general symptoms of scurvy are loss of appetite, loss of weight, weakness and pallor. But the characteristic signs are hemorrhages in the skin and mucus membranes, swelling and blue discoloration and bleeding of the gums; the teeth become loose and fall

THE HUMAN FU EL 

out; and frequently hemorrhages between the bone and its covering make their appearance and cause the extremities to swell. 

The disease is especially prevalent in children at the time of dentition, when due to the exclusive use of pasteurized milk or patent cereal foods, Vitamin C is absent from the diet. 

The disease also occurs in adults. There is really no great differance between adult and infantile scurvy. The symptoms in adults are weakness, shortness of breath on exertion, swelling of ankles, loss of appetite, joint pains, often mistaken for rheumatism, sore and bleeding gums, often slight fever, hemorrhage from the nose and kidneys, rectum and skin, etc.

The disease is readily, often miraculously cured by foods rich in 

Vitamin C, especially citrous fruits such as oranges, orange juice, lemons, limes, grapefruit, tomatoes or tomato juice. It is on record that as early as 1795 lemon juice was introduced in the rations of the British Army to prevent scurvy. That raw meat is an anti-scorbutic is shown by the experience of Artic explorers. 

One of the astounding things about the treatment of scurvy is the rapidity with which children afflicted with it respond to correct treatment; within 48 to 72 hours a great change in these patients becomes apparent. 

It can be stated that the routine use of orange juice in babies almost from birth on constitutes one of the important contributions to the feeding of infants. 

(d) RICKETS 

Of all the Vitamin diseases, rickets is probably the most common and the most serious both with regard to its immediate effects and to its remote results.

The principal reason for its great prevalence is the fact that the supply of Vitamin D in our natural foods is never quite sufficient and unless they are supplemented by cod liver oil, irradiated foods or the use of the quartz lamp in the winter and plenty of sunshine in the summer, rickets is bound to develop. So frequent is the disease that it has been estimated that, in cities at least, as many as 90%

DIETS 

137 of the infant child population is afflicted with abnormal conditions of bones and teeth and also exhibit the more obscure symptoms of rickets. Rickets is primarily a disease of children below the age of 

2 years and consists in deficient calcification of the bones. But it does not affect the skeleton alone, but the whole body. The children grow pale, lose their appetite, become constipated, sweat a great deal especially about the head and show a great tendency to colds and various nervous manifestations of greater or lesser seriousness, such as holding the breath or even convulsions. It is this early stage which is often overlooked by parents. This is all the more deplorable as it is in this stage that rickets is easily curable before serious damage is done.

The disease is more frequent in the spring because the ultraviolet rays of the sun which play a great role in its prevention are not available and not very potent during the long winter months. 

The disease is especially prevalent in Europe and in America. 

In the United States it is the Negro and Italian population which are the most frequent sufferers, because their living and housing conditions are such as to admit little sunshine and also because the newer knowledge of nutrition has not as yet become a vital part of their lives.

Its chief causes are either an insufficient supply of calcium and phosphorus or a lack in the diet of Vitamin D. Vitamin D makes the retention of calcium and phosphorus in the bone of a growing child possible.

The prevention and cure of rickets, by Vitamin D antedates our knowledge of its cause.

It has long been known that cod liver oil cures rickets. Already in 1870 its use in Germany as a household remedy was an established procedure because experience had taught that it had the power to protect children from the disease and its many complications. But the nature of the effective substance in cod liver oil remained a mystery until the recent discovery in 1922 of Vitamin D in the oil.

Our principal sources of Vitamin D are: 

(1) Cod liver oil and similar fish oils. Puffer fish oil, for instance, is 15 times as powerful a cod liver oil; likewise goose fish liver, shark liver, haddock liver and especially halibut liver (haliver oil) are all rich sources of Vitamin D. (2) Bourbot, the nearest fresh water relation of cod fish, found in the Great Lakes and seldom used as human food, was found to have a liver oil eight times as active in Vitamin D as cod liver oil. Likewise salmon oil given in doses of 1/2 to 1 tablespoonful daily is said to be more potent than cod liver oil; besides it contains Vitamin A, and is well taken and well tolerated. 

The next best source of Vitamin D is egg yolk. When added to regular milk formulas it prevents rickets. The diet of hens and the amount of ultraviolet rays (sunshine) available to them effects the Vitamin D content of eggs. Eggs produced in the absence of ultraviolet rays do not prevent rickets. Ultraviolet irradiation increases the Vitamin D content of eggs tenfold.

Cod liver oil, too, if fed to chickens to the extent of 22 of their ration, improves the Vitamin D content of the eggs. 

It may be of interest here to mention that ultraviolet rays also increase egg production and hatchability of the eggs. At the 

University of Wisconsin a dozen hens without light treatment produced 73 eggs in a month; with irradiation 173; and the hatch-ability increased from 28.5% to 63%. These facts are especially important during the winter months when the natural ultraviolet rays of the sun are not available. (3) The third source of Vitamin D is butter fat, which, however, compared with the other two is rather poor in Vitamin D. 

Sunlight, irradiation and feeding of the milk cows with cod liver oil increases the Vitamin D content of the butter fat considerably. (4) One of the richest sources of Vitamin D is Irradiated Ergosterol also called Viosterol. Ergosterol is a fat-like substance contained in nearly all vegetable cells and animal tissues. When irradiated with the ultraviolet rays of the sun or quartz lamp it changes

DIETS 

139 into a Vitamin D rich substance. This irradiated ergosterol when dissolved in a neutral vegetable oil and adjusted to a Vitamin D content of 1333 units per gram, is called in the United States "Viosterol" and it is 100 times as potent as is cod liver oil. If the irradiation is kept up long enough, the potency of 

Viosterol may range as high as 200,000 or 700,000 times that of the average cod liver oil. Thus it is seen that Viosterol is far superior to cod liver oil in Vitamin D potency. (5) Irradiated foods: In 1924, less than 3 years after the discovery of Vitamin D, it was reported that certain foods could be endowed with the properties of Vitamin D by exposing them to the ultra violet rays of the quartz lamp. Olive oil, lards, cottonseed oil, linseed oil, corn and cocoanut oil, oleomargarine, butter, wheat flour, shredded wheat, cream of wheat, oatmeal, corn starch, meat, milk, milk powder, evaporated milk, vegetables, yeast, orange juice, chocolate, ice cream, egg yolk, butter, are some of the foods which are now offered to the public as Vitamin 

D rich or sunshine foods. 

Liquid petrolatum, sugar and rancid oils and fats cannot be irradiated. The commercial irradiation of these foods is quite successful and economical and does not increase their cost. 

It requires an exposure to ultra violet rays of only a few seconds. to one minute; the maximum exposure is 15 minutes, after which time the food is 250,000 times as active in Vitamin D as is cod liver oil.

These irradiated foods are quite stable in their Vitamin D content which is not destroyed by ordinary cooking. The importance of these irradiated foods and of Viosterol, especially in the dietary of children, pregnant and lactating mothers is all the greater since the Vitamin D content of our common foods is very limited. Even milk contains only a small quantity; not enough to prevent or cure rickets. 

In adults ordinary diet is probably sufficient to cover our needs of Vitamin D. But in children, especially during the winter months, larger doses of Vitamin D are necessary than are generally provided for in their diets. The same is true when in times of famine or in

blockaded countries the natural supply as ordinarily furnished by eggs and milk and butter is seriously curtailed.

In pregnancy and lactation, too, where there is an extra demand for Vitamin D, ordinary foods are not potent enough to supply the necessary amount.

Our poorest sources of this important Vitamin are: fruits, green vegetables, cereals and even milk. Especially poor are the vegetable fats, cottonseed oil, peanut oil, olive oil and cocoanut oil. Oleomargarine, made from vegetable fats lacks Vitamin D; that made from animal fats contains a little. 

As previously stated, the main function of Vitamin D is the prevention and cure of rickets and caries by promoting absorption and assimilation and deposition of calcium in the bones and teeth. 

In doing so it also protects children against deformities of the thorax which lead to so-called pigeon breasts and limit the vital capacity of the lungs; and it likewise prevents pelvic malformations which in female babies result in later years in the narrowing of the birth canal of women and thus make labor difficult and dangerous and Caesarian section necessary.

Vitamin D also cures the tendency of children to convulsions so frequently associated with and caused by rickets. It is also indicated in the management of delayed union of fractures and in the treatment of softening of the bones, occasionally seen in pregnant and lactating mothers.

Vitamin D is often called the Sunshine Vitamin, because it is a substitute for the actinic rays of the sun. It was in 1909 that it was discovered that ultraviolet rays of the sun had the power to cure rickets.

In the tropics, or where babies are able to spend a great deal of time outdoors in the sun, rickets is rare. Sunshine, however, filtering through window glass, heavy clothing, dusty and smoke-laden atmosphere, loses a large portion of those rays and becomes ineffective. 

And even under the most favorable sunshine conditions, the intensity of the ultraviolet rays in a court between two buildings is only 10% of the intensity of the rays on top of the building. The shady streets,

DIETS 

141 the gloomy courts and dark rooms, together with a diet poor in vitamins, are the cause of the pale faces of city dwellers and the prevalence of rickets amongst their offspring. 

To counteract this condition, the more general introduction of roof gardens, organized hikes of children in the parks or green fields, and the provision of sand piles for children's play are recommended, as sand has marked capacity to reflect ultraviolet rays. Ten minutes play outdoors in the sun will make up for the dearth of light during the school hours.

Vitamin D therapy must be commenced early in life to insure proper development of skeleton and teeth; even the prenatal supply through the mother is important since the calcification of the first teeth takes place before birth.

Experiments indicate that if the diet of the mother during the last three months of pregnancy can be made approximately correct in mineral intake and vitamin content, it will, to a large degree, prevent the development of rickets. When, however, the diet consists almost exclusively of cereals, especially oatmeal and corn, which lack not only calcium but also Vitamin D, rickets is inevitable. On such a diet children grow fat, but exhibit increased susceptibility to infection and colds and finally develop rickets. 

The diagnosis of rickets is rather easy. The children get pale and develop flabby muscles and pot belly; when asleep they sweat about the head so as to wet the pillows; and they finally develop spinal curvature, bent legs (bow legs), pigeon breasts and serious pelvic deformities. In addition they suffer from vague pains which cause them to cry out, unexpectedly, especially in the nights; they sleep poorly, constantly move the head on the pillow so that they rub the hair off the back of the head; and many of these children are in the habit of holding their breath and become victims of frequent convulsions. 

Prevention and treatment are easy and consist: (1) In exposure to the rays of the sun in the summer and to the ultraviolet rays of the quartz lamp in the winter months.

(2) In administration of cod liver oil or similar oils, of viosterol, of irradiated foods, and other rich sources of Vitamin D. (3) In correct diet which should contain much animal fat, such as butter, cream, egg yolk and cream cheese; also milk, green vegetables and potatoes, with meat and liver in older children. 

Such a diet, while not rich in Vitamin D, contains an abundance of calcium and phosphorus as well as Vitamin A. A preponderance of cereals must be avoided and what cereals are given on account of their energy-producing quality must be supplemented by the daily use of milk and one or two eggs. 

Regarding ultraviolet irradiation, it should be stated that the child should be exposed daily to ultraviolet rays such as are present in sunshine, especially between the hours of from 10 a.m. to 2 p.m. 

Sunshine is especially effective in the summer months. Its place in the winter can be taken by irradiation of the nude body with the rays of a quartz lamp under the direction of a physician.

As to the medicinal treatment, every child should receive cod liver oil, a source of Vitamin D two hundred times richer than the richest food, and a sure remedy for rickets. 

In this country, certain manufacturers tried to convince the medical profession and the public that olive oil was just as good; that cod liver oil owed its virtue to no mysterious substance and was no different from any other fat. The result was the manufacture and sale of such preparations as "Maltine and olive oil". The discovery of Vitamin D in cod liver oil and the demonstration of its absence in olive oil, has enabled cod liver oil to stage one of the most remarkable comebacks in medical history. 

(e) VITAMIN E DEFICIENCY 

One of the most startling announcements in the last decade has been that laboratory animals, such as mice and rats, after having been successfully raised on simplified diets containing proteins, carbohydrates, fats, mineral substances, and all the known vitamins, reach full maturity, become pregnant but are unable to keep their new foetus beyond a few days. This has been found to be due to the absence

DIETS 

143 from the diet of the new Vitamin E, which has also been called the "Vitamin of Reproduction".

Vitamin E containing foods given as late as the 5th day after copulation prevent the death of the foetus. Even a single administration of Vitamin E often proves sufficient. Whether this vitamin is of any significance in or is concerned with the reproductive function of man is a matter of serious doubt. 

Vitamin E occurs abundantly in green leaves of lettuce, spinach, watercress, alfalfa seeds! also in germ oil of cereals; wheat germ oil is the richest source.

Small quantities are found in muscle meat, egg yolk and fat. 

(f) PELLAGRA 

Pellagra is a disease which is endemic in the southern states of the United States; also in Italy, Spain, and less so in other countries. 

It had its inception in the introduction, in 1880, of highly milled cereals, especially corn and wheat.

It is the one deficiency disease, which like beri-beri in Japan and China, assumes endemic proportions in this country. Thus, in 1930, there were recorded 7146 deaths from pellagra in this country, and figuring that for each death there were 35 cases of outspoken and many more unsuspected cases, one can get an idea of the widespread incidence of pellagar. 

Of these 7146 deaths reported, 7000 or 99% occurred in those states which lie within the cotton belt and in more isolated rural communities of the South.

The disease can be produced by a diet consisting largely of cornmeal, white flour, polished rice, sugar, molasses, fat pork, sweet potatoes and coffee. The addition of milk to this diet, prevents its outbreak or brings about a cure.

The initial symptoms of pellagra are a beefy red tongue, persistent diarrhoea, roughness of the backs of hands with dark discoloration, preceded by obstinate salivation, lack of appetite, belching, constipation, insomnia and loss of memory. Later acute mania and dementia set in and many of these patients end in the insane asylum.

There is no disease which is more amenable to preventive and curative measures than is pellagra. By simple and inexpensive additions to the daily menus one state institution for the insane has reduced its annual mortality from pellagra from 6.2% to 0.1%. The treatment is purely dietary. So far 35 foods have been tested as to their ability to prevent pellagra. Of these, milk, including buttermilk, canned milk and dried milk, fresh beef, canned corn beef, canned salmon, canned haddock, liver, tomatoes, turnip greens, and canned English peas, have been found to prevent pellagra if added in a sufficient quantity to an otherwise faulty diet. The best source of Vitamin G, however, is undoubtedly dry yeast, commercial wheat germ, and fresh lean beef.
