(2) MINERAL DEFICIENCIES (a) DENTAL CARIES 

Dental caries is a very common and, from an economic standpoint, a very expensive disease. Although there should be no decay either of the first or second set of teeth, such an ideal condition is very rare in civilized countries. In a considerable percentage of children the temporary teeth hardly appear before they begin to rot; and not infrequently they are decaying before the second set arrives. Nor are the permanent teeth of much better material; the six year molars especially seem susceptible to decay. 

The prevalence of caries in school children can be judged by the statement that for the city of Boston it would require a staff of 1000 full-time dentists to cope with the situation. Likewise, it is reported that of 1192 children in public schools and orphanages in Michigan, tooth decay is present in from 80 to 99%; active caries in 72 to 

90%; and that the number of teeth affected between the fifth and thirteenth years averaged from 3 to 4, and the number of cavities 

7 per child.

These figures, no doubt, could be duplicated in every part of our country and they throw a great doubt on the claim often heard that the average American diet is adequate so far as preservation of sound teeth is concerned.

DIETS 

145 As to the cause of caries it is definitely regarded as a dietary deficiency disease. True, there exist even today wide differences of opinion regarding the particular dietary principle involved in the prevention of tooth decay. Some emphasize the value of Vitamin C; others hold that Vitamin D or an abundance of sunshine or ultraviolet rays is the vital factor; even the importance of Vitamin A is stressed by some; others, again, regard a proper supply of calcium, phosphorus, fluorine, magnesium and other mineral substances as the important dietary factor; others deny all this and seek to connect bad teeth with excessive consumption of sugar and other fermentable foods.

The truth probably is that while many different factors contribute their share to tooth decay, the development of strong healthy teeth and the prevention of caries is, in the last analysis, dependent on correct diet, notwithstanding the claims made for tooth powders, tooth pastes, tooth brushes and mouth washes; it certainly is far from proven that the use of such agencies has any measurable effect on preserving teeth from decay. Wild animals certainly use no tooth brushes or other artificial aids to clean their teeth, yet tooth decay amongst them is almost unknown.

Many problems have yet to be solved and many seemingly contradictory facts relating to tooth decay have to be cleared up before a full solution of the subject of tooth decay is at hand. 

Much of our knowledge of caries and its relation to diet has come from observations and experiments made by Ed. and May Mellanby of England. According to Ed. Mellanby it is possible to produce in animals any degree of perfection or imperfection of teeth by different diets given during the period of dental development. He contends that since teeth are closely related to bones in chemical composition, the dietetic conditions which control sound bone formation also determine formation of teeth. Accordingly, milk, egg yolk, vegetables and cod liver oil, and all substances rich in Vitamin D and calcium, produce perfectly calcified, bright, shiny and evenly arranged teeth, while cereals which contain no Vitamin D and little calcium, produce defectively calcified, dull discolored and unevenly arranged teeth.

According to May Mellanby, the same kind of diet which has been shown to influence dental structure and caries in experimental animals also influences the incidence and spread of caries of the teeth of children.

She experimented on a group of children averaging 5½ years of age, giving them a cereal-free and calcium and vitamin rich diet for a period of six months. The result was that the spread of old carious points present at the beginning of the test and the development of new ones was checked in spite of the fact that at their age dental caries is common and tends to spread rapidly. 

These facts easily explain why the inhabitants of Chili, who live chiefly on cereals, have bad teeth, whereas Eskimos, living on fats, fish oils, organs and bones have excellent teeth.

How much the eating of candy and its fermentation in the mouth and the failure to clean teeth, contribute to tooth decay is still in doubt. 

According to most investigators, tooth decay is primarily a nutritional disease; and neither acidity of the mouth nor lack of oral cleanliness will, in any way, interfere with the arrest of caries, if correct diets are given. Correct diet beats tooth brushes and tooth pastes.

Only if sugars, starches and cereals form the main diet, to the exclusion of the more essential elements and protective foods, does caries result.

That Vitamin C also is important in the production of sound teeth is borne out by an investigation made by the Department of 

Pathology, University of Chicago. Studies were made on 422 children between 10 and 17 years of age; 323 of these were observed for two years and 99 for one year as a control group. 

All children had a good, mixed and complete diet. But the test group of 323 received 16 ounces of orange juice and the juice of a lemon per day and the control group did not. As a result, all inflammatory changes of the gums disappeared and dental caries was arrested in 49.8% and retarded in 16.4% of the test group.

Since tooth development in the foetus starts as early as the third month of pregnancy and at birth the first teeth are fully formed

DIETS 

147 in the jaw, it follows that the quality of the teeth is determined during the prenatal period. The calcium and phosphorus necessary for formation of good teeth come from the mother. The period of lactation is quite as important as the period of pregnancy. During both there is an increased demand of calcium in the mother by the foetus and the growing infant for perfect formation of teeth and bones. When the food does not supply ample calcium and phosphorus, the mother takes these minerals from her own system to meet the needs of the child, mobilizing the calcium from her own hones and teeth to regulate mineral metabolism. The result is caries and possible softening of bones (osteomalacia) in the mother, and rickets and tetany and bad teeth formation in the child. 

(b) ANAEMIAS 

In no department of medicine has the increase in knowledge been so pronounced and so productive of results as it has been in the subject of anaemia. This change has been very largely brought about by the contribution of American research workers and clinicians. Ever since the discovery of the cure of pernicious anaemia with liver by Minot and Murphy, a flood of light has been shed upon this subject, dissipating the darkness and ignorance which prevailed up till then; and a hitherto incurable and fatal disease has been added to the ever growing list of curable affections.

From a dietetic point of view all anaemias may be divided into two kinds: (1) Those that respond to liver treatment, i.e. all pernicious anaemias. (2) Those which are cured by iron. The latter used to be known as secondary anaemias.

PERNICIOUS ANAEMIAS 

Until the discovery of the liver treatment pernicious anaemia was a fatal disease. Characterized by extreme pallor or lemon yellow color, great debility, increasing weakness, loss of appetite, gastric and gastrointestinal disturbances such as diarrhoea and sore tongue, nervous symptoms, such as numbness and tingling of feet and hands, coldness

of extremeties, its onward course used to be interrupted by remissions, either natural or due to medication, such as arsenic. During these remissions, which often lasted months and occasionally longer, the patient, though feeling fine, continued to show signs of illness both externally and in his blood picture, and generally, after one or more such remissions, continued to lose ground and died. 

All this has been changed by the epoch making discovery of G. R. Minot and W. P. Murphy of Boston that feeding these patients daily with adequate amounts of liver brought about a cure. 

This liver treatment is so effective that it has been said that any case which does not respond to it is not true pernicious anaemia. 

There is only one requirement that is regarded essential, i.e., that the amount of liver given daily must be between 2/3 and 1 pound, or even more in some cases, and that after a cure if effected, a certain maintenance ration of about 1/3 pound of liver daily be continued indefinitely. Instead of liver the use of liver extract in powder form overcomes the monotony of a liver diet and is a distinct step in advance. 

ANAEMIAS CURED WITH IRON (SECONDARY ANAEMIAS) 

Red blood corpuscles, like all other substances in the body undergo a constant wear and tear; failure to produce new ones leads to anaemia.

Most of these anaemias, being due to an iron deficiency, are cured by the administration of iron. Iron in these cases acts almost as strikingly as does liver in the pernicious type. It appears to stimulate the bone marrow in much the same way as liver extract does in pernicious anaemia. This iron treatment is aided by a diet composed of iron containing foods. 

In the order of their efficiency these are: (1) Liver, kidney, heart, chicken gizzard, spleen and sweet breads. (2) Raw meats. (3) Green vegetables such as spinach, broccoli, beet greens, turnip greens, lettuce, tomatoes, asparagus, carrots, brussel sprouts.

DIETS 

149 These vegetables are important also on account of their vitamin content. Also, peas, beans and nuts. (4) Fruits, especially peaches, prunes, grapes, apples, raisins and apricots.

Dairy products, especially milk and curds, as well as fish and fish livers, are poor sources of iron. 

(c) SIMPLE GOITRE 

Goitre is an enlargement of the thyroid gland and is caused by a lack of iodine in the gland. If the right amount is not supplied and maintained by the food, the thyroid gland becomes enlarged in an effort to meet the demand for iodine. This demand is especially urgent during adolescence, in pregnancy and lactation and at the time of the menopause.

The disease is especially common in young girls during the age of puberty and occurs especially in those parts of the country where the water and soil contain but little iodine and where the foods grown in that soil have a very low iodine content. 

The sea is the great source of iodine salts. Agar-agar, kelp and seaweeds in general; the flesh of seafish and shellfish, salmon, roe and cod liver oil, all are rich sources of iodine. 

The deficiency of iodine in the soil and in the water and the foods is especially marked in the so-called goitre regions of the Pacific Northwest and the Great Lakes, the mountains and in nearly all places far removed from the ocean. To secure the necessary amount of iodine through the food grown in such soil is often difficult. 

The prevention of goitre is as simple as it is effective. 

It consists in the administration of small amounts of iodine to every young girl before and during adolescence, and also to pregnant and nursing mothers. The amount needed is very minute and may be given in the form of iodized salt.

This administration of iodine will prevent 90% and will cure one-half of uncomplicated goitre cases. Of 2190 children in Arkon, Ohio, who received iodine, only 5 developed goiter, while of 2300 untreated ones 495 developed the disease.

Foods rich in iodine, especially those growing near or in the sea, are also good preventives. Shrimp, crabs, lobster, smoked herring, fresh salmon, oysters, fresh cod, tuna fish, eel, agar-agar and especially kelp are some of the foods available for this purpose. 

Lately the use of iodized eggs has been highly recommended to increase the iodine content of the diet. These eggs are produced by feeding hens foods rich in iodine and supplying them with a drinking water which in reality is a weak solution of iodine. Analysis of these eggs in the chemical analytical laboratories in Bucharest, revealed that they contained about 600 times as much iodine as do ordinary eggs. 

It may be interesting to note that farm animals, such as cows, pigs, horses, sheep and chickens, raised in those inland regions, also suffer from goitre. Incorporation of iodine in their food will prevent the disease. 

(B) MISCELLANEOUS DISEASES 

(1) Acute Diarrhoea and Dysentery in Infants and Children An interesting dietary cure (this is said advisedly) is the treatment of dysentery and diarrhoea of babies and children by the "two day raw apple diet". The disease is especially prevalent in the hot, sultry summer months, often lasts many weeks and is the cause of a great deal of suffering and loss of weight and frequently results in death.

Up till lately the feeding of babies under two years of age who were suffering with this disease was especially difficult and required great care and discrimination. Often first one food then another was tried, only to find that the trouble became aggravated, with the result that both doctor and parents were greatly worried. All this has been changed and greatly simplified by the two day raw grated apple diet.

This consists in the administration of finely grated raw apples. The apples must be fairly ripe, peeled, cored, and grated on a fine glass sieve. From 3 to 6 or 10 ounces are given per meal, according to age. 

This is equal to a daily consumption of about 7-20 apples of medium size. No other food is allowed for two days, when a remarkable change in the character of the stool is usually seen. When this is ap-

DIETS 

151 parent, one may increase the diet by adding tea, water, cocoa, toast, bananas, cottage cheese, broth, strained barley soup, mashed potatoes, lean scraped beef, or even ham in older children. 

In case of relapse, the apple cure is started again. 

Even the worst diarrheas and dysenteries accompanied by frequent bloody and mucous stools and pain, cease within two days.

I have used this treatment in babies 11/2 years old and in children up to the age of 14, and even in grown people, and always with the most striking results. There was no failure. The only difficulty encountered was the dislike of some children for the large amount of grated apple offered. 

(2) FOOD ALLERGY AND FOOD POISONING ANAPHYLAXIS, FOOD IDIOSYNCRASY, FOOD HYPERSENSITIVENESS 

Amony the diseases, in which food acts as a cure the same as it does in the various deficiency diseases, are the food allergies. There are individuals who are hypersensitive to some foods and in whom these foods, while perfectly harmless to the great majority of people, act in some mysterious way as poisons. 

What it is that makes foods poisons to some is not yet determined beyond doubt. There is hardly any food which may not act as a poison, including our most commonly used foods, such as wheat, eggs, milk, strawberries, tomatoes, cabbage, orange juice, fish, shell fish, lobster, peaches, prunes, pork, and many others.

Even salt, fruit acids and sugar may act as poisons. A case is described of an itching skin disease of 10 years standing in a woman 59 years old, which was finally found to be caused by the ingestion of cooking salt and completely relieved by a salt free diet. 

Food allergy gives rise to a great variety of symptoms: Pain in abdomen with nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea, or even signs of appendicitis; hives and eczema; migrain headaches, profound shock and even epilepsy; perennial rhinitis and asthma; these and many others are some of the manifestations of food allergy.

These symptoms often disappear as suddenly as they come. I remember having seen a case of what was thought to be an appendicitis suddenly get well while the patient was in the anaesthetic room waiting to be operated on.

The diagnosis of allergy is generally easy in cases presenting such symptoms as hives and asthma; but with symptoms simulating appendicitis and other gastro-intestinal disturbances, the exact nature of the trouble is not always apparent, and many mistakes are undoubtedly made unless this possibility of food idiosyncrasy is kept in mind. 

Still more difficult is the indentification of the offending food. 

When you consider that any and all foods may be responsible, you can realize the difficulty of catching the real offender. This is made more trying by the fact that the patient is often sensitive, not only to one food, but to many foods. 

With the aid of skin tests or the so-called elimination diets, the responsible food can often be discovered. Frequently, too, the patient himself is aware of which foods agree with him and which do not. 

With the identification of the cause the treatment becomes at once simple and positive. The elimination of the offending food from the permanent diet is the surest method. How long such a food or foods have to be avoided differs in every case. Babies especially, and frequently adults, outgrow their idiosyncrasies after a period of time, sometimes weeks, months or years.

FOOD POISONING 

Related to food allergies are the various diseases caused by food poisons; the fact is that in the presence of such manifestations as abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhoea and frequently, too, hives and migraine, the question as to whether we are dealing with food poison or a food allergy is often difficult to decide. 

Food poisoning may be caused by: (1) Mushroom poisons, ptomaine poisons due to spoiled sausage, cheese, meat, fish, etc. (2) By germ contaminated foods, giving rise to a great variety of symptoms and diseases, such as:

DIETS 

153 

Typhoid and paratyphoid fever. 

Septic sore throat, undulant fever, tuberculosis, and others.

In recent years a disease called "Botulism" has attracted a good deal of attention. This is caused by the bacillus botulinus, which develops in improperly sterilized canned foods, and is especially common in home canning. 

Non-acid vegetables such as string beans, corn and spinach are the most liable to cause such poisoning. The symptoms are prostration, difficulty in swallowing, double vision, rapid heart action, respiratory paralysis and death. The best remedy is prevention by sterilizing these foods under pressure at a temperature of 240 degrees F. Home canning of the foods mentioned is dangerous. 

Trichinosis, caused by trichina infected pork or squirrels must also be included in this chapter. Its symptoms are loss of appetite, vomiting, cramps, diarrhoea, fever, headache, painful swelling of the muscles and pneumonia. The remedy is obvious. The avoidance of raw or poorly cooked meat of such animals will with certainly prevent the incidence of this disease. 

(3) CORYZA AND SINUS DISEASE 

Amony the diets which act as a cure must also be mentioned the 2 or 3 day thirst cure for coryza or common nasal cold. If combined with rigid salt restriction, practically any case of acute coryza will recover rapidly without leading to complications which are often serious, occasionally even fatal. This cure also prevents sinus trouble which often complicates colds and which has almost become a sort of obession with many people suffering from stubborn colds. No doubt every nasal cold affects the sinuses somewhat. Why not? There is no insurmountable barrier between the nose proper and the sinuses. 

But most of these mild cases quickly recover with the disappearance of the cold. A 2 or 3 day thirst cure, followed by a diet of milk, fruit, vegetables and potatoes, and a rigid restriction in salt intake will cure even apparently pronounced cases of sinus disease. Such a dietary management should be instituted before surgical intervention is decided upon.

(2) DISEASES IN THE TREATMENT OF WHICH DIET PLAYS THE MOST IMPORTANT PART (A) DIET AND THE DIGESTIVE TRACT 

Diseases of the digestive tract are among the most frequent disorders for which man seeks relief. It has been estimated that 25% of all patients suffer from digestive troubles, so that indigestion has often been called "The Great American Disease".

There are many reasons for this prevalence of gastro-intestinal diseases. Amongst the principal ones are badly balanced diets and an inadequate supply of the vitamins. 

In an address delivered before the Society of Biological Research, University of Pittsburgh, November 18, 1921, Robert McCarrison related many observations and many remarkable experiments which seemed to establish the fact that the chief cause of gastrointestinal disease was faulty food. By experimenting on animals with ill balanced and faulty diets, he was able to reproduce nearly all the gastro-intestinal diseases with which we meet in man. 

Some of these experiments were made on 36 wild monkeys, just recently captured. All were kept under the same roof and the same hydienic conditions; but 12 were fed on natural foods and the rest on foods either deficient or badly balanced. The result was that the former remained free from intestinal disorders whereas the majority of those fed on faulty diets suffered loss of appetite and weight, developed vomiting and diarrhoea, and often were taken sick with such organic diseases as dysentery, gastric and duodenal ulcers, atrophy of the intestines and colitis. These experiments forced on him the conclusion that the various dietary deficiencies, so common amongst civilized people, played an ever increasing role in the origination of many gastro-intestinal disorders.

Next to the deficiencies McCarrison accused badly balanced diets, especially excessive consumption of either carbohydrates or fats. 

On the other hand, when the diet was complete and well balanced, health and well-being was the usual result.

DIETS 

155 It means that if we could have full control of the peoples diet, we could prevent most gastro-intestinal disease and rid the civilized communities of one-fourth of their ailments.

Add to all these deficiencies in our diet faulty eating habits such as imperfect mastication, over eating, indulgence in alcoholic drinks and tobacco, and the inevitable result is disturbance of digestion, absorption and assimilation. 

The correction of these bad food habits and promotion of health and vigor amongst the great mass of our population can only be accomplished by bringing the message of the newer knowledge of nutrition to the people.

It is quite obvious, then, that in the treatment of gastro-intestinal disease diet plays the important role, more important by far than drugs or other measures.

As a preliminary to dietetic treatment it is neccesary to see to it that defective teeth, oral sepsis, diseased tonsils, and other conditions likely to disturb the appetite, prevent mastication and irritate and poison the stomach, are promptly attended to.

Like in diabetes, dietary directions in gastro-intestinal diseases must be explicit, especially as to quality; written instructions are better than oral advice; and it is well for the patient to know not only what not to eat, but also what to eat; and often, too, to be advised as to the composition of the various foods. Only by a knowledge of the different problems to be met can successful co-operation between him anr his physician be expected.

As digestion commences in the mouth, so have many cases of indigestion their origin there.

Dyspepsia, indigestion, gas formation, etc., are often caused by pyorrhoea or by deficient denture rendering mastication of food difficult. 

In every case of indigestion, be it gastric or intestinal, it is important that the teeth and mouth be examined by a competent dentist. This becomes especially imperative in older people. The constant presence and swallowing of bacteria laden secretions of the mouth is undoubtedly responsible for the frequent incidence of bad odor from the mouth as well as for acute and chronic gastro-intestinal indigestion.

The same is true of faulty or deficient dentures. Such artificial dentures are often wholly incapable of permitting proper mastication and frequently cannot be used at all. In such cases it becomes necessary to adopt the diet to the toothless mouth. Such a diet must be one which requires no mastication, consisting of milk, tea, coffee, cocoa, postum, soups, broths, fruit juices, pureed vegetables, strained cereal gruels, minced chicken, scraped beef balls, finely chopped fish (known amongst the orthodox Jews as "gefillte fish") soft boiled, poached or soft scrambled eggs, bread or toast soaked in milk or coffee; custards, puddings, ice cream, sherberts, cottage cheese, mashed potatoes, soft cheeses, butter, cream, buttermilk, jello, etc., a plentiful and varied supply indeed. 

Naturally, it goes without saying, that the premature loss of teeth due to caries should be prevented by those dietary measures which are dealt with in detail in the chapter on "Caries and Diet". 

In cases of canker sores and other inflammatory and ulcerated mouth conditions the diet, in addition to being smooth, must be free from irritants, must not be too hot, but rather lukewarm or cold, must not contain acids, either fruit acids or vinegar, nor any condiments, alcoholic drinks, tobacco or too much salt. 

(a) DISEASES OF THE STOMACH GASTRIC AND DUODENAL ULCER 

Of all diseases of the stomach, this is by far the most frequent one encountered and the most amenable to successful dietetic management. In fact, it is my opinion that nearly all cases of gastric ulcer can be cured by diet if their treatment is instituted early enough and if the medical management is persisted in for a long enough period of time and faithfully, conscientiously and intelligently carried out in all its details. Complications such as hemorrhage, perforation and obstruction would then be rare and operations be found unnecessary in nearly all cases.

The treatment of ulcer is comparatively simple. It consists: (1) In mental and physical rest.

DIETS 

157 (2) In a diet which is non-irritating; this includes the strict avoidance of alcohol and tobacco. (3) In continuing the treatment for a period of not weeks nor months, but of years, until the danger of recurrence seems remote.

The principles and demands of a correct dietary treatment are met by a number, if not all the various ulcer cures which have from time to time been proposed. Ulcer diets are pretty well standardized, Standardization, however, is often carried to extremes; to adhere to one formula with almost religious zeal without taking the patient's personality, individuality and constitution into account, his likes and dislikes, his habits and idosyncrasies-is bound to lead to failure. In ulcer, like an other disease, individualization is imperative. 

The tendency today is toward a more liberal diet almost from the start, making sure to incorporate in it all the essential requirements and factors, such as vitamins, sufficient calories and mineral substances, so as to avoid under-nutrition and deficiency diseases, which have often been the result of a too rigid adherence to one dietetic formula. All this can be done without sacrificing the principal demands of an ulcer diet.

No patient should be promised a cure unless he agrees to give up tobacco and alcohol. The former, especially, I regard as one of the most vicious, irritating and harmful habits in gastric ulcer and one which is sure to prevent its prompt and complete healing. In this no compromise is possible. The use of tobacco, even in the absence of ulcer, often produces ulcer symptoms, especially heart burn, and success in the treatment of ulcer often coincides with a cessation of smoking. The same is almost equally true of the use of alcoholic drinks. 

Aside from food the most important adjunct in the treatment of gastric ulcer is rest. In acute cases and in the beginning of treatment of all cases rest in bed from at least three to four weeks and longer, if there is a tendency to hemorrhage, has long been one of the recognized and most beneficial measures in all ulcer cures. It is in the ambulatory treatment of chronic cases that this principle of

rest has not had the recognition it deserves. I have practiced this rest treatment on myself and have seen such wonderful results in the relief of pain and in the initiation of orderly digestion that I have become the apostle of rest and have recommended it to all my ulcer patients and have repeatedly stressed it in medical discussions on the subject. A rest in the reclining position of one hour after each meal will materially help to relieve or prevent the symptoms of pain, heart burn, nausea, and vomiting and contribute to healing. That it is possible in nearly all cases to practice this rest treatment there is no doubt, provided the will to do so is there. Breakfast taken in bed, for instance, at 6 or 6:30 in the morning with an hours rest afterwards, will still enable the patient to reach his office or work by 8 or 8:30 o'clock.

A like rest of 12 to 1 hour, depending on the size of the meal, should and could be taken at the noon hour; making this meal the lightest of the day will make a half hour of rest sufficient. And surely one hour's rest after the evening meal is within the reach of everybody. 

Exercise, either active or passive in form of massage, has no place in the management of an ulcer patient; even the driving of an automobile is harmful and opposed to the principle of rest. 

Mental rest, too, is important. Worry, aggravation and anger, which often upset even the finest stomach, should be avoided in order to facilitate ultimate recovery.

A so-called Modified ulcer diet may be instituted in all cases of chronic ulcer right from the start, without subjecting the patient to the expense of a preliminary strict ulcer cure. Not all patients are economically so situated that they can afford a prolonged physical rest, which, in the absence of hemorrhage and other serious complications is not absolutely necessary; a modified ulcer cure gives satisfactory results, provided the principle of rest, as previously outlined, are observed.

Such a diet, which is identical with a bland and smooth diet, is, of course, constipating. But this constipation can be overcome either by the use of milk of magnesia or mineral oil, or both. 

The foods which may be safely used in such ambulatory treatment are:

DIETS 

159 (1) Milk (best boiled or pasteurized), cream, cottage cheese, sweet milk, milk toast, malted milk, egg malted milk, vanilla ice cream. (2) Soft boiled (2 minute) eggs or poached or soft scrambled eggs, custards. (3) Very crisp white bread toast, zwieback, melba toast, crackers. (All these must be thoroughly masticated.) (4) Cereals, well cooked and strained so as to be free from roughage and served with plenty of cream. (5) Plain puddings without raisins, made of tapioca, well boiled rice, farina or white bread. (6) Peas or beans or lentils in puree form; in form of pureed soups they make excellent and nourishing foods. (7) Asparagus tips, cauliflower tops, puree of spinach, mashed cooked carrots, perhaps even finely cut lettuce, eaten with olive oil dressing. (8) Tentatively; orange juice, prune juice, even lemonade are often a splendid addition to an ulcer diet, preventing scurvy. It is remarkable how well these fruit juices are tolerated by many ulcer patients; the fact that grapefruit juice agrees when orange juice does not shows the necessity of individualization in every case. (9) Weak tea. (10) Any tender, lean meat or fish or fowl; especially recommended are scraped beef balls, if properly prepared. They are delicious, satisfying and easy to digest; also white meat of chicken, breast of turkey, lean fish (black bass, brook trout, croppie); bluepoints in half shell, oil sardines.

It is important to mention that in every modified ulcer diet thorough mastication in addition to the proper preparation of the foods in the kitchen is indispensable to success. The tenderest and most unirritating food, if swallowed hastily and in large pieces, may prove irritating to a stomach, the seat of an ulcer, and thus defeat all efforts of curing the ulcer. It is here that Fletcherizing finds one of its most legitimate fields of usefulness.

If all these instructions as to diet, rest, exercise and length of time are followed, there is every reason to expect that the ulcer will be cured. I differ from those who state that the doctors are mostly to blame for the poor results generally achieved in the treatment of gastric ulcer. No doubt, many are blamable. But more often it is the patient who is most at fault. A patient with tuberculosis, when advised to go to a sanitarium and take a rest for from six months to one and one half years, readily consents. The publicity and propaganda given this subject makes the advice and its compliance a matter of course, and no long arguments are needed. But when it comes to using the same rest treatment in cases of gastric ulcer, it is hard to convince the patient that it is rest he needs most. If he consents to take a rest at all and goes to a hospital, the moment the pain ceases, which is frequently after five days, he gets impatient and wants to leave. And if put on a permanent diet and told to remain on this diet for a year and a half to two years, he seldom sticks to it longer than six weeks.

DIETS 

161 And yet, it takes from six months to two years of careful dieting to cure an ulcer.

Similar directions as to diet also apply in cases of catarrh of the stomach. 

(b) DIET IN INTESTINAL DISEASES 

Among the many disorders of the intestinal tract only those will be mentioned in this book which are of popular interest and are amenable to dietetic treatment. Chief amongst them are acute gastrointestinal upsets, colitis and appendicitis, diarrhea, constipation and flatulence.

The dietetic treatment of acute gastro-intestinal upsets, while comparatively simple, is often puzzling to the layman. It is for this reason that I give somewhat in detail the following procedure which to me has given the best results. (1) In severe cases, especially where vomiting is a symptom, start with complete starvation or, in the absence of vomiting, with a semi-starvation diet consisting of sweetened tea served without milk or cream, and of fat-free chicken or beef broth. (2) If after a day or two improvement is evident, add cereal decoctions or well strained cereal soups made of rice, barley or oatmeal, the so-called "Schleim suppe" of the Germans. These soups are very tasty, absolutely non-irritating and contain a very small amount of nourishment. (3) If with tea, coffee, toast, broths and "Schleim suppe", the symptoms do not become aggravated, add rice cooked for three hours sweetened with a little sugar and flavored with cinnamon; also water cocoa, cooked cream of wheat or oatmeal strained free from roughage and mixed with boiled skimmed milk. (4) After two days or more add scraped beef, white meat of chicken or turkey, rare, lean tender roast beef, rare tender beef steak, etc. (5) After two more days add soft-boiled or poached eggs, custards, plain puddings or rice or tapioca or farnia, mashed potatoes,

cottage cheese, asparagus tips, cauliflower tops, pureed vegetables, and, finally. (6) Go to the usual diet, including boiled milk; fruits and other roughage rich foods are the last to be added or best to be avoided for some time.

Such a "graduated diet" as I call it, insures best against the many relapses which are so common in this class of disorders. A familiarity with such a schedule is a great help and comfort to the patient who never knows what to eat next.

DIARRHOEA 

After the acute stage has passed and diarrhoea persists as the main symptom, the diet must be restricted to foods which are free from roughage and other irritating ingredients; in addition, the food must be well masticated or so prepared in the kitchen (pureed) that it does not irritate the intestinal canal mechanically. Experiments on animals have shown that the best basis for such a diet is lean tender meat. 

Add to this well-boiled strained oatmeal or rice, mashed and run through a sieve, soft boiled eggs, pudding without raisins, custard, cottage cheese, sugars, and probably a small quantity of fruit juices and strained pureed vegetables, tea, coffee, and broths or Schleim soups, all taken in small amounts and rather frequently repeated, and you have a diet which secures the highest degree of absorption, the smallest amount of residue and the least irritation to the inflammed bowel Even milk should be given cautiously, best skimmed, always boiled and only in small amounts; such milk sometimes turns the diarrhoea definitely to constipation.

Other additions to the diet may be made in the form of gelatin, junket, mashed or baked potatoes, macaroni and spaghetti, if well masticated, orange juice, grapefruit juice, grape juice, if they will not aggravate the diarrhoea or increase the irritability of bowels. Different patients react differently to fruit juices. Another delightful dish is made from the sweetened juice of stewed and strained blackberries or 

Logan berries thickened with custard.

DIETS 

163 An intelligent management of diarrhoea presupposes a knowledge of the cellulose or roughage content of the various foods. Foods high in cellulose content: (1) All raw fruits: apples, grapes, peaches, pears, plums, prunes, figs, dates, raisins. (2) All raw green vegetables: asparagus, brussel sprouts, cabbage, celery, cauliflower, endive, watercress, lettuce, tomatoes, onions, radishes, spinach, stringbeans, broccoli, pineapple. (3) All legumen foods: peas, beans, lentils, peanuts. (4) 

All bran foods: bran bread, bran muffins, rye bread, rolled oats, oatmeal, barley, corn, olives.

(1) Fruit juices, applesauce, stewed fruits. (2) Asparagus tips, cauliflower tops, puree of spinach and other puree'd vegetables. (3) Baked potatoes, toasted white bread, crackers, puree'd peas, beans or lentils, peanut butter, strained cereals. (4) Broths, beef juice, meat, fish, fowl. (5) Dairy foods: butter, cream, milk, cottage cheese and other cheeses, eggs, custard, junket. (6) Tea, coffee, cocoa, strained cereal soups (Schleim soup). (7) Honey, sugar, syrup, jellies. sugar added.

Such dietetic treatment fits any chronic, inflammatory intestinal disease such as colitis, dysentery, etc., in which diarrhoea, with or without pain, is the dominant symptom. Diarrhoea often leads to loss of weight, emaciation, weakness, nervous irritability as well as to dehydration and circulatory collapse. 

Among the other inflammatory intestinal troubles appendicitis is by far the most frequent and most feared. 

ACUTE APPENDICITIS 

It is, of course, not intended that the layman either diagnose or treat his own appendicitis. But the frequency of this disease and the fact that it is often overlooked and badly mismanaged and that many patients with acute attacks of appendicitis over-physic themselves and sacrifice their lives at the altar of ignorance and carelessness, make a few remarks apropos.

Whenever acute abdominal pain and tenderness develops and finally centers in the right lower abdomen and especially if fever is an accompanying symptom, call for your physician. In the meantime, observe the following rules: (1) Go immediately to bed. (2) Apply ice bag over the appendix region. (3) Do not under any circumstances use any cathartic nor other means to bring on a bowel movement. (4) Take no food or drink of any kind. (5) If diagnosis is established an operation at the earliest moment is advisable; this is especially urgent in children in whom perporation of the appendix develops very rapidly and early.

DIETS 

165 

INTESTINAL FLATULENCE-Flatulence is one of the com monest digestive complaints with which we meet in practice and is frequently the "bete noir" and "enfant terrible" of the physician and patient. It has been asserted that it is the third most frequent digestive disorder complained of. Flatulence is generally caused by either fermentation of excessive amount of carbohydrate food or by putrefaction of protein foods.

If fermentation is the cause, resulting in gas formation, distention and colicky pains with expulsion of odorless flatus, the use of cellulose rich and starchy foods ought to be greatly restricted and a protein diet substituted, consisting of eggs, meat, cheese, boiled milk, toast, with pureed vegetables and fruit juices, tea and broths. The very opposite type of a diet is called for in putrefactive type of flatulence, characterized by the formation of foul smelling stools and gases, which, when absorbed into the bloodstream give to the breath a foul odor, cause headache, malaise, drowsiness and other symptoms of auto-intoxication. 

Here the free use of fruit juices, sweets and starches and especially milksugar and buttermilk or better acidophilus milk with very limited amounts of eggs and meats is the diet to be followed. The daily use of about 3 ounces of milk sugar together with 1 pint (2 glasses) of acidophilus milk causes a rapid termination of the existing putrefaction.

NERVOUS DYSPEPSIA-This condition is due to a multiplicity of causes and presents a multiplicity of symptoms. We find it generally in nervous patients, and it is often aggravated by an over-dose of roughage in the diet, which causes fermentation, gas formation, discomfort and colicky pains.

When these patients look at their stools, a habit in which they invariably indulge, and see the many food remnants and mucus, they begin to worry and become convinced that they are in a grave condition. In consequence, they stop eating nearly altogether, become thin and weak, all of which confirms them in their belief that they are the victims of a serious disease.

The dietetic treatment is obvious; omit bran foods and foods rich in roughage from the diet; quit worrying over constipation and stop taking pills and all kinds of cathartics, and quit looking at your stools.

CONSTIPATION 

In this chapter we are discussing so-called habitual constipation, i.e., the kind which is not caused by obstruction or other organic difficulties.

Constipation is the most frequent intestinal complaint encountered. 

Amongst women it is so frequent that a definition of women as "a constipated animal" has been coined. Nor is it by any means rare amongst men.

What really constitutes constipation depends on a number of different factors. First and foremost amongst these factors is the kind of food a patient lives on. From this standpoint it can be truly said that what is constipation in one case is normal in another. On a milk diet, for instance, especially if the milk is free from germs (certified or sterilized or boiled), constipation, generally symptomless, is the rule. In such cases I have known the constipation to become so aggravated that the rectal contents had to be removed and dug out with the fingers. Likewise, a diet largely consisting of eggs, meat and cellulose-poor foods, with much tea, coffee, or cocoa, leads to constipation.

Fletcherizing, too, is not productive of many bowel movements; in fact, Fletcher states, and I can carroborate this statement, that while fletcherizing, the bowels need to move only once or twice a week. 

On the other hand, a diet in which fruits and green vegetables and other cellulose-rich foods abound, is not constipating. 

Next to faulty diet, the main causes of constipation are ignorance of the mechanism of defecation and a disregard of nature's efforts to establish it on a regular schedule. If the desire to go to stool is habitually overlooked or deliberately neglected, it grows weaker and weaker, and is finally not felt at all. It is an everyday experience that the call to go to stool if felt and for some reason not heeded, will not

DIETS 

167 be felt again the same day or day after. This sequence of events, frequently repeated, leads to so-called habitual constipation. 

Constipation is generally accompanied by a pathetic state of nervousness and apprehension, which causes these patients to center their whole attention on their bowels. They are in constant fear lest dire results follow. Such fears, are of course, unfounded.

From what has been said, the treatment of constipation is obvious. (1) Explain to the patient the nature, the causes and the mechanism of constipation. Make it plain to him that constipation is neither a crime for which he can be put in jail, nor a sin which shuts against him the gates of heaven, nor a calamity which should cause him to worry his head off and center his whole attention on his bowels to the exclusion of more important and more beautiful things in life. 

That constipation is not the terrible thing the minds of the patients imagine it to be, is proven by the fact that in years gone by when appendicitis was treated with opium, it was nothing unusual for a patient to have no bowel movements for two or three weeks, without leading to bad results.

And I know many patients whose bowels normally do not move oftener than once or twice a week, without causing the slightest trouble. Not everybody needs a daily bowel movement, and there is no reason to be unhappy and to worry over it and to resort to physics. Nor are such symptoms as headache and dizziness, etc., caused by constipation, at least not in normal people. It is the nervous type which worries and frets itself into a headache. (2) To re-educate the rectum and its sensitivity. This can only be done by an adequate diet and by making it a habit of going to stool two or three times a day, or oftener, until the desired result is obtained.

In this way, any ordinary constipation, even of many years' standing, can be cured in a few days. To this end it has been suggested that patients be hospitalized, that they be locked up in a room with bath, be given an adequate diet, and be asked

to use the toilet three to six times a day and to sit there for half an hour each time, if necessary, without, however, making the slightest effort at straining. An interesting book to while the time away is helpful. In this way, every case can be cured within a week. I have used this method with unfailing success in all cases where co-operation was possible. It is true that many patients get tired and careless and suffer relapses, but those who persist are rewarded by complete and permanent success. (3) Stop all drastic medication, especially pills, mineral waters, salts, etc. No regulation of bowels is possible while such drugs are being used. Regulation of bowels is only possible by regulation of faulty habits. (4) Give a diet adequate in amount. There is such a thing as a spurious constipation, seen especially in young anaemic shop girls and nervous women, who, due to neglect, loss of appetite, worry, etc., do not take a sufficient amount of food to excite a call for daily evacuation. (5) Give a correct diet, one which contains a minimum of tea, rice and meat, but instead sufficient fat and roughage to insure proper elimination. While a diet of common foods, such as fruits, vegetables and cereals will be satisfactory to correct constipation in most patients, it will not do so in all and requires the addition of some form of roughage. The use of commercial wheat bran or the processed bran products is very afficacious. One to two tablespoonfuls with each meal will generally prove satisfactory. This can be mixed with the cereal or taken with cream or milk.

DIET Recommended: 

(1) Cereals: bran cereals, oatmeal, shredded wheat, U. S. breakfast food. (2) Cooked and raw vegetables with the two principal meals: cabbage, celery, lettuce, slaw, peas, corn, carrots, spinach, tomatoes, asparagus, onions, brocolli, brussel sprouts, etc.

DIETS 

169 (3) Rye-bran-whole wheat or graham bread. (4) Vegetable soups. (5) Fruits with each meal: such as prunes, apples, pears, grapes, peaches, plums, cherries, figs, dates, pineapple, melons, oranges, strawberries, raspberries, currants, gooseberries, raisins. (6) As desserts: vegetable salads, salads, honey, preserved fruits. (7) Fats such as cream, butter, bacon, and oil dressings. (8) Beverages: buttermilk, fruit juices, cider. (9) Nuts. 

Avoid: Tea, coffee, cocoa, too much meat, rice, sugar, eggs, milk, candy, pastries, tapioca, potatoes, macaroni, cheese, huckleberries, alcoholic drinks and claret.

A special cure for constipation which is såid to be quite effective is the so-called "Sauer Kraut Kur" consisting of the use of sauerkraut three times a day before meals.

(B) METABOLIC DISEASES 

By metabolic diseases we understand those in which the normal utilization of foods is disturbed. (a) Diabetes Mellitus.

In normal people all starches and sugars after being changed into glucose are converted under the influence of insulin into glycogen which is either oxidized into carbonic acid or water to produce heat and energy or stored in the liver for emergency purposes. In diabetes this change of glucose into glycogen fails to materialize at all or only to a limited extent and as a consequence glucose accumulates in the blood and appears in the urine. All this is due to a failure of the pancreas to secrete the necessary amount of insulin. As contributary causes we may mention a hereditary disposition often found to run in families, over indulgence in starches and sweets, obesity and infection of the gall bladder. Likewise infection of the teeth, tonsils and adenoids may so injure the insulin secretion as to cause diabetes.

From a dietetic standpoint I distinguish three degrees of diabetes. 

The mild form in which a moderate reduction in starch and sugar intake alone will result in the disappearance of urinary and excess blood sugar-this is often called "alimentary glycosuria" and is due to over-consumption of food, especially carbohydrates and fats, and occurs principally in obese subjects; its treatment is almost identical with that of obesity. A severe form in which a more drastic reduction in carbohydrate intake is necessary to bring this about; and the severest type in which even this drastic dietary reduction does not succeed in rendering the urine sugar free or to reduce the increased blood sugar to normal.

In the beginning diabetes of adults is a relatively mild and innocent affliction, which exhibits few symptoms and is often discovered accidentally in the course of health or life insurance examination. But as the disease progresses and especially if it is neglected more serious symptoms arise. The body being unable to obtain its necessary energy from the carbohydrates, uses for its energy production the proteins of the food as well as of the tissues; this leads finally to those extreme

DIETS 

171 states of emaciation sometimes seen in rapidly progressing and uncontrolled diabetes. 

Of still greater importance is the disturbance brought about in the utilization of fats. In the absence of carbohydrate combustion the oxidation of fats is incomplete with the result that instead of carbonic acid and water they yield abnormal acids which accumulate in the blood and lead to acidosis and coma, that much dreaded terminal complication which in pre-insulin days spelled death. 

The diagnosis of diabetes is the function of the physician. In as much, however, as the dietetic treatment must be left in great part to the patient, it is important that he be sufficiently informed and educated regarding the nature of his trouble. There is in fact no disease in which the intelligent co-operation of the patient is so imperative as is diabetes. Such co-operation is only possible with knowlegde as the basis. 

Not only should he know how to examine his own urine for sugar; not only is it necessary that he be acquainted with the danger signals of coma and gangrene, but he should also know enough about foods, their composition and caloric value, to be able to vary his diet intelligently so as to avoid the monotony which is fatal to the success of dieting; besides, he must be instructed in the use of insulin, in the care of the syringe and the prevention of skin infection through the syringe, if it becomes necessary to supplement the diet with this remedy, and also in the symptoms of insulin shock and their quick relief. 

The examination of the urine for sugar is best made with the use of Benedict's solution, which the patient can buy in any drug store. A few test tubes, a test tube holder and an alcohol lamp complete the equipment. Add to about a teaspoonful of Benedict's solution 

8 drops of urine and bring to a boil. A change of the blue color of the Benedict's solution to a turbid green discoloration means a trace of sugar; a change to yellow means a fair amount and a change to red a large amount of sugar.

The symptoms of a fairly advanced diabetes are elimination of a large amount of urine of high specific gravity; excessive thirst; increased appetite with a craving for sweets and finally loss of weight.

Slow healing wounds or the appearance of boils also must direct attention to the possibility of an underlying diabetes. 

In addition such symptoms as intolerable itching around the female genitals with aczema and urinary infection make their appearance; later neuritic pains in the extremities with loss of sensation in hands and feet appear and finally failing vision and gangrene of lower extremeties. And the imperfect oxidation of the fats caused by a failure to utilize sugar results at last in acidosis, coma and death.

PREVENTION OF DIABETES is by no means a hopeless task. 

In those who are by heredity susceptible to diabetes as well as in others, preventive measures often are successful. These measures consist in: (1) Moderation in the total food intake and in avoidance of concentrated sweets and excess of starches and fats. (2) In the prevention of obesity by suitable diet and exercise. (3) In the removal of foci of infection such as bad teeth or tonsils. (4) In the removal of gallbladder trouble and gallstones which are apt to lead to infection of the pancreas.

How effective these various measures are only a mass experiment carried on over several generations can determine. 

TREATMENT OF DIABETES 

It is not intended that the following advice as to dietetic treatment shall in any way be construed as encouraging the layman to dispense with the services of a physician. Such construction could only end tragically. But in no disease does success depend so much on the whole hearted co-operation between physician and patient and a knowledge of the patient of the nature of his disease, of the whys and wherefores of certain measures and instructions as it does in diabetes. Above all it must be insisted that patients present themselves at the office of their physician for a blood sugar determination at least once a month, even though their own examination proves the urine to be sugar free. It is well known that a urine may be sugar free when the blood sugar is high, and this is especially true in older people. Under those circumstances a determination of the blood sugar

DIETS 

173 becomes necessary. Only in such a way can the success or failure of a given diet be established. If successful the blood sugar as well as the urine must be within the normal limits. 

In addition a correct diet must maintain the patient's strength and well being, and prevent and postpone as long as possible those dangerous complications which, like acidosis, coma, gangrene and angina pectoris, are usually the end picture of diabetes. 

Since no two cases are strictly alike, there ought to be as many different diets as there are diabetics. This is almost literally true. In no disease is generalization so impossible and individualization so imperative as in the dietetic treatment of diabetes. 

INSULIN 

Nor has the dietetic management of diabetes lost any of its importance since the discovery and introduction of insulin. Diet is still the supreme remedy, the sine qua non of the treatment of diabetes. 

When it is possible to give a diet which makes the urinary sugar disappear, renders the blood sugar normal and prevents the formation of acidosis, insulin is not necessary. The fact is that 75% of diabetic patients do not require insulin. It surely is not required in obese diabetics.

Insulin has one disadvantage. It has to be given hypodermically. 

When administered by mouth it is absolutely valueless. This requires the training of diabetics in the use of the hypodermic syringe. The use of insulin by the patient himself, however, calls for special precaution. It is known that an overdose in insulin will produce a serious symptom complex known as "insulin shock" consisting of nervousness, weakness, excessive hunger, rapid pulse, sweating, tremor (like after smoking a heavy cigar), feeling of faintness, double vision, mental confusion, delirium, convulsions, collapse, coma and death. 

Such symptoms immediately call for interference. Fortunately this is very simple and successful. The eating of candy or sugar or a glass or orange juice will quickly dissipate the symptoms. Recovery from insulin shock, even with a patient in profound coma, within a

few seconds after the administration of glucose is one of the dramatic évents in medicine.

The possibility of producing insulin shock, even with small doses of insulin, must always be kept in mind. Occasionally, too, an overdose of insulin is given through a mistake on the part of the nurse or patient. 

Insulin is best given half an hour before the principal carbohydrate meals and, if possible only twice a day; all the carbohydrates of the diet can be taken at those two meals. 

It is quite desirable that the patient or a member of his family give these injections. This saves many trips to or by the physician and renders the treatment more convenient and less expensive. This nessitates instruction of the patient in the care of the syringe, the needles, the skin, etc., so as to avoid infection.

The question of when to use insulin in diabetes may be answered in this way: 

Insulin is a necessity: (1) When the urine does not become sugar-free with any reasonable diet i.e., when the sugar tolerance is low. (2) In all children to enable them to use diets rich in carbohydrates and adequate for energy and growth. (3) In badly emaciated and under-nourished patients, when it is desired to increase their weight and efficiency. (4) In local infections or operative procedures and when the use of anaesthetics is contemplated. (5) When acidosis persists and coma threatens.

In obese patients, however, suffering from diabetes as the result of luxus consumption of carbohydrates and fats, insulin usually can be dispensed with.

As a general principle it can be stated that a diabetic should never be overweight; on the contrary a slight under-nutrition amounting to a 10% reduction from his normal weight, is best for the average patient. It is remarkable how easy it is to rid most patients of high urinary and blood sugar, if this principle of under-nutrition is adhered to. Under-nutrition, however, should not be carried to the point of

DIETS 

175 weakening the patient's physical and mental powers. If the low carbohydrate tolerance of the patient results in too great a degree of under-nutrition, incompatible with efficiency, then insulin must be given to permit larger carbohydrates consumption. 

If at all possible oatmeal, bananas, potatoes and especially bread should be included in the diet of every patient. This contributes to his contentment and happiness, a great factor in his successful management. As a matter of fact, and contrary to general belief and custom, a patient should be told that theoretically there are no forbidden foods. 

If he is allowed 80 grams of carbohydrate daily, it matters little where he gets them. As a result we no longer speak of forbidden foods, but we simply state their chemical composition, their carbohydrate, protein and fat content, and let the patient choose what he wants to eat, provided always that he remains within his allowance of carbohydrates, fats and proteins. Concentrated sweets, however, such as candy, sugar and syrups should be omitted since with their use the carbohydrate allowance is too quickly covered and large food intake made impossible.

Of equal importance to carbohydrate restriction is a limitation of fat consumption. This I regard as one of the most important dietary recommendations. It makes a larger consumption of starches and sugars possible and goes far to postpone hardening of the arteries, angina pectoris and gangrene.

Great emphasis has been laid in the past on accurately weighing food; in diabetes more than in any other disease in which dietary treatment plays an almost exclusive role, such accuracy was deemed absolutely necessary for the best results. But this is not strictly true. 

The weighing of foods does not make for such accuracy in the computation of the nutritive value of a food as to offset the trouble and the nuisance. Foods differ greatly in their composition both in different climes and seasons, and even from day to day. The composition of meat, for instance, varies considerably according to the amount of fat it contains; the sugar in oranges has been known to vary from 5.14 to 14.3%; the carbohydrate in the edible portion of carrots has a variation from 5.9 to 11.5%; beets vary from 3.2 to 14.2%; even

eggs vary in protein from 11.4 to 17.4,% and in fat content from 

10 to 15.1%. How, then, can weighing make for accuracy? Cooking, too, changes the chemical composition of foods. It removes much carbohydrate from vegetables and cereals and much fat from meats; how much, is never accurately determined. Raw spinach, for instance, contains 2.79 carbohydrates, whereas cooked spinach contains only 

0.85%. Nor is the process of digestion and absorption so uniform as to permit an accurate estimate of what is actually utilized. In the last analysis, it is the utilization and not the chemical composition of foods which ultimately determines their caloric value. 

This does not mean that a knowledge of the approximate composition of the principal foods and the determination of the approximate amount used in the diet is not a good thing. The use of scales and the weighing of foods, in the beginning at least, is desirable, until the patient has learned to estimate and approximate by eye sight the weight of the different portions. Household measures and natural food units should be made use of as much as possible; they are easily understood and are better guides to go by and require less effort and less time.

In some clinics the quantities of food allowed are for purposes of educating the diabetic visualized with the aid of paper moulds or wooden blocks. Thus, one slice of bread weighing 30 grams is represented by a block of wood about 31½ inches square and 1/8 inch thick. Samples of these moulds can be purchased in the market. 

In addition to watching his food, a patient must also watch his weight, being careful not to gain or lose an undue amount. As long as a patient keeps his minimum weight and feels strong and able to work, his diet is adequate. For a simple method of determining proper height and weight relationships Rabinowich divides individuals into 

4 age groups, i.e., 15-25, 26-30, 31-35, and upward.

Assuming all to be 5 feet tall the corresponding weight of the 

4 groups are 120, 125, 130, and 135 lbs. Allow 3 pounds for each inch above 5 feet in case of males and subtract 5 pounds from the final result in case of females. For instance, male, 33, 5'6", normal weight 130 plus (6x3)-148 lbs.

DIETS 

177 

Female, age 38, height 5'4", normal weight 135 plus (4x3)— 

147 minus 5-142 lbs.

The patient must also keep track of his urinary sugar by making a daily or twice daily analysis of the urine. If at any time sugar is found he can then immediately, with events fresh in his mind, discover the cause and correct it.

Above all, every patient must visit his doctor at least once a month for a blood sugar determination and a checking up of his condition. 

Worry, fright, fits of anger, do harm and must be avoided; they often undo in one day the good done by weeks of dieting. 

To me it seems that extreme starch rich diets such as is the tendency today to use have to be avoided and that the wisdom of diets containing 250-350 grams of carbohydrates, except in children, is very questionable, and this in spite of the statement made that this high carbohydrate diet has come to stay. Such a diet makes it necessary that the patient follows instructions to the letter and co-operates in every possible way.

It is very doubtful, indeed, if that control over patients which is necessary under these circumstances can be exercised outside of a hospital. I have rarely been able to enlist that full co-operation on the part of patients, while engaged in their various pursuits, which is so important and indispensable in the management of their trouble. 

Like in many other things, medical and non-medical, it seems wise to steer a middle course. Even a carbohydrate content of 75 grams daily permits the physician to prescribe and the patient to partake of such foods as cereals and bread which make the diet seem more normal and make for greater contentment and happiness of the patient. Such an allowance of carbohydrate, for instance, permits the use of 2 pieces of bread daily, one ounce each, and of a daily cereal, such as 1/2 ounce of dry oatmeal allowing an additional ounce of carbohydrate which can be taken in the form of 300 grams of 3 to 5% vegetables or their equivalents, and 200 grams of 10% fruit.

A great aid and benefit to the patient is the introduction of

"special diet days", such as are being recommended in the treatment of obesity. 

SPECIAL DIETS IN DIABETES 

(1) Hunger days: to be carried out for 24 to 36 hours, while patient is resting in bed. The diet during these days to consist of coffee, tea, broth, lemon juice, brandy. (2) Vegetable days: also combined with rest in bed, consisting of 

3 to 5 meals of green vegetables. (3) Fruit days: on which 2 to 3 pounds of either apples or strawberries or sour cherries may be consumed, or instead 10 to 12 bananas a day. (4) Milk days (Karell cure): consisting of 11/2 pints whole milk given per day in 4 meals. Instead of whole milk 1 quart of skimmed milk or 2 quarts of buttermilk may be given.

The special diets are generally reserved for more severe forms of diabetes. The insertion, once or twice a week, of either a fruit day or a vegetable day or a milk day to take the place of the regular diet rests the pancreas and has a tendency to raise the carbohydrate tolerance.

SAMPLE VEGETABLE DAY 

Breakfast: 1 cup of tea or coffee.

Lunch: 

1 slice of toast. 

1 cup fat-free broth. 

1/4 pound cooked green vegetable. 

1/4 pound raw green vegetables or salad. 

Afternoon: 1 cup of tea or boullion. 

Evening: 1 cup of broth (fat-free). 

1/4 pound cooked green vegetables. 

1/4 pound raw green vegetables or salad.

There are two serious complications frequently arising in the course of severe diabetes which demand special attention and often immediate emergency treatment, i.e., acidosis and coma. The danger of the latter should always be suspected when a diabetic patient complains

DIETS 

179 of headaches, nausea, vomiting, disturbances of vision and mental confusion. These symptoms frequently usher in drowsiness, air hunger and coma. In as much, however, as its treatment constitutes an emergency, is too technical and can only be carried out successfully in a hospital, it is deemed best to omit details as to diet and medication. The sooner a patient who exhibits such symptoms gets under the influence of insulin, the greater his chances of recovery. 

Statistics show that insulin, given in large doses, saves 100% of all patients, if treatment is started within 6 hours of onset of symptoms. This is a strong argument in favor of consulting a physician as soon as any of above named symptoms develop in the course of diabetes.

In as much as diabetes is a serious disease it is deemed best to omit a detailed list of various diets. Such diets have to be prescribed by a physician who after a thorough examination of the urine, the blood and the other organs knows just exactly the patients capacity to take care of the various foodstuffs. 

It is only when we are dealing with a fat diabetic whose sickness is caused by over consumption of starches and sweets and fats that a laymen can attempt to formulate his own diet. Such a diet is almost identical with an obesity diet and consists in great reduction of his starch, sugar and fat intake. Very rarely, indeed, does the use of insulin become necessary in these cases. 

Sample Diabetic-Obesity Diet

A cup of skimmed milk.

Diabetes in children in preinsulin days was a rather hopeless disease. The rapid growth and great expenditure of energy so characteristically displayed in childhood made adequate nutrition practically impossible. All this has been changed by the discovery of insulin; and the possibility of saving the lives of these young patients has been increased a thousand fold. "To witness the remarkable phenomenon of an emaciated boy, dying of diabetic coma, being trans- Formed through the use of insulin into a healthy, well nourished lad within a period of three months is quite thrilling".

As far as their dietetic treatment is concerned it must be stated that a high CH diet i.e. 8 to 12 ounces daily to supply the necessary energy and a sufficient amount of complete proteins for growth is eminently indicated. Such a high CH diet, of course is only possible if insulin is used simultaneously. When insulin is used, however, mothers must be given to understand that neither more or less food should be given than the diet list calls for. Too little food might easily bring on a dangerous insulin shock and too much food will undo all the good work of months of careful dieting. 

If on this diet children get too fat, restrict the fat consumption rather than the carbohydrate intake. Always give at least 1 pint to 1 quart of milk daily; the calcium of the milk is required for the bony growth and for the teeth. The younger the child, the more milk. The child also needs raw fruits and green leafy vegetables to supply the necessary vitamins and mineral substances. Vegetables ought to be cooked under steam pressure so as not to lose the greater part of their mineral and vitamin content. In this respect canned vegetables are better than home cooked vegetables.
