As far as the use of insulin is concerned, this is a necessity in at least 50% of all children suffering from diabetes. But it must always be remembered that it is easier to produce insulin shock in children than in adults, and that the need for insulin is less on days when the child indulges in severe muscular exercise and in the summer. 

As to the dose of insulin required, that must be left strictly to the calculation of the physician. 

(b) OBESITY 

When the weight of an individual is 25% higher than the standard for height, age and sex we call it obesity.

The causes of this excessive fat accumulation are not yet fully understood. Neither the supposition of a constitutional or familial tendency, nor a disturbed function of the glands of internal secretion, nor excessive food intake, nor insufficient exercise fully explain the phenomenon of obesity. The fact is that many patients with so-called constitutional or glandular obesity fail to lose weight in spite of the administration of glandular extracts and that others with so-called dietary obesity fail to respond to restricted food intake. Everybody, with any experience in the treatment of obese patients knows that some people grow fat in spite of a low caloric diet and that even with

a considerable reduction in food intake it is often impossible to bring about a marked reduction in weight. 

For some unknown reason obese patients require less food for maintenance than normal people do, and every ounce of food above those requirements is changed into and stored as fat. Indulgence between meals and before retiring, in the form of midnight lunches, are often responsible for large increase in fat depots and weight. An addition, for instance, to the daily tea or coffee of a comparatively negligible quantity of sugar, about 1/2 ounce or 2 teaspoonfuls may mean the formation of 7 grams of fat or an increase of 2.5 kilograms (5 pounds) in a year, and of 25 kilograms (50 pounds) in 10 years.

It must be admitted, however, that a perfectly normal person does not increase in weight with such slight excess in intake. This shows that a normal person is able to burn the slight excess, but that the obese is not.

In the last analysis all obesity is "simple obesity" which means that it is due to an excess in food intake as compared with the output and that most people become fat because their output does not keep step with their intake.

The simplest form of obesity is, like the simple forms of diabetes, due purely and simply to excessive use of carbohydrates and fats. 

Being uncomplicated by any constitutional tendency or any glandular disturbance, it is easily and quickly reduced to normal by simply cutting out sweets and fats and reducing the carbohydrate intake to one-half or less of the accustomed figure. Nor need we concern ourselves with weighing the food nor even with salt and water restriction, as in this form of obesity this factor plays none or at most a very minor part. 

The glandular type is caused either by thyroid or testicular or ovarian or pituitary insufficiency. The obesity following menopause and after castration, are instances of the glandular type. The last two named are harder to manage because they neither yield to dietary restriction nor, strange to say, to specific glandular treatment. The kind caused by thyroid deficiency seems to be the only one amenable to specific glandular treatment.

DIETS 

185 Strictly speaking, obesity, especially the food kind, is not a disease. 

Yet every fat individual has difficulties such as shortness of breath, palpitation, cardiac weakness or exertion, and others. But it is especially the frequent association of obesity with diabetes, gallstones, gout, organic heart disease, hardening of the arteries, high blood pressure, its tendency to increase the risk of surgical operations, and the fact that the mortality in the obese subject is considerably higher than in those of normal weight, which makes obesity a serious if not a dangerous disorder. Life insurance statistics show that at the age of 

40 to 44 an excess of 20% in weight means a 30 to 40% decrease in life expectancy normal for that age, and a 40% excess involves an 80 to 100% increase in mortality.

The object of any obesity cure is to stop luxus consumption, to compel the patient to live on his own stored fat, to get his system permanently used to a lower food intake and to bring the weight down to normal figures, taking into consideration his age, sex, occupation and state of health. To estimate the ideal weight of a patient add 5½ pounds for each additional inch in height above 5 ft., at which height the proper weight is 110 pounds. Thus, for a patient measuring 5'6" 

6 times 512, plus 110-143 pounds would be his ideal weight.

Frequently with the lowering of weight there is a reduction in high blood pressure, which we often find associated with obesity. A low calorie diet is the largest single factor in the control of this type of hypertension.

A reduction in weight is best brought about by dietary treatment. We cannot alter the constitutional tendency toward obesity except in as far as it is caused by thyroid deficiency, but we can curtail the excessive food intake and increase the patient's activities and overcome to some extent any existing disposition to gain weight. 

The principle of such a diet is to reduce the caloric intake. 

This must be brought about at the expense of the carbohydrates and fats and not of the proteins. As far as possible, all fats should be excluded from the diet, and the amount of carbohydrates should reduced to about 1/4 a gram for each pound of body weight. On such a diet any loss in weight is at the expense of the stored body fat and

not of vital tissue. At least 1/2 gram of protein for each pound of the patient's weight must be incorporated in the diet. 

Taking these figures as a basis for a dietary prescription; a man weighing 180 pounds, would require the following: 

1/2 gram of protein per pound = 90 grams X 4 = 360 calories 

1/4 gram of carbohydrate per = 45 grams X 4 = 180 calories 

1/20 gram of fat per pound 9 grams X 9= 81 calories 

This is, of course, plain under-nutrition or semi-starvation, and it requires some will power on the part of the patient to submit to it and continue with it until the desired reduction in weight is obtained. 

Without the cheerful and determined co-operation of the patient obesity cures are impossible. The fact is, that most attempts to reduce weight by excessive dietary restrictions suffer shipwreck on the rocks of the great hunger and weakness which overcome these patients. 

To relieve this hunger and weakness I recommend eating 1 or 

2 raw tomatoes or apples between meals.

Even on such a greatly restricted diet it takes more than a week to bring about a reduction of 2 pounds or 8 pounds per month. 

Reduction at a faster rate than 1 or 2 pounds a week is undesirable.

The patient loses strength, his skin does not have time to adjust itself to the loss of the underlying fat, hangs in folds and gives a haggard appearance which frightens patients and friends alike with the result that the cure is prematurely interrupted. Frequently greater loss of weight is not due to loss of fat, but loss of water. 

Reduction cures are not advisable in children unless the obesity is excessive; nor in tuberculosis, because experience teaches that loss of weight is unfavorable in tuberculosis and that gain in weight marks a turn for the better. The same is true of people who suffer from great nervousness, irritability and insomnia.

There are many obesity cures; all are based, however, on the same principle. There is no magic obesity cure without dietary restriction; all patent medicines advertised and guaranteed to cure obesity,

DIETS 

187 take great pains in calling attention to "the enclosed printed dietary advice". 

Experience, however, teaches that some obese patients fail to lose weight in spite of considerable restriction in food intake and that others lose more weight than one could reasonably expect from diet alone. These seemingly contradictory facts are due to water retention on one side or water loss on the other. The tissues of obese patients have a tendency to retain water and it is this water retention which explains an otherwise unexplained gain in weight. Such periods of water retention and gain in weight alternate with periods of excessive water elimination, and since these variations often amount to 6 or 8 pounds daily, and since the actual daily loss of fat as a result of strict dieting can hardly be more than 1/4 of a pound, it proves that water intake has 30 to 40 times greater influence on daily weight than the limitation of food consumption. Water retention in obesity calls for water and salt restriction and for a liberal protein intake in the diet.

The following, then, are the principles on which every obesity diet is constructed: (1) The caloric intake should be reduced to between 1000 and 600 calories a day or even less in exceptional cases. (2) The carbohydrate intake should not be more than 1/4 of a gram per pound of body weight 50 grams. (3) The protein requirement is from 1/2 to 3/4 grams per kilogram 

75 to 125 cc. (4) Adequate amounts of the various vitamins and mineral substances should be provided for. This is attended to by giving these patients plenty of green vegetables and fruits and, if necessary 

Vitamin B and D concentrates. (5) The fat allowance should be practically nil, and should seldom exceed 10 to 20 grams a day, i.e., 1/20 gram per pound of body weight. (6) A restriction of water intake to 1 or 1½ pints daily and of salt to 1 gram per day is often necessary.

(7) Bulk to appease the appetite and insure proper elimination must be provided.

Indulgence in alcoholic drinks is usually inadvisable. Two pints of beer f.i. (equal to 4 glasses) produce 550 calories. One pint of wine, i.e. about 2 glasses produce 350 calories. Three ounces of brandy produce 350 calories.

Remembering that the total caloric intake calculated to bring about weight reduction is seldom higher than 700 calories it can easily be seen that such alcoholic consumption would leave very little room for actual food.

As an aid to diet exercise and cold baths or cold showers is usually advisable. But while exercise f.i. undoubtedly increases our energy output, it is a two edged-sword; it leads to an increase in appetite with a corresponding increase in food consumption to more than make up for the loss of fat and weight it causes. 

The use of thyroid preparations in connection with obesity cures can be generally dispensed with, especially with the newer diets. 

Thyroid is not an indifferent drug. Many patients after taking it for a few weeks complain of nervousness, insomnia, tremor of hands and palpitation of the heart which demand an interruption of the thyroid medication. 

A new drug, dinitrophenol, has lately been experimented with in the treatment of obesity. While it promises interesting possibilities, such as weight reduction without strict attention to diet, its exact place in the treatment of this disease has not yet been definitely established. Reports to date indicate that the use of this drug is not free from harmful and dangerous possibilities and should never be used except on advice and under strict supervision of a physician. This caution is equally pertinent regarding the use of thyroid. I never prescribe such drugs, unless patients promise to present themselves at least once a week at the office for examination.

An analysis of this 18 day cure, shows that it does not differ materially from our modern obesity diets and that it owes its virtue to no mysterious or unknown quality. Neither can a loss of more than 

5 or 6 pounds a month be expected with this cure: and a continuation of it beyond the 18 days is necessary to effect further reduction in weight.

Nor is there anything mysterious about the banana and skimmed milk diet recently advertised. It consists in taking daily four glasses of skimmed milk and one pound of bananas, furnishing about 850 calories.

This diet offers no advantages and its monotony makes it much inferior to the various dietary samples given above. 

The insertion of one or two raw fruit or raw vegetable days or a Karell day every week increases loss of weight.

Nuts, olives and peanuts have to be omitted from such a raw food diet.

To be able to add the necessary variations to the menus, patients ought to know the equivalents of other foods which can be substituted without increasing the caloric value of the diet.

30 grams of farina or rice.

150 grams of milk.

A cure recently published in one of our daily papers consists in the daily fluid intake of 14 or more glasses, i.e., 7 pints in form of fruit or vegetable juice, milk or buttermilk and water. Such a diet may be all right as long as the kidneys eliminate the excess fluid; when this fails to materialize, the weight will not only not decrease but may actually increase as the result of abnormal water retention. 

This is especially liable to occur in cases where dropsy due to heart or kidney trouble complicates the obesity. In such cases, a Karell cure as described in the chapter on "Dietetic Cures" meets the indications far better. 

(c) UNDERNUTRITION, MALNUTRITION, LEANNESS 

When the weight of an individual is 15% or more below the standard for his age, height and sex, it constitutes undernutrition. 

This is often caused by insufficient food intake due to fasting and starvation or is the result of a number of acute and chronic diseases which result in loss of weight which is often enormous and frequently amounts to 40 and 50% of the original weight. Such a loss is not only serious, but dangerous.

There is another form of undernutrition or leanness (and it is this form with which we are concerned here) which is constitutional

DIETS 

199 and is a disease per se. The condition affects mostly women but not exclusively so. These individuals are born that way; they constitute the lean and lanky type; many resemble the tubercular type, though very few of them ever become tubercular.

They have a long narrow chest and poor musculature, no mental or physical stamina; they are afflicted with falling of one or all of the abdominal organs; even their heart drops an inch, and they suffer from symptoms of malassimilation, painful digestion often simulating gastric ulcer or even appendicitis.

They complain of palpitation and pain in the chest, and suffer from a myriad of nervous symptoms, fears, phobias, worries, headaches and insomia, all being aggravated by work. Besides, they have absolutely no appetite nor desire for food. These individuals run in a so-called vicious circle; the lack of appetite leads to insufficient food intake, under-weight, fatigue, weakness, irritability, loss of sleep, anaemia, frequent colds and other infections, and these, in their turn aggravate the original condition. Unless this vicious circle be broken, these patients are doomed to a life of invalidism and finally become nervous wrecks.

As to treatment, it can be asserted that diet constitutes our chief remedy; this diet must be of such caloric value that the intake exceeds the output of energy. To accomplish this we have two supreme remedies which will bring about a great improvement in these patients. 

True, this improvement as a rule only lasts for a limited time, often only for few months, seldom for years. These patients are like clocks; they run down periodically and have to be wound up again. The two measures which accomplish this are rest and over nutrition as exemplified by the celebrated Weir Mitchell cure. This cure consists mainly in long enforced bed rest, taken in a hospital away from home, friends, relatives, husband and children. This rest cure is designed to prevent undue energy loss and is kept up from 4 to 6 weeks or longer, during which time patients are not permitted to leave the bed, have a daily massage to take the place of exercise, receive attention to bowels, get complete mental relaxation and above all a diet of from 3,000 to 5,000 calories daily.

On this regime, if the co-operation of the patient can be had, there is often a gain of from 30 to 40 pounds within 6 weeks with a corresponding improvement or disappearance of all the symptoms.

Patients who are not able to take this treatment can be given a straight milk cure. The daily use of 6 quarts of milk or 3 quarts of milk and 1 quart of cream which yields over 4000 calories in 24 hours can be depended upon to bring about the desired increase in weight. Taken in quantities of 6 ounces (1 tumblerful) every half hour between 7 a.m. and 7 p.m., it causes an increase in weight of two and a half pounds a week.

A recent cure and one which is often very successful is the socalled "Insulin-Mast" (fattening) Cure. But in as much as this has to be given in a hospital and under the physician's daily supervision a detailed account of it is here omitted. 

(d) GOUT 

Gout is a disease characterized by an increase of uric acid in the blood and its deposition in the tissues and joints, causing arthritis. 

The exact cause of this disturbance is not known. Often it appears in families and the tendency to it is inherited. Acute gout generally commences in the form of arthritis of the first joint of the big toe; swelling of the joint with extreme tenderness and pain, and fever are the outstanding symptoms.

Patients suffering from gout are generally over-weight, indulge too much in food and too little in exercise, and partake too freely of alcoholic drinks. The disease is much more common in Europe, especially in England and Germany where beer drinking is a national sport.

That over-indulgence in uric acid-containing foods alone is not responsible for the disease is proven by the fact that amongst the

Greenland Eskimos, who live largely on meat and liver, gout is almost unknown. 

To prevent gout it is best to keep the body weight 10 to 15% below normal, to lead a more active life and to abstain from all alcoholic drinks and not to indulge too much in animal foods. 

The treatment consists in the use of foods which are uric acid free, so far as this is possible. 

In the acute stage of gout a starvation diet, either complete or partial, is the best. A large intake of alkaline water and the use of fruit juices are the best foods used in this stage. 

In the chronic forms the use of uric-acid-free food together with a moderate total caloric intake is the best dietetic treatment. 

It is especially in gout that a so-called raw food diet is eminently indicated and effective. The same can be said of a lacto-vegetarian diet. The insertion of one or two fruit and vegetable days and one 

Karell milk day a week, increases the loss of weight and hastens the rapidity with which the disease is conquered. 

To prescribe a correct diet a knowledge of the uric acid content of the various foods is necessary. 

Forbidden are: 

Meats, meat broths, soups, gravies, sweet breads, liver, kidney, spleen, brains, (gland organs), fowl, game, fish, oysters, lobster, shrimp, sardines and anchovies. Coffee, tea, cocoa and alcohol. 

Lentils, peas, beans, peanuts, yeast. All these foods are rich in uric acid. Sparingly: 

Fats, oatmeal, spinach, asparagus, mushrooms and cauliflower. Allowed: 

Milk, cream, butter, cheese, buttermilk, ice cream, eggs, custards, junkets, tapioca and rice puddings, cereals, white bread, corn bread, rye bread, most vegetables, especially carrots, tomatoes, pickles, cucumbers, onions, beets, turnips, garlic; fruits, strawberries, peaches, grapes, pears, cherries, bananas, etc., potatoes, sugar, plenty of water, noodles, macaroni, spaghetti, shad roe, caviar, peanut butter, prunes.

3. DISEASES IN WHICH DIET PLAYS AN IMPORTANT, THOUGH NOT THE MOST IMPORTANT PART 

Under this heading a number of diseases are listed which, while undoubtedly very important from a dietetic point of view, demand besides diet other forms of treatment and the management of which is involved in so many technical difficulties and discussions that a detailed account of even their dietetic treatment in a book intended for laymen is not deemed advisable. A few remarks, however, will be made which ought to contribute to a better understanding of the problems involved and be of aid to those who happen to be interested in these diseases. 

(a) DIET AND KIDNEY DISEASE 

Physicians have long realized that the changes which take place in the kidney and other organs as a result of disease cannot be altered either by drugs or diet. At the same time we have learned that frequently we can favorably influence the function of these organs. To accomplish this we rely chiefly on correct dietetic treatment. 

From this point of view it matters little what form of kidney trouble we are dealing with. It is the functional capacity of the kidneys which counts and which determines the kind of diet which is to be prescribed. If the kidneys are unable to eliminate protein waste products, so that they accumulate in the blood and cause high blood pressure and symptoms or uraemia, the protein consumption will have to be restricted, and, at times, even stopped altogether. If, on the other hand, there is inability of the kidneys to eliminate water and salt so that dropsy results, the intake of salt and water will have to be curtailed no matter what form of Bright's disease we are treating. 

The result is that today the dietetic treatment of diseases of the kidney is not only very rational but simple, as well. 

So far as carboyhdrates and fats are concerned they can be used in any reasonable quantity under all conditions and at all times in the

DIETS 

205 diets of kidney patients. Since they are converted into carbonic acid and water which are easily eliminated they do not ordinarily burden the kidney. A liberal consumption of starches and sweets rather than fats, meets most indications. 

The use of spices and condiments, especially mustard and pepper, should be avoided at all times and in all cases. 

The moderate use of alcohol is as a rule harmless. The propaganda that alcohol has a destructive effect on the kidneys is not justified. 

Alcoholic subjects generally develop chronic gastritis and often hardening of the liver, but not kidney trouble, and their arteries are often remarkably free from signs of hardening. This coincides with my experience; I have seen many whiskey drinkers but few with either high blood pressure or kidney disease.

The same is true of the use of protein foods. As long as the kidneys function well there is no reason why the consumption of meat or fish or fowl or eggs should be greatly restricted. On the contrary in all chronic cases with kidney function intact or nearly so, a sufficient intake of protein is necessary to maintain the tissues in a state of good nutrition. It is well known that these patients often are unaware of their illness and feel unusually well. In consequence they do not consult a physician and in that way they frequently escape being dieted to death. Too great a protein restriction in such cases not only causes weakness and anaemia but is one of the causes of dropsy. There is no use nor sense in denying these patients the use of meat or other protein foods, always provided that the kidney function is fair. This, of course, can only be determined by an examination of the blood by the physician.

A diet consisting largely of alkaline producing foods (i.e., consisting of milk, fruits and vegetables) with meat, fish or fowl or eggs once a day meets the indications in Bright's disease and high blood pressure best.

(b) NERVOUS DISEASES 

At first glance, diseases of the nervous system do not seem to offer a very promising field for dietotherapy. Naturally, most organic diseases of the nervous system are uninfluenced by diet treatment, except in an indirect way.

But the role which food plays in such nervous disorders as palpitation of the heart, fast heart and insomnia is common knowledge. 

The necessity of abstaining from the use of tea or coffee or other stimulants in these cases is admitted by all. In the summer it is especially the use of large amounts of iced tea which is the frequent cause of many insomnias encountered.

DIETS 

207 Another condition of the nervous system which is favorably influenced by dietetic treatment is: 

NERVOUS PROSTRATION 

(Nervous Breakdown)

There is a doubt in the minds of many ultrascientific physicians, if there is such a entity as "Nervous Breakdown". It is true that the dividing line between it and the graver psychoses is a very narrow one. Never the less, many lay people and physicians share with me the conviction that the disease exists. 

It is generally caused by overwork combined with worry. Work alone, either mental or physical, does not bring on a nervous breakdown.

Frequently, too, there is a family, i.e., herditary predisposition towards this trouble. This especially evident in those ill nourished, emaciated, lean and lanky psycho-neurotics, whose improvement and welfare depends absolutely and solely on increased food intake. 

Practically all of them are women who are constitutionally inferior and are sufferers from various degrees of dropping of abdominal organs and from a bewildering multiplicity of complaints. The number of scars on their abdomens and their health history prove the many attempts that have been made now by one surgeon, then by another to relieve them of their suffering and disabilities. First an appendectomy, next a gallbladder operation, then a fixation of the kidneys or colon or uterus have been done, but all to no avail. True, while in bed in the hospital they feel better, but when up and around they soon return to their former state of ill health. They continue to be thin, under-nourished with no appetite to get enough food down, and if they do force it down they suffer from gas formation, palpitation of heart and pains. They are running in a vicious circle. The less food, the weaker and more nervous, and this in return affects the appetite. 

To break this vicious circle means recovery. What these patients need is not a surgeon to cut out their pains, (such pains can never be removed with the knife), but a physician whose experience and human

sympathies and understanding are the best guarantee for help and possible cure.

Most of these patients suffer from an almost insuperable repugnance to food; unless this can be overcome and the food intake be increased, improvement is out of the question. 

To accomplish this we have a number of dietary measures at our command. Chief amongst them is the old, well known Weir Mitchell

Fattening Cure. The details of this will be found in the chapter of "Malnutrition". 

Other dietary schemes like the "Insulin-Glucose Cure" f.i. are too technical and require the close attention of a physician, so that a discussion of them in this book is deemed inadvisable. 

There are other nervous diseases which are favorably influenced by diet. The treatment of polyneuritis (Beri-Beri) f.i. and other forms of neuritis by Vitamin B or by diets directed against the underlying cause (gout, diabetes, alcohol) is too well known to require more than scant mention at this time. (c) 

DISEASES OF THE LIVER 

What has been said about the kidney diseases is equally true about diseases of the liver.

The liver is the largest gland of the body. Its immense size has long suggested its great importance in the animal economy; but it is only in recent years that its various functions and its great influence on digestion and metabolism have become better understood and recognized. The liver today is known to be the "Grand Central Station” of metabolism of all foodstuffs. Its vital importance is shown by the fact that extirpation of the liver in animals results in death within three hours, unless postponed by injections of glucose.

Its external secretion, the bile, is a necessary product; without bile death takes place sooner or later, and can only be postponed by a large consumption of carbohydrates.

The liver also is the great detoxifying organ of the body. Poisons, carried into the liver by the circulation are there detoxified and made harmless.

DIETS 

209 Naturally diseases of the liver seriously affect one or all of these various functions and cause grave disturbances of digestion, absorption and utilization of the various foodstuffs. 

In as much, however, as both the diagnosis and treatment of liver trouble requires the expert services of a physician, detailed account of even the dietary treatment is here omitted. 

(d) TUBERCULOSIS 

Among diseases of the lungs tuberculosis (generally called consumption) has always been the one in which diet was stressed as an important part of the treatment. are: 

The three cardinal principles of a successful cure for tuberculosis (1) Rest. This is absolutely necessary if there is a rise in temperature. 

Such a rest is to be continued until the temperature becomes normal and remains so even after slight exercise. Any return of the temperature calls immediately for resumption of the rest cure. (2) Fresh air and sunshine. This can be used along side with the rest by having the patient lie in the open, on a sleeping porch or in a tent. In the winter when sunshine is not available, the daily use of the ultraviolet rays by means of the quartz lamp is the best substitute. For the same reason a change of climate to a place where open air treatment and sunshine can be had the year round is advisable. Any climate which furnishes an abundance of sunshine, dry, pure air and few sudden changes is suitable. (3) Food in the form of a well balanced, high caloric diet, supplying 3500 to 5000 calories, from 120 to 150 grams of protein, 500 grams of carbohydrate and from 100 to 150 grams of fat, with the necessary vitamins and mineral substances in the form of fruits, green vegetables, milk, cheese and eggs. The fat is best given in the form of butter fat or cream and cod liver oil. The latter, while not exactly the cure in tuberculosis it was once thought to be, furnishes in addition to the fat an abundance

of Vitamins A and D; it is the Vitamin D which, in tuberculosis, plays an important part. It aids in the calcification and cure of tubercular foci, reduces fever, stops night sweats and increases weight. These beneficial effects become apparent after six months of treatment. 

Of late years a new dietetic treatment of tuberculosis, known by the combined name of Gerson-Sauerbruch-Hermanadorfer diet has been introduced. Originally advocated by Dr. Gerson of Germany, it was tested and scientifically established by Professor Hermansdorffer under the direction of Professor Sauerbruch of the University of Munich (now 

Professor of Surgery in Berlin).

Dr. Gerson's attention was called to this diet by the often striking results obtained by some nature doctors in the treatment of various diseases and also by the relief from migraine he himself experienced, after having been a sufferer from it since early life. As a result he extended this dietetic cure to the treatment of various forms of tuberculosis and achieved often miraculous cures in the treatment of skin tuberculosis (lupus), tubercular joints, bones and glands, in cases which for months and years had resisted all other forms of treatment. 

The superiority of this dietetic treatment in these special forms of tuberculosis is now generally conceded. But the value and superiority of the Gerson diet over the old high calorie diet in the treatment of tuberculosis of the lungs is still denied by some. 

The two chief features of the Gerson diet are a high Vitamin content and rigid salt restriction.

Which one of these two principles, singly or combined, is responsible for the good results obtained is hard to decide. Gerson himself, in his original diet, did not lay as much stress on vitamins as he did on salt restriction. Large quantities of fruit juices and vegetable juices, 1 pound of raw fruit daily, with salads, 3 raw yolks of egg and a tablespoonful of cod liver oil, constitutes the diet. 

In actual practice, of course, it is necessary to individualize and be guided by the digestive powers and idiosyncrasies of the patient. 

The diet is rather expensive, requires a good cook, and on account of the restricted use of salt, is difficult to prepare and hard to render

DIETS 

211 tasty; it requires skill, practical experience and a sympathetic attitude towards and an enthusiasm for the work. 

Hermanskorffer accomplished all this in his own hospital with the aid of his wife, who supervised the preparation of the special diets, so as to make them acceptable to the patients. Improvement in their condition took place only after weeks, often months of faithful adherence to this dietary treatment. But it is not sufficient in these cases to prepare a good meal and put it before the patient and leave it to him what of it all he wants to eat. The nurse must insist and see to it that the patient eats what is put before him. This takes time and patience. Of great help is it to make sure that all meals are daintily served so as to stimulate the appetite. Loss of appetite in these cases is often due to the disagreeable taste which the purulent sputum produces and may be corrected by the use of cleansing and deodorizing mouth washes and throat gargles, as well as by a little wine taken before meals. This lack of appetite, amounting sometimes to an almost unconquerable aversion to food, especially in cases where suppurating fitulas complicate the picture, often threatens to defeat all efforts. 

Again, mental depression and irritability must be counteracted by suggestive treatment and sympathetic and encouraging words. Here is where the right nurse, inspired by a desire to help, can be of great service.

To compensate for the lack of salt in the food, fennel, thymian, and other spices are extensively used to make the food tasty. These must be crushed to bring out their taste and flavor. The use of nutmeg in the preparation of vegetables, salads and meats; the use of chives for all foods; of parsley for soups, potatoes and fish; wine for red and white cabbage; of lemon juice for goulash and swiss steak, and the liberal use of home made, salt-free tomato catsup, these are the many substitutes used for salt. 

Milk soups are made with cream, eggs and tomatoes.

Meat is prepared rare or roasted and flavored with garlic and served with horseradish sauce (made from scraped horseradish, sour cream, oil, vinegar, or lemon juice), or Italian parsley sauce (made

with chopped parsley, little oil, yolk of egg, lemon juice, pepper, mustard and sour cream).

Vegetables for the very sick patient are to be served in puree form and always steamed in butter. The use of vegetable juices made of spinach, tomatoes, carrots and lettuce and the eating of a half to three-quarters of a pound of raw fruit and 100 grams of raw vegetables daily is recommended.

While these minute instructions belong in a cook book rather than in a book on dietetics, their incorporation here is intended to show the importance and necessity of these details to make the food palatable and the diet a success.

In addition to the diet and cod liver oil, Gerson uses a mineral mixture called "Mineralogen" the composition of which while not accurately known, includes mainly calcium.

(e) DIET AND FEVER 

The dietetic treatment of fever has of late years undergone a radical change. Fever patients generally show a greatly diminished appetite which results in a greatly reduced food intake and in a great

DIETS 

213 loss of weight and strength. In short fevers which do not last more than a few days or at most a week this is of little consequence. But in fevers of longer duration, such as typhoid fever or tuberculosis, such under nutrition often assumes serious proportions and grave aspects. This did not fail to attract the attention of physicians and friends with the result that the old adage, "feed a cold and starve a fever" was finally changed into the slogan, "starve a cold and feed a fever". 

It can safely he said that the new dietetic treatment of typhoid fever, based on this principle of adequate nutrition, has been productive of remarkable results. 

While long fevers demand adequate nutrition to prevent loss of weight, in short fevers of but a few days duration a total starvation or semi-starvation diet does no harm and is at times necessary. This is especially the case if the fever is caused by an acute intestinal upset, intestinal toxaemia or appendicitis, or where such symptoms as nausea and vomiting are a prominent feature. 

In most short fevers water, tea and coffee, and fruit juices may be taken freely and food be entirely dispensed with for several days without detriment to the patient. As soon as the patient is able to eat, such articles as fruit juices, stewed fruits, applesauce, baked apples, scraped raw apple, skimmed milk, whey, butter milk, cooked cereals and vegetable soups, toasted bread, milk toast, sherbets, soft boiled and poached eggs, custards, puddings, gelatin foods, veal broth, soup made of calves' feet, glucose candy sticks, etc., may be given. One or two of the above mentioned articles may be taken every three hours together with liberal quantities of cold water. In some cases the use of ice pellets is indicated, especially when vomiting and nausea prevent the taking of water or other foods. If these ice pellets are swallowed whole they will often settle an irritable stomach and stop the vomiting. 

Vomiting is such a frequent symptom in the beginning of many fevers, that its proper management is a matter of importance. 

In those accustomed to the habitual use of alcohol, this should not be withheld during a fever. It is remarkable how much alcohol a fever patient can consume without showing any signs of intoxication.

In fever, at least, alcohol acts as a food; and as a vehicle for admistering milk in form of milk punches or eggnogs it is invaluable. Such keen clinicians as the late Dr. Abraham Jocobi, the dean of American pediatricians, claimed alcohol to be our best antidote for blood poisoning. Besides, it allays restlessness and promotes sleep. 

In Scarlet Fever the diet must be liquid or soft, and non-irritating. 

On account of the danger of acute Bright's Disease, which usually makes its appearance after the second week, not much change in diet ought to be made even after the fever has left. If four weeks after the onset of the fever, no Bright's Disease has set in, this danger is usually past. 

During all this period the use of alcohol, spices and condiments, too much salt and too much food, must be avoided on general principles, even though food bears no direct relationship to this complication and can at most be only a contributing factor. 

A scarlet fever diet then should consist of milk, fruit juices, butter milk, vegetable junices, koumiss, junket, ice cream, sherbets, cooked fruits; later, cereals and cream, toast, plain puddings, custards, soft boiled eggs. After four weeks the regular diet may be resumed. 

(f) DIET IN SURGERY 

The importance of diet in surgery is daily being recognized to a larger degree. But in spite of this there is as yet little team work and co-operation between the surgeon and physician for the benefit of their mutual patients. It is certainly important in most cases of major surgery, both before and after the operation, that the diet be regulated; this insures for the surgical patient numerous hitherto unused possibilities and advantages and at times even proves life-saving. 

Why any patient wants to go to the hospital for a major operation necessitating an anaesthetic without a previous thorough examination by his own physician, and why any surgeon should undertake such an operation and its after-treatment without consulting the family physician, can only be explained by the ignorance of the patient and the superiority complex of some surgeons.

There are a few outstanding medical disorders which frequently

DIETS 

215 affect the success of operations and ought, therefore, be of great interest to the patient and the surgeon.

First amongst them is obesity which has often been termed "the enemy of the surgeon". Obesity interferes with proper healing of wounds and increases the liability of infection. This is especially true in hernia operations, in surgery of the abdomen, but also in most other serious operations. 

Another important disease from a surgical standpoint in diabetes. 

Here the main dangers arising from operations are: (1) Acidosis and coma, following the anaesthetic. (2) Delayed healing of wound. (3) Increased danger of infection.

Diabetes must always be thought of when a wound is indolent and shows slight tendency to heal. A sugar-free urine does not always mean absence of diabetes. It is well to know that after the fifth decade of life, it is not rare to observe diabetic patients with a very high blood sugar but with no sugar in the urine. Whereas normal persons show glycosuria with a blood sugar of 170 to 180, diabetics often show none with a blood sugar of 250.

If this condition is not recognized and treated and the cause removed, the wound does not heal, becomes infected, and the patient may die. On the other hand, the favorable change following the reduction of blood sugar is often marvelous.

Why under these circumstances any patient should allow himself to be operated without the presence and advice of his family physician, is hard to understand.

No less important is the post-operative dietetic treatment of patients in surgery of the gastrointestinal tract. In as much, however, as the patients themselves never have a voice in the selection of their diet under such circumstances, details are here omitted. 

(g) DIET IN HEART DISEASE AND CIRCULATORY DISORDERS 

The stomach and heart are in such close proximity anatomically and even connected by a common nerve supply, that the trouble in

one organ easily causes a disturbance in the other. Thus it is an every day experience that palpitation, irregular heart action, shortness of breath, are often the result of gastric and intestinal indigestion and that gas formation, belching and loss of appetite are frequent symptoms and often the first ones of heart disease.

For these reasons the diet in heart disease is a very necessary part of its treatment. The foods must be strictly digestible and nongas forming; the meals must be small and rather more frequent, with rest after each meal as a véry important adjunct; and in fat people with heart disease, a careful obesity cure frequently gives great relief. 

Diet in heart disease scores its greatest triumphs in dropsy. These are the cases in which digitalis frequently fails. When, as is often seen, there is general dropsy, congestion of lungs, enlargement of the liver, all resulting in severe shortness of breath, compelling the patient to assume the upright position to be able to breathe at all, and making it impossible for him to remain in bed, and forcing him to spend the days and nights in a chair to be able to get a little rest and sleep; when the urinary output is greatly diminished, the appetite completely gone, and the end seems but a matter of a few days, it is then that diet steps in and helps where digitalis alone often fails. 

The recovery of these patients under such conditions is often nothing less than miraculous. And the miracle is performed by the Karell 

Milk cure. 

This consists in the exclusive use of a milk diet, supplemented or not by fruit juices. By giving the patient a cup of milk 4 times a day, with a few tablespoonfuls of orange juice in between to keep the lips, mouth and throat moist and quench the thirst, you are giving a diet which is poor in calories (from 340 to 544 per day), poor in proteins (from 20 to 30 grams a day), poor in water (590 to 830 cc a day) and poor in salt. On such a diet the output of urine increases immensely, with the result that within comparatively short time, barely more than a week or ten days, the whole picture changes. The dropsy diminishes and finally disappears, the size of the liver grows smaller; the congestion of the lungs clears up and the patient is able to go to bed and sleep in the reclining position, thus getting the

DIETS 

217 much needed physical and mental rest; the shortness of breath disappears or at least greatly improves; the heart action returns to nearly normal, and the patient again feels that life is worth living. 

After the patient is well on the way towards recovery, the diet is gradually and carefully enlarged, always keeping in mind the necessity of not distressing the heart or overburdening the stomach. 

(h) HIGH BLOOD PRESSURE 

Intimately connected with the subject of heart disease is that of high blood pressure. The popularity of this subject warrants a few remarks, if for no other reason than to correct a few misconceptions lingering in the peoples minds.

How much dietary factors contribute to the cause or relief of high blood pressure is more or less speculative. Certain it is that a familial disposition is often strikingly in evidence, the disease if we can call it such often running through many members of the same family and through many generations. 

From a dietetic standpoint it is extremely doubtful if any diet used can materially influence this trouble. The avoidance of excess consumption of animal protein foods and fats as well as of undue worry, excitement, anger and other consuming emotions, together with an abundance of physical rest, both by day and night, is, no doubt, conducive to prolong life. Such patients should live largely on milk, vegetables and fruits, called a "lacto-vegetarian" diet, but they should not be asked to forego every joy of life until it can be definitely proven that these joys do great harm.

As long as the kidney function is fairly intact, there is no use to omit meat or to practice a greatly restricted salt intake. 

The habit of resting after each meal, a very effective remedy indeed, need not interfere with the usual activities. But no man with excessive chronic high blood pressure has any business to play eighteen holes of golf or practice handball during his noon recess hour; nor has he any right to drive his own automobile through our busy streets and over our highways, if not for his own sake, at least for the protection of others. What these patients need is rest, and not exercise.

And what they should cultivate is the habit of a periodic health examination at least twice a year to get correct advice and instructions as to diet and exercise. If the same propaganda by periodicals, radio broadcasts, periodic examinations, etc., would be used for the preservation of the health and lives of people between 45 and 60 which is now so successfully practiced in the case of babies and children with the result that the average span of life has been extended from 35 to 55, the frightful and ever increasing mortality amongst people of between 45 and 60 could be greatly reduced.

The use and especially the abuse of alcohol and tobacco while not necessarily responsible for the existing condition, nevertheless, frequently aggravates it. It has been determined that 1 pint of 3% beer (2 glasses) or 5 ounces of wine (1 glass) or one ounce of whiskey, bring about a temporary rise in blood pressure of about 20 points. This insult, frequently repeated, can lead to permanent changes. 

The same is true of the use of tea and coffee; their connection with hypertension is very questionable: yet the acceleration of the heart action and the over-stimulation of the nervous system caused by them is not conductive to bring about that circulatory rest which these subjects need.

In hypertension with obesity a careful but persistent reduction cure with the avoidance of sweets and fats and reduction of starches in favor of an abundance of vegetables and fruits, with moderate quantities of milk, is often very successful. In fact it is asserted that a balanced reducing diet, intelligently supervised, is the largest single factor in the control of this type of high blood pressure.

(i) DIET AND SKIN DISEASES 

It has always been a popular belief that food plays an important role in diseases of the skin. 

This relationship between food and skin is very apparent in the so-called allergic skin diseases, such as hives, some forms of infantile eczema and a few others. The incidence of hives, for instance, following with clock-like precision the ingestion of strawberries in many, of eggs and other foods in others, is an everyday experience. Such incidence and coincidence leave no doubt as to the role diet plays in the causation of skin lesions.

The diagnosis, i.e., the indentification of the particular food responsible in a given case is at times difficult. The so-called skin tests often fail completely to throw light on this problem. I am treating now a case of persistent, violent, chronic hives, in a girl 12 years old, in which all food tests proved negative; yet an improvement on the one hand, or marked aggravation on the other, was quite noticeable after the ingestion of certain foods. In such cases we are dependent for diagnosis on the history of the skin eruption and the experience of the patient who is frequently able to incriminate a certain food. 

The foods most often responsible for allergic reactions are in order named: cow's milk, eggs, wheat, shellfish, strawberries, nuts, pork, buckwheat, tomatoes, high carbohydrates diets, alcohol, tobacco, tea, coffee, spices, concentrated sweets, and even salt, fruit acids and sugars.

The simplest and best treatment for these food idosyncrasies consist in the elimination of the offending food from the diet; this, of

DIETS 

221 course, is only possible, if the particular offender is known. When this is not the case there are other methods of treatment, which are often as successful, as they are simple. in 

Helpful, for instance, is a daily dose of castor oil. Effective also my hands has been the recommendation of giving 1 to 2 teaspoonful of mineral oil in the midst of each meal in stubborn cases of hives.

All these are household remedies and are never harmful and require no expert advice.

Other dietary causes of skin diseases are excess of salt and water intake; this is especially the case in certain forms of eczema, especially the moist, weeping variety, and in tuberculosis of the skin, called lupus. All call for a salt and water poor diet.

Nor must the role which vitamin deficiencies play in skin diseases be overlooked. Hemorrhages in the skin caused by lack of Vitamin 

C, and the specific skin disease seen in pellagra, are well known examples and a further proof of the role which diet plays in the cause and cure of diseases of the skin.

Other skin diseases are due to metabolic disturbances, which change the chemical composition and reaction of the skin. We know, for instance, that diabetes may cause eczema, hives, boils and other skin diseases, all yielding rapidly to a diet directed against the existing metabolic disorder. 

Whether food is a direct cause of acne and pimples is very doubtful; that it is a contributing factor is more likely. The fact that acne is especially prevalent in boys and girls during the time of puberty when the function of the skin is very active, as evidenced by the growth of hair where it did not grow before, proves that there is more than a dietary cause involved. Nevertheless, experience teaches that while diet does not cure, it is apt to aggravate the trouble if badly chosen. 

The diet best in acne consists of: (1) Restriction of carbohydrate intake; especially to be avoided are concentrated sweets, such as candy, syrups, pastry, ice cream, as they are known to increase the function of the sebaceous glands. (2) Especially harmful are pork and beans and strong cheeses.

(3) The restriction of fatty and fried foods. (4) The avoidance of iodized salt and bromides. (5) The avoidance of spices and alcoholic drinks.

The best diet is one composed of vegetables, fruits, lean meats or fish or fowl, toast, cereals and some simple deserts such as jello, sherbet and fruit salad.

All that has been said about skin diseases shows that their successful treatment depends on a full knowledge of their cause, based on complete examination of the patient, including blood chemistry, urinalysis, history of dietary habits, of constitutional tendencies, etc. 

Such examination and the advice based on it is essentially the function of the physician.

PART IV DIETETIC "CURES"

DIETETIC CURES 

By dietetic "cures" (and in this connection the word derived from the Latin "curare" does not mean "cure" in the English sense but has its original meaning, i.e., "treatment") we understand the methodical and exclusive use of certain dietetic measures and foods for therapeutic purposes. I am referring especially to butter milk cures, whey cures, grape cures, hunger cures, thirst cures and a few others.

These cures have been and are still quite the fad in Europe, especially in Germany. In this country they are little known and much neglected.

One of the most popular of these cures is 

(1) The Grape Cure 

Grape cures are always taken in the fall at the time when grapes are ripe and being harvested. Along the Rhine a number of resorts are visited every year by those whose belief in the efficacy of these grape cures makes them their favorite vacation place. No doubt, the beauty and attractiveness of these resorts, to which both nature and art contribute their share, the outdoor life, the freedom from business worries and house cares and drudgery, pleasant companionships, beautiful walks, neighborhood excursions, with music offered morning, noon and night,—all contribute to make these cures both enjoyable and profitable from the standpoint of health.

Overweight men and women, who spend their days in offices and homes, with little exercise save stepping in and out of their automobiles; who gorge themselves day after day on the luxuries of the table, ending with cigarettes and cigars and alcohol; who know not the satisfaction of a natural and free bowel movement; whose daily worries, trials and tribulations add to their mental and physical discomfort; who literally puff and smoke like an engine with faulty draughts; who on the slightest exertion are short of breath; whose urine perhaps contains a trace of albumin or sugar; whose blood pres- 225

sure shows a dangerous tendency to rise above normal; whose only real enjoyment is a sumptuous meal, a glass of wine, a good cigar, and a sleep after dinner, those are the ones who are greatly benefited, yea, literally rejuvenated by a sojourn in those places and the grape cure taken for a period of from 3 to 6 weeks. True, these benefits are seldom permanent. A return to their faulty habits of living will make a yearly repetition of these pilgrimage a necessary. 

The composition of grape juice and pulp, is:

Water 

Sugar 76-84% 10-33%

Protein 1-2% Fats

None 

Mineral Substances 

0.2-04% 

Cellulose and vitamins.

The main characteristic of this composition is its high sugar, low protein, low salt and high water content, and its lack of uric acid. 

In other words, we have here a salt-poor, protein-poor, calorie-poor, uric acid free diet, furnishing an abundance of vitamins, water and mineral substances.

The amount of grapes consumed daily varies from 2 pounds the first day to 6 to 12 pounds thereafter. After about 2 weeks on this exclusive grape diet, other foods, such as buttermilk, potatoes, vegetables, perhaps even a little meat once a day, are added, in which case the grapes are taken before meals, thus stilling the most acute appetite and hunger with grapes and making a large food intake as unnecessary as undesirable.

The indications for a grape cure are obesity, gout, kidney and heart disease, liver trouble, constipation, etc. On account of the cellulose content of grapes, however, the cure is inadvisable whenever we are dealing with a subject suffering from intestinal irritability such as diarrhoea, colitis, chronic appendicitis. In these cases grape juice may be substituted for grapes.

What is true of grape cures is equally true of other fruit cures, i.e., apple cures, orange cures and strawberry cures.

DIETETIC CURES 

227 is— More strenuous than the grape cure, but meeting similar indications 

(2) The Whey Cure 

Whey is the watery part of the milk from which it is separated by the action of rennet. It is prepared by adding to 32 ounces of milk (1 quart) heated to 104 degrees F. 2 teaspoonfuls of rennet and setting it aside until it clots. The clot is then broken up and the whole is strained through muslin. The resulting milk water, or milk serum, or whey has the following composition:

Water 

Protein 

Lactose (Milk-sugar) 93.264% 1.080% 5.100% 0.116% 0.410% Fat 

Salts (especially Calcium)

It is evident that aside from milk-sugar, water a small amount of protein and mineral matter, the nutritive value of this whey is almost nil. 

The cure is especially intended for people whose complaints are caused by habitual inordinate consumption of food, especially nitrogenous foods. By stimulating the function of the organs of elimination it rids the system of retained and accumulated waste products. Rheumatism and gout, abdominal plethora with diminished function of the kidneys and bowels, jaundice and certain forms of dyspepsia are some of its more important indications. 

The daily amount to be taken is from 1 to 5 pints or more, commencing with a tumblerful night and morning and gradually increasing this amount to 10 tumblerfuls a day. For the first two weeks nothing else is to be eaten. Afterwards other foods such as fruits, vegetables, bread, potatoes and cereals are gradually and carefully added. 

The cure is generally taken in the summer months in forest or mountain resorts where the climate and the beautiful surroundings add to the effectiveness of the treatment. Such places are Interlachen, Switzerland, with an altitude of 568 meters, Reichenhall, Bavaria, with an altitude of 459 meters, and Moran, Tirol, with an altitude of 28 meters, and others.

(3) Buttermilk Cure 

One of the best known cures is the Buttermilk cure. A few years ago it was so popular that it amounted to almost a "fad". Its popularity was largely the result of the propaganda made in its favor by the celebrated French bacteriologist, then the head of the Pasteur Institute in Paris, Professor Metchnikoff. He noticed that there were more centennarians living in Bulgaria and amongst the peoples of the Bulkan States than anywhere else in the world, and he ascribed this fact to the national habit of drinking Buttermilk. This fermented milk, he argued, owed its virtue to the lactic acid bacillus, also called Bacillus Bulgaricus, which changed the sugar in the milk to lactic acid, carbonic acid and water. And it was this lactic acid and this bacillus which opposed and prevented putrefaction in the colon and with it the formation of putrefactive poisons which, when absorbed into the bloodstream, produced chronic fatigue, headaches, and a number of conditions such as high blood pressure and Bright's disease, all resulting in premature old age and a shortened span of life. 

Ever since Metchnikoff has published these observations and researches the world has been drinking buttermilk, hoping thereby to prolong youth and defer old age. 

At least 3 glasses of buttermilk or better still acidophilus milk daily are necessary to limit the growth of putrefactive bacteria in the colon.

The beneficial effect of the use of fermented milk, especially acidophilus milk fortified by milk sugar has been proved by animal experiments. Monkeys after having been on a meat and egg diet for 

2 weeks became sleepy, stupid, manifested little interest, took their food very deliberately and passed little urine and what they passed contained products of putrefaction. On a milk and sugar diet a great change in their psychical and physical attitude took place. The animals became alert and bright, showed keen interest in their surroundings, had a good appetite, bright eyes and they no longer chewed the woodwork of their cages after a meal. 

Naturally it is very doubtful if this buttermilk cure will prolong our life and perpetuate our youth. This quest for youth,—as old as

DIETETIC CURES 

229 it is, is still a mere delusion and nothing else. The technique of perpetuating youth is not as simple as that. Neither the drinking of acidophilus milk, nor Fletcherizing, nor vegetarianism or its more extreme form, the raw food fad, can long postpone the inevitable or offset the results of unfavorable heredity and reckless wear and tear. 

It is quite likely that the influence of certain endocrine glands is at least as potent as that of diet. Personally I believe that the dream of 

Brown-Sequard who sought to prolong youth with his "Elixir of Life" will some day come true and that with the advent of another "Banting" and an improved method of manufacture of the true endocrine secretions, ovarian and orchitic preparations will be forth coming which will replace that which is lost and will rekindle the spirit of youth in prematurely aged men and women. Like Brunhilde asleep and surrounded by a wall of fire waiting for Siegfried's kiss to awaken her, so does the prematurely aged man impatiently await the coming of the discoverer of the fountain of youth.
