(4) Hunger Cures 

Hunger cures have long been advertised as a cure all for every imaginable ill. C. E. Wunderlich, a Leipzig physician of fame in 1845, wrote "With food restriction and rest nearly all curable diseases can be cured." And a Bern, Switzerland, physiologist wrote in 1844 in his book on Physiology: "Food restriction in the hands of a physician is one of the most important remedies at his command"; and in our

own day hunger cures, partial or complete, enjoy a great reputation and a widespread use in a variety of ailments. 

Restriction and even total abstinence from food is not nearly as painful and as serious as in total deprivation of fluid intake. It has been said that a strong man can do without oxygen for a few minutes, without water for a few days, but without food for many weeks. 

Professional hunger virtuosos have gone without food for 40 days or more, with a loss of but 15 to 20% of their body weight and without any apparent great harm to the individual. Succi, for instance, fasted several times for 30 days. Dr. Tanner, an American

Physician, for 40 days, and Merlatti of Paris for 50 days. During these fasts Succi took laudanum and Merlatti water to stay the hunger pains in the stomach.

One of the longest fasts on record is that of Kumagawas' dog which died on the 98th day after a loss of 65% in weight. And a yet longer fast has been reported in which a dog fasted from February 

6 to June 2 i.e., 117 days.

I myself have gone without food and drink for five days and have in years gone by, in cases of appendicitis under my care, absolutely starved my patients for from 9 to 13 days not only without injury but with apparent great benefit. Some almost miraculous cures of seemingly hopeless cases of appendicitis have been the result of such absolute starvation.

Experience during the World War has taught that under a long continued restricted food intake such diseases as diabetes, gout, obesity and others improved and gradually decreased in number. 

Complete fasting is beneficial in a number of affections such as appendicitis, peritonitis, bleeding gastric ulcers, dangerously high blood pressure with headaches and other symptoms of impending apoplexy, migraine, uraemia, hives, vomiting, nausea and uncontrollable continuous epileptic attacks.

Modified hunger cures in the form of fruit and vegetable days are of great benefit in the management of gout, obesity and diabetes.

DIETETIC CURES 

231 It cannot be emphasized too much and too often, that the treatment of all these diseases by hunger cures or otherwise is strictly the function of the physician.

More strenuous by far than hunger cures are (5) Thirst Cures

It will be remembered that our body needs a daily intake of about 212 quarts of water to replace that which is lost through the kidneys, skin, lungs and bowels. A loss of water, with resulting dehydration, occurs frequently in the course of a number of diseases, and frequently assumes a serious aspect. With the loss of 20% of body fluids, death ensues within 10 or 12 days or less.

A lack of water intake is frequently observed in nursing babies, especially during the hot summer months. Such babies become restless, lose their appetites, become somnolent, their mucous membranes and the skin become dry, the pulse small and fast, and fever often develops as high as 103 degrees F. Such a fever is called "dehydration fever" and is quickly checked by giving the babies a sufficient amount of water to drink.

Dehydration in babies is also frequently the result of severe diarrhoea, cholera infatum, incessant vomiting, etc. And whenever after an operation on a nursing baby there is an increase of body temperature with no sign of infection of the wound, water deficiency is to be thought of.

Thirst cures have many therapeutic indications and applications. 

A strict thirst cure, excluding all liquid intake as well as all food intake, can only be carried out safely for two or three days and is sometimes necessary in gastric or pulmonary hemorrhage, in strokes and in uncontrollable vomiting.

A moderate thirst cure is frequently used in certain forms of of water which increases the weight, and which can only be combatted obesity, especially the kind which does not respond well to food restriction. In these cases we find that the tissues retain an excess by decreasing water consumption and increasing water elimination. 

It is also of great benefit in dropsy. The often miraculous results of a

strict Karell cure, already referred to elsewhere, are based on the principle of water restrictions. 

It likewise aids in the treatment of high blood pressure and is very efficacious in coryza, that disease which is often so innocent but which frequently results in complications like suppuration of the middle ear, sinus trouble and meningitis. In fact a strict thirst cure will cure almost any case of coryza in 2 or 3 days, and must be included in our dietary specifics. There are other indications, but they are too technical to be included in a book intended for the layman. 

Naturally such a drastic remedy has also its contra-indications; it is dangerous in very lean persons and before and after certain operations.

A modification of the thirst cure is the celebrated 

(6) Schroth Cure 

This consists in the application of 3 thirst days, 2 small and 2 large drink days a week and is continued for 4 to 6 weeks. Its holy shrine is in Nieder-Lindewiese in Silesia, where it is used and has achieved considerable fame as a cure-all for nearly all diseases. 

(7) VEGETARIANISM 

The word "vegetarian” has been coined from the Latin “vegetus” meaning healthy and strong, suggesting that here we have a special dietary cure which confers upon those who practice it, health and strength. With this idea in the minds of its followers, vegetarianism has become a veritable cult. Vegetarians regard flesh eating not only as unwholesome but as unethical and immoral, and they base their belief on various arguments and facts, some of them sound but most of them exaggerated and distorted. 

A strict vegetarian diet excludes the use of milk and eggs. 

The question is often asked if it is possible to thrive and remain well on vegetable proteins alone. While it is true that the proteins of many vegetable foods are notoriously incomplete and unable to sustain life indefinitely, it must be admitted that if a vegetarian diet includes green leafy vegetables, potatoes, nuts, almonds and soya beans, all of

DIETETIC CURES 

233 which contain complete proteins, it is possible to live and thrive on it. 

Examination of athletes shows that while a meat diet allows a sudden spurt of effort, it is distinctly inferior to a vegetable diet when it comes to endurance, as this requires not so much a large amount of protein as it does carbohydrates and vitamins, all of which are lacking in meat and are present in vegetables. The experience of vegetarians proves that we can get along with almost any form of vegetable diet, if only we see to it that the diet contains the necessary calories and about 50 to 80 grams of perfect proteins.

That with a vegetable diet an individual is able to do much physical work is shown by the result of a competitive march from Dresden to 

Berlin in 1902 in which the first six victors were all vegetarians, the first one even a raw food faddist. 

It must be emphasized, however, that the extreme form of vegetarianism is not suited to everybody's gastro-intestinal tract and cannot be recommended unreservedly as a steady, exclusive diet. After all, man is not a herbivorous animal but an omnivorous one; he always lived on what he could get either of animal or vegetable origin. Our alimentary canal, its length, size and shape, and our teeth clearly indicate that we are an omnivorous being. Even our ancestors, the apes, eat insects, worms, small birds and other animals, provided they are able to capture them.

The question, after all, is not so much is it possible, as is it desirable to try to live and maintain adequate nutrition on a pure vegetable diet. 

As an answer I desire to state that a vegetable diet neither prevents putrefaction nor fermentation in the colon, the two conditions which are supposed to be the cause of so many serious ailments; nor does it prevent gout, as many vegetable foods, such as lentils, beans, peas, oatmeal, asparagus, tea, and coffee, contain an abundance of uric acid. 

Nor does a vegetable diet seem desirable from an economical point of view. It is true that bread, cereals, legumens and potatoes are very cheap, but, on the other hand, the cheapest green vegetable, cabbage, is three times as dear as bread, and much dearer than sugar. Certain it is that from a caloric point of view, spinach, cauliflower, head lettuce, are dearer than eggs, and the cheapest fruit, apples, is as dear as meat. The

reason for this low economic value of fruits and vegetables is that their nutritive value is small. For instance, an adult would have to eat 40 pounds of lettuce and digest it to cover his energy requirements for 1 day, and of apples he would have to consume 8 pounds to accomplish the same end.

Figure this out in dollars and cents, as well as in terms of digestive burden, and you have the answer. A vegetable and fruit diet is too voluminous. In order to supply enough proteins a vegetable diet must contain a large amount of cereals and legumens which are deficient in vitamins and minerals. Such a bulky diet is difficult for man's intestines to handle.

Moreover, the great amount of cellulose which it contains, and which is very indigestible, makes these vegetable diets decidedly irritating to the human intestines. Cellulose stimulates the motor function of the intestines. This stimulation is necessary for herbivorous animals on account of their long and sluggish intestines, but does not seem so important for carnivorous animals. 

Summing it all up, there is very little evidence that a strict vegetarian diet promotes health, prevents sickness or prolongs youth, whereas there are many reasons to believe that, for the majority of people, a mixed diet is to be preferred. 

(8) RAW FOOD DIET 

What is true of vegetarianism is even more true of the "Raw Food Diet", which is in fact an extreme form of vegetarianism. By it we mean a diet which is taken exclusively from the vegetable kingdom and which is neither exposed to heat nor to the process of cooking.

Being an offspring or branch of vegetarianism, a raw food diet excludes raw meat, raw milk, and uncooked eggs. It has the same virtues and the same faults as the ordinary vegetable diet only in an aggravated degree, and its supposed superiority is based on a great deal of sentiment and very little logic and scientific reasoning. 

It is evident that with the recognition of the importance of the mineral elements, such as calcium, iron, iodine and the various vitamins, the use of raw, uncooked foods in the diet has taken on a renewed inter-

DIETETIC CURES 

235 est and a new meaning. Time honored methods of preparation of food have been shown to destroy or waste a considerable part of these food factors. Long heating, for instance, especially in open utensils or in an alkaline or neutral medium reduces the amount of all the known vitamins, and the various salts and sugars soluble in water are leeched from the foods.

These facts have led to certain food fads. Thus raw food cranks appeared who either for sanitary, ethical or religious reasons lived mainly on fresh raw leaves and root vegetables, fruits, nuts, uncooked cereals, oil and honey.

Amongst the principal raw foods used we have: (1) Roots, such as radishes, onions, beets, carrots and garlic. (2) Leafy vegetables such as watercress, endive, lettuce, red cabbage, white cabbage, sauerkraut, celery, parsley, chives. (3) Vegetable fruits, such as cucumbers, melons, and tomatoes. (4) Fruits, such as apples, strawberries, etc. (5) Bananas. (6) Nuts and olives.

Nearly all, with the exception of the nuts and olives, are characterized by being poor in proteins and fats and rich in water, minerals, vitamins and perfect proteins.

But while such a diet shows some notable successes in the treatment of a few conditions, for general purposes and especially as a diet for the perfectly well it has its distinct disadvantages. 

On this diet the energy intake is low, amounting to not more than 1490 to 1650 calories a day; besides, the utilization of raw food is very incomplete. Losses of 1/4 or 2/5 are nothing unusual, and in consequence the fecal output is large, two or three times the ordinary amount, and contains from 22 to 28% of the food consumed, thus leaving only 

997 or 1294 calories, which are utilized.

While some uncooked vegetables and fruits are essential in a well balanced diet, man cannot exist on such an exclusive diet and attain optimal condition. Moreover, cooking is not the unmixed evil it is made out to be; in many instances it develops pleasant odors and flavors which influence the flow of the digestive juices, renders foods more

sanitary and more perfectly utilizable and does not unfavorably affect growth and reproduction.

It all shows that the old saying "A little knowledge is a dangerous thing" is nowhere more applicable than in this field of practical dietetics. 

Neither the all too prevalent protein phobia, nor the widespread vitamin propaganda justifies such a radical departure from a normal diet. Vitamin deficiency on a general, mixed, well-balanced diet is not to be feared: only Vitamin C is completely destroyed under ordinary methods of cooking and can easily be replaced by eating oranges or orange juice; and the larger protein intake speaks rather in favor than against such a mixed diet. Dispensing with the art of cooking is a step backward towards primitive conditions. 

Used for a limited time, however, a raw food diet has proved of value in a number of diseases such as habitual constipation, chronic Bright's disease, high blood pressure, diabetes, gout, obesity, and certain skin diseases, and the almost miraculous cure of dysentery in children by a raw apple diet shows that a raw food diet is not as black as it is often painted. But as a steady food for the well it is to be rejected. 

A modification of this raw food diet is that enthusiastically advocated by its author under the name of into: 

(9) BIRCHER-BENNER DIET 

Bircher-Benner divides all foods according to their importance (1) Those of the highest value: Included in these are fresh raw foods of vegetable origin, fruits, nuts, raw milk and raw eggs. These are really the protective foods. (2) Those of lesser value, including cooked, heated or similarly prepared vegetables, fruits, potatoes, cereals, bread, boiled milk and cooked eggs. The only bread he allows is graham bread. (3) Those of least value: Cooked muscle meats and organs, such as liver, sweetbreads, etc.

To keep the uric acid content of the diet low, it is necessary to avoid peas, beans, lentils, coffee, tea and cocoa. He also forbids the excessive use of salt and alcohol; he likewise demands that consumption

DIETETIC CURES 

237 of sugars and pastries be reduced to a minimum and that lemon juice be used in place of vinegar. The raw vegetables must be of selected purity, carefully cleansed before being used, and if need be, given in puree orm. 

According to Bircher-Benner the best results are obtained by a diet composed of nuts, fruits, vegetables, milk, butter, cream, all in the raw state.

The indications for such a diet are universal: pregnancy and lactation, growth and childhood, many gastro-intestinal diseases, chronic diarrhoeas and constipation of the adult, rheumatism and gout, affections of the liver, kidneys and heart; hardening of the arteries and high blood pressure; migraine, hives, eczema and related skin affections; asthma and bronchitis; diseases of teeth and gums; tuberculosis, sinus infections; all toxic and allergic diseases; and a number of others too numerous to enumerate.

This sounds indeed fantastical and like a veritable panacea. Physicians are notoriously suspicious of everything that is advocated as a cure-all. If all this were true, the millennium surely would be here. 

And yet when conscientious physicians and investigators like Bircher Benner and others, speak seriously of the remarkable results obtained from a raw food diet, modified, if need be, to satisfy the individual needs, it behooves the rest of us to sit up and take notice. That in it there lies a large kernel of truth is denied by nobody. The whole truth? 

Hardly. And yet perhaps we may find here a solution for the cause of the ever increasing mortality rate from such diseases as hardening of the arteries, high blood pressure, Bright's disease, apoplexy, which cause the death of our captains of industry and of many other prominent people in the prime of their lives in ever increasing numbers. And when we think of the treatment of dysentery with the grated raw apple diet, of the treatment of spru with strawberries, favorable results in other diseases by raw food diet seem not impossible. 

Perhaps even cancer will some day be placed in the list of dietary diseases. In the meantime, a search for a cure of cancer along these lines need not be despaired of on the ground that we do not know its cause. Quinine was used long before the germ of malaria was discov-

ered; mercury for syphilis long before the discovery of its cause; orange juice for scurvy and cod liver oil for rickets, long before vitamins were known; and liver for pernicious anaemia, although the nature of it is even today unknown. 

(10) FLETCHERIZING 

This special dietetic cure has never attained to the popularity it rightfully deserves.

Fletcher was not a physician. Though a trained athlete when young, his physical condition at the age of 40 had become impaired to such an extent that he was rejected as a bad risk by a life insurance company; and he then and there determined to make a strong effort to regain his former health and strength. To this end he devised a method of eating, now known under the name "Fletcherizing". The result of this new diet was that his health and vigor returned and that he could do more mental and physical work and with less fatigue than most men 

20 years younger.

Fletcherizing has very few rules. To begin with, Fletcher conceived the idea that our so-called deglutition reflex had become lost through bad habits of eating, and that as a result everybody gulped his food down before it was well masticated and in a condition to be received by the stomach. This reflex, he argued, had to be re-educated and reestablished so that every bit of food, solid or liquid, after being thoroughly masticated or munched, by means of that reflex disappears from the mouth without any conscious effort at swallowing on the part of the individual. Food thus prepared in the mouth, he reasoned, was not only more thoroughly digested and utilized, but caused a hitherto unknown gustatory enjoyment. And he finally formulated the following few rules of eating known as "Fletcherizing”. (1) Eat all you crave, but no more; and eat anything you want. (2) Thoroughly mix every morsel of food with saliva and masticate it until it disappears from the mouth without being consciously gulped or forced down. (3) Everything that does not disappear from the mouth by itself should be rejected under any and all circumstances.

DIETETIC CURES 

239 This, he contended, did not need to violate any social customs. 

Nobody, he argued, would dream of swallowing a piece of bone, accidentally found in the food, just to avoid offending the host. But that is just what people did in case, for instance, a piece of tough meat was put before them. You could masticate such a piece of meat from now until doomsday, and it would still be a piece of meat in the mouth, until either forced down or rejected. If gulped down it would, no doubt, largely defy digestion and assimilation and reach the colon, there to become a prey of the putrefactive bacteria, and form poisons which would be absorbed and cause symptoms of toxemia and in the end give rise to various disorders, such as high blood pressure, hardening of the arteries, Bright's disease, etc., and leave the individual in a continuous tired and worn out condition.

As a result of Fletcherizing the appetite improves and bodily efficiency increases, and this on a protein consumption of less than onehalf of that usually recommended, and on a caloric intake of 2,000 or even less.

In addition, gas formation and flatulence absolutely cease; the urine becomes inoffensive, the bowels move only once or twice a week, and then in the form of small balls like those of a sheep, which are covered with a little mucus and are absolutely devoid of any odor. In fact they smell like hot biscuits rather than like the bowel contents as we know them. The result is less fatigue, increased vigor, longer youth and a greater span of life. 

Analyzing "Fletcherizing" and its modus operandi, the conclusion is justified that in addition to the low protein content and the low caloric value of the diet it owes its beneficial effect to the thorough mastication and to the rejection of all indigestible substances, thus preventing putrefactive processes in the colon. The absence of all odor from the stools of patients who adhere to this diet is the best proof that putrefaction has been absolutely stopped. Certain it is that all those who suffer from flatulency should practice Fletcherizing in order to rid themselves of this most disagreeable and embarrassing nuisance.

Fletcherizing may be a fad and a fallacy but it contains a large kernel of truth, which ought to be preserved. Thorough mastication and a lower food consumption are, no doubt, steps in the right direction.

PART V DIETETIC FADS, FASHIONS AND FALLACIES

DIETETIC FADS, FASHIONS AND FALLACIES 

It is perhaps not generally known that there is a fashion in drugs, which the physician uses for the alleviation and cure of sickness, just as there is a fashion in clothes and other things. Drugs used for many years with perfectly satisfactory results are suddenly discarded in favor of more modern ones, which are more attractive looking, but often not half as effective. Even the use of foods is subject to this tyrant called "Fashion". 

Yesterday it was the universal use of orange juice which was stressed as a necessary part of the diet of young infants and children, to such an extent even that mothers came to believe that the very life of their babies depended on the daily use of orange juice. Today, however, it is tomato juice which is proclaimed as a means to supply the indispensable Vitamin C, and this in spite of the fact that orange juice has never failed in a single instance where there was genuine need for it. 

The same is true of the use of sugar for modifying cows' milk. 

Yesterday it was dextrimaltose which was urged on and used by physicians and parents with almost religious zeal and fanaticism as the best sweetening agent for milk. Today its place has been largely taken by 

Karo Corn Syrup; and why? Nobody knows or can satisfactorily explain, unless it must be assumed that Dame Fashion put it on the throne where dextrimaltose once seemed to reign supreme. 

Again yesterday it was milk which was regarded as an ideal food in the diet in fevers. Today this opinion seems to have been reversed. 

At first I refused to believe this new thing, but when I heard it daily over again I realized that I was up against another one of those new fashions and fads. Of all the nonsense ever spread regarding foods and feeding, this beats them all. What, prey, shall take the place of that most wonderful of all foods, milk? That the insane desire to introduce new things, new fads and new fashions should lead to such freak teachings and practices, makes one almost exclaim: "Es waer sum lachen wenn es nicht zum weinen waere" "It would be laughable if it wasn't so sad."

The whole thing wouldn't be so serious, if it wasn't for the awful look which mothers give you should you still cling to orange juice, 243

THE HUMAN FU EL 

dextrimaltose or milk in fevers, a look by which she puts you at once in a class of old foggies and has-beens. 

It is true, that as long as food is necessary to man, just so long will there be dietary fads. These fads have expressed themselves in the past by the advocacy of such exclusive diets as the vegetarian diet, the fruit diet or the nut diet, giving their respective followers the names of "vegetarians", "fruitarians", and "nutarians"; the latter should simply be called "nuts", (the number of which I place as high as 20% of the living population).

There is, of course, a kernel of truth in each and any of these fads. 

As a special diet for the relief of gout, obesity, high blood pressure, auto-intoxication, constipation, etc., these fads have their place, but to raise them to the importance which some people give them and to regard them as the only road to health, happiness and long life, is preposterous. The propaganda in favor of vegetarianism is so full of prejudice, ignorance, scientific inaccuracy and mis-statements, that it is hard to take it seriously. Historical and anthropological facts, such as the structure of the teeth in prehistoric skulls, the character and length of the digestive canal, the nature of various organs and secretions prove that man has always been an omnivorous animal; and of the aborigines we know that some were largely vegetarians, some meat eaters depending on the season of the year and the opportunity for easily obtaining one or the other form of food; but all have been mixed feeders when possible.

Moreover, history shows that in spite of the fact that three-fourths of all mankind are vegetarians, a people on a vegetable diet never rose as high in the scale of civilization as nations on a mixed diet,and that most of the world's progress has been and is still being made by people with whom a mixed diet is the rule. Some vegetarians, it is true, have become famous but not because of their dietary custom, but rather in spite of it. "Timeo Danaos et dona ferentes"-I fear the vegetarians, in spite of the few virtues they exhibit, remembering the French saying "Dis-moi que tu frequent et je te dis qui tu est,”—“Tell me with whom you associate and I will tell you who you are." In other words, I don't like

DIETETIC FADS 

245 the company they keep. With them we generally find associated antivaccinationists, anti-vivisectionists, anti-tobacco, anti-tea and coffee, and anti-drink cranks and anti everything that makes life more secure, more beautiful and more happy; and when I go into a vegetarian restaurant and see all the measly, sickly and consumptive looking people sitting around their banquet tables, sipping buttermilk and munching health breads and health foods, the sight becomes quite ridiculous. Many, too, combine with their dietary habits certain forms of clothes as especially liable to promote health and long life. Thus Jaeger advocates wool, Lahmann cotton and Knapp linen clothes for wear. 

And what is true of vegetarians is equally true of fruitarians, nutarians and the advocates of the raw food craze, all using as their slogan "Back to nature". I am sure that if it was left to them, they would, like the nudists of today, want us to break up our homes, leave the cities, and retire to the forests and the jungles, there to build our nests amid the branches of the trees and live the lives our common ancestors lived millions of years ago. 

There are many other fads and fallacies which may here be mentioned. Why f.i. 4% sweet milk in moderate quantity and if people are fond of it, or why a sugar-coated pill, should be on the forbidden list of a diabetic diet when so-called 5% vegetables or 10% fruits are permitted, I could never understand. Much less could I comprehend why oysters and liver should have been among the forbidden articles of diabetics. The small amount of glycogen (animal starch) they contain is as nothing compared with the starch and sugar content of vegetables and fruits. But book after book has been copying this dietetic contradiction and nonsense.

The same thing is true, though not to the same extent, of the dietetic treatment of high blood pressure. Here we find such foods as tea, coffee, meats, and alcoholic drinks on the forbidden list. And yet I know from my own experience that tea and coffee taken in moderation do not raise the blood pressure. Moreover, we physicians do not hesitate to give caffeine or similar remedies in high blood pressure, especially in those cases associated with angina pectoris because they are known to cause a dilation of the coronary arteries. And yet,

year after year, coffee is put on the list of forbidden foods in cases of high blood pressure, thus unnecessarily depriving these patients of much of the joy of life.

Coffee has its strong and definite contra-indications but its moderate use in uncomplicated high blood pressure per se is not ordinarily one of them.

The same is true about alcoholic drinks. I have know many whiskey drinkers, and they all have come to me with low pressure rather than with a high one. Like caffeine, alcohol has its definite contra-indications but, like coffee, it should not be lightly and thoughtlessly taken away from unfortunate sufferers because, after all, it adds a little cheer and joy to an otherwise gloomy existence and does no harm, if used in moderation.

The same thing can be said of meat. As long as the kidneys are intact, there can be no objection to the moderate use of meat. Nor does it matter much whether it is white meat or red meat.

Surely, scientific accuracy is not yet the greatest virtue of the dietitian.

Equally fallacious is the common objection of physicians to the use of meats in rheumatism. Though the old uric acid theory of rheumatism which held such a sway over the medical profession and the laity thirty-five or forty years ago has long been put into the discard; though we shout from the housetops that rheumatism is caused by focal infection, and though we pull tonsils and teeth with shameless abandon and open sinuses and abdomens with an almost reckless lack of discrimination, we still cling in practice to the worn out theory of uric acid, and forbid the use of meat and other articles which might generate uric acid. What a contradiction! What a spectacle of thoughtlessness!

In connection with the subject of Vitamins it is well to call the attention of the public to the fallacy which is preached by manufacturers of cod liver oil that Vitamin A protects against infection and strengthens immunity. While this has been found true in a few experimental animals there is no good evidence that such a relation

DIETETIC FADS 

247 exists in man. Infections and colds have been just as common in children who received cod liver oil as in those who did not. 

A word of caution, may be justified, regarding the use of sunshine foods, i.e., foods which have been irradiated with ultraviolet rays of the quartz lamp to impart to them Vitamin D. Today these foods are so plentiful on the market that the possibility of an overdose of Vitamin D and a resulting damage to the blood vessels is to be taken into serious consideration.

Like many another discovery, so has the discovery of irradiated foods been exploited far beyond its actual merit, if not to the danger point.

Few words need be wasted on the latest nonsense, i.e., the use of irradiated toilet paper. It merely shows to what extremes some manufacturers go to capitalize prevailing fads in order to sell their wares.

And, now, just one more word about another dietetic fallacy, i.e., the use of vitamin tablets. Aside from the fact that many of these tablets have on investigation been found greatly lacking in the very vitamins they advertise to contain, why should anybody be foolish enough to use tablets when we have delicious dietary articles to supply these vitamins? Butterfat as contained in milk, or cream, or butter, and most of the leafly vegetables, contain an abundance of Vitamin A. 

Whole grain foods are rich in anti-neuritic Vitamin B; oranges and tomatoes and vegetables supply an abundance of anti-scorbutic Vitamin 

C; Vitamin D is supplied by sunshine and cod liver oil and eggs.

Under these circumstances the use of tablets becomes a riddle which can only be solved on the theory that the strong suggestive power of constant advertisements and the old belief in the efficacy of drugs is still powerful enough to make people in their misery forget the blessings which are offered them in form of sunshine, fresh air, and natural foods. 

Nor are the many "Health Foods", "Body Builders" and "Food

Tonics" so much advertised any better than our every day household foods. In fairness it must be said that it was not the medical profession that coined the phrase, "Health Food, Body Builder, etc."; these

slogans originated in the brains of advertisers and manufacturers. 

One of the most common fallacies is the belief that acid fruits, such as oranges, grapefruit, lemons and limes, as well as others, increase the acid condition of the system (whatever the people mean by that) and thus produce rheumatism. Science has long furnished us with proof that just the opposite is true, that instead of producing acidity these fruits increase the alkalinity. In other words, they have the same effect as has the administration of bicarbonate of soda. In spite of this, it seems hard to dislodge the old fallacy. 

Why mushrooms are called "vegetable beefsteaks" is another fallacy hard to understand. The composition of mushrooms differs little from that of green vegetables, whose nutritive value is very small and certainly unlike and much inferior to that of meat. 

Nor does it seem logical for vegetarians to use milk, which is of animal origin, while they reject tea and coffee, which come from the vegetable kingdom. Their fear of tea and coffee is entirely unfounded. Tea and coffee may be injurious to nervous people, they may cause insomnia if taken in excess, may increase or cause tremors, may accelerate the heart action or cause acidity of the stomach; yet their enormous sale and use testifies to their popularity and perhaps even to their harmlessness and usefulness. To many people coffee and tea are an almost indispensable drink, contributing a feeling of comfort, joy and happiness, and even inveterate coffee drinkers generally live to a ripe old age. Truly it can be said about tea and coffee that what is food for one, is poison for another.

Another popular fallacy is that the curdling of milk is evidence of indigestion. Often anxious mothers rush in great excitement to their doctor and tell him about the large curds the baby has been vomiting. They do not know that it is not the vomiting of curdled milk, but the vomiting of uncurdled milk, which is a sign of stomach trouble. If they knew, this fallacy would soon be dispelled. Curdling of milk is the first stage in its digestion and is necessary and as natural as getting wet on a rainy day.

One of the worst fads is the indiscriminate use of reducing diets. 

Everybody, especially our female friends, seem to be possessed of

DIETETIC FADS 

249 this craze, often in entire disregard of its possible harmful consequences. 

It is true, and we are told by eminent authorities and life insurance statistics, that obesity may lead to high blood pressure, hardening of the arteries, diabetes and heart disease even, and that fat people do not live nearly as long as lean ones. It is a question, however, if those dire consequences are not rather the result of dietary excesses coupled with constitutional inherited tendencies, rather than to the obesity itself. This line of arguing seems like splitting hair, but it is one thing to curb excesses and another to use enforced undernutrition. The latter causes loss of energy, produces anaemia, loss of resistance to infections, nervousness, sterility and even tuberculosis. 

Under these circumstances, it is easy to see that the promiscuous use of obesity cures may invite disaster and is to be discouraged except on advice of and under strict supervision of a physician. 

Another fad is the roughage fad. It is true that a reasonable amount of roughage is required in our daily diet to insure regular intestinal elimination. This roughage is plentifully supplied by the many vegetables, fruits, cereals, nuts and leguminous plants, which form part of our daily dietary. In spite of this, we are constantly reminded to eat more roughage, and the use of the various bran foods and bran itself, advertised and advocated so extensively by their manufacturers, has become a veritable fad. And yet their indiscriminate use for the purpose of stimulating intestinal elimination is not without possible harm. It must not be forgotten that an excess of roughage may interfere with the digestion and assimilation of important foodstuffs, that it may cause irritation of the bowls and excess formation of mucus, all of which may defeat the benefits derived from their more moderate use. 

Another fallacy is exhibited in the saying "Starve a fever and feed a cold". It is just the opposite which is true. Haig, the great advocate of the uric acid theory of disease, mentions many instances which prove that over-eating is a frequent contributory cause of colds; and the quick cure of a cold by a starvation and thirst cure, is one of our brilliant modern dietetic achievements. Moreover, our latest teaching regarding the feeding of fevers is that while short fevers

may be starved with benefit and without harm, long continued fevers, such as typhoid fever, fare far better on a sufficient and even on an increased food intake. 

There are other minor fallacies and preconceived notions which certain experiments made on groups of students, one of whom was a habitual regurgitator, dissipated. For instance, it was found that water drinking with meals, even the drinking of cold water, is not harmful; that fried foods have no damaging effect on the digestion of a perfectly normal individual; that dark meat of chicken is as digestible as white meat; that fish is no more a brain food than many other foods, such as eggs and meat; and that it is a mistake to think that exercise after meals is better than rest. There is an old saying "post cenam mille passus meabis" (After meals walk a thousand steps); like many another saying this is a fallacy. A full stomach needs rest, not exercise.

Nor have aesthetic conditions surrounding the meal much effect on the gastric secretion of a healthy man with a good appetite. A workman who eats his food amidst unpleasant sights and sounds and odors gets as much benefit from his food as the epicure "who dines in the gilded banquet hall while listening to soft strains of music”. 

And perhaps more so.

I have by no means exhausted this subject, but enough fashions, fads and fallacies have been mentioned to emphasize the necessity of using more common sense and thought in the use of dietetic measures, and not to trust blindly to every fad exploited in print and propaganda, or by books which are written for profit only and which year after year dress up old and worn out things and offer them to the public and the profession, like a second-hand dealer in old clothes, as new and true facts.

PART VI MISCELLANEOUS SUBJECTS

(a) SPECIAL RECIPES 

While this is not intended to be a cook book, a few recipes which I regard very highly and which are little known by the general public and profession are here inserted in the hope that their popularization will render a valuable addition to the dietetic treatment of various conditions. 

1. Scraped Beef Balls: Get lean round steak and scrape the meat fibres off with a spoon. Make into a meatball about 2 inches wide and 

3/4 inch thick (the thicker, the better); make pan very hot, add a little butter to keep steak from sticking to pan and provide for a little gravy; place meatball into hot pan long enough to brown it, turn over and brown other side and serve. If correctly made, the inside must be rare, if not raw; add salt to taste. 

2. Swiss Steak: Get 3 pounds of a 2 inch thick round steak; pound 

1 cup of flour with the edge of a saucer into steak, ½ cup into each side, for 20 minutes. Then put 2 tablespoons of fat in skillet and heat it; put steak in and brown it, 10 minutes on each side; add 2 cups of boiling water and put in hot oven for 30 minutes; add salt, sliced onion and can of tomato sauce; after 30 minutes add two more cups of water, put hood on and let bake in moderate oven for 1/2 to 2 hours. 

3. Puree of Spinach: Wash thoroughly in 3 different waters; cook for 

10 minutes without additional water in an open vessel, drain all water off (pressing it with hand) run through a colander or fine food-chopper; then mix 2 tablespoonfuls of chicken or beef fat and about 2 tablespoonfuls or more of flour (to 1 peck of spinach) and a little water; mix this thickening thoroughly with the spinach. 

If this makes it too thick, thin it with soupstock; garnish with sliced hard-boiled egg and serve. 

4. German Schleim Soup: Make soup in ordinary way with meat stock and either barley or oatmeal or rice; let simmer for 3 or 4 hours; strain and season. In this country we call it strained cereal soup. 253

5. Bean Soup: Take 2 cups of small navy beans, soak them over night; boil for 15 minutes; put in Dutch oven and add plenty of water, small onion, 2 tablespoonfuls of tomato sauce.

Season by cooking garlic sausage with it, and bake in Dutch oven for 2 hours.

For those suffering from flatulency mash the cooked beans through a sieve and serve in puree form.

(c) DIETARY MATHEMATICS AND EQUIVALENTS 

In computing diets it is absolutely necessary to use the metric system of weights and measures; therefore a knowledge of equivalents is very desirable. 

1 gm, 5 gms, 10 gms, 15 gms, 30 gms, 

480 gms, 960 gms, = 5 cc. 10 cc, 15 cc, 16 drops. 

1 tsp. (teaspoonful). = 1 dsp. (dessertspoonful). 

1 tbsp. (tablespoonful). 2 tbsp. 

1 cc, = 

16 grains, 1/6 ounce, 

1/3 ounce, ½ ounce, 30 cc, = 

1 ounce, 

480 cc, 16 ounces, 1 pint. 960 cc, 32 ounces, 1 quart. 

500 gms, 1000 gms, 500 cc, = 1 pound (metric s.). - 1000 cc, = 1 Kilgm. (kilogram) 60 cc, 2 ounces, 120 cc, 4 ounces, 180 cc, 6 ounces, 240 cc, 4-2 quarts 1 ctm. (centimeter) 1 inch 1 foot 1 klgm (kilogram) 1 pound 

Thermometer scales: 

ounces, = 1 gallon. 0.3037 inch. = 2 pounds metric s.). 

1 Whiskey glassful. 

1 wine glassful. = 1 small cupful. = 1 large cupful (tumbler). =2.540 centimeter. 12 inches. 

2.2046 pounds. = 0.4635. 

Fahrenheit (F.): freezing 32°; boiling 212° to convert °F. into °C., subtract 32, multiply by 5, divide by 9. 

Centigrade (C): freezing 0°; boiling 100°. celsius) 

To convert °C. into °F., multiply by 9, divide by 5, add 32.
